# Foreword

Decades of remarkable progress against poverty and inequality have given way to a period of slower and more uneven gains. Between 1990 and 2020, the share of the world's population living in extreme poverty fell from 43 percent to 11 percent, driven largely by gains in large middle-income economies. The number of economies with high inequality, measured by a Gini index above 40, declined from 70 in 2000 to 42 in 2024. But progress has slowed, and extreme poverty is increasingly concentrated in a few economies, most of them in Sub-Saharan Africa and in fragile and conflict-affected situations. In 2026, an estimated 811 million people, 1 in 10, live below the international extreme poverty line of $3.00 a day, a rate unchanged since 2019. Without faster growth, the number will rise as populations grow more quickly where poverty is highest. Escaping extreme poverty does not guarantee economic security. Nearly half of humanity, 3.7 billion people, live below the $8.30-a-day poverty line typically used to measure poverty in upper-middle-income countries, leaving many vulnerable to falling back into extreme poverty after a shock. Weather-related shocks add to this vulnerability: 1.5 billion people, most living above the extreme poverty line, are exposed to climate hazards but lack the means to cope with these hazards. At the same time, the growth needed to end extreme poverty in the poorest countries would add little to global emissions. This edition has a thematic focus on jobs, which the World Bank Group has made central to its development agenda toward a world free of poverty on a livable planet. Drawing on new data for more than 130 economies, it provides the first global profile of the work done by poor and nonpoor adults. Jobs are the most direct pathway out of poverty for most households. Poor and nonpoor adults have similar employment rates. One in three adults in poor households are not employed, many of whom are women. Poor workers not only earn less, they also support more dependents. This means that reducing poverty requires both more jobs and better-paying jobs. Expanding employment can make the greatest difference for households living between the $3.00 and $8.30 poverty lines, while raising the earnings of poor workers can have the greatest impact on the poorest households.

**xiii**

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026 How to achieve this differs across countries. Where farms and household enterprises account for most jobs, raising productivity must go hand in hand with creating opportunities for wage employment. Where wage employment is established, access to better-paying jobs can lift households out of poverty. Jobs also help families that have escaped poverty withstand shocks and avoid falling back and, with this help, build a stronger middle class. Reducing poverty requires both expanding opportunities to earn a living and increasing the returns to the work poor people already do. By bringing together new evidence on poverty, inequality, climate risks, and jobs, this report seeks to help chart a path toward renewed progress in ending extreme poverty and boosting shared prosperity on a livable planet. Building on a decade of evidence and analysis, it seeks to help turn this knowledge into lasting progress. Michael Kremer *Senior Vice President and Chief Economist* *The World Bank Group* Paschal Donohoe *Managing Director and Chief Knowledge Officer* *The World Bank Group*

# About the Team

## Co-leads of the report

**Kathleen Beegle is a Lead Economist in the Development Research Group at the World**

Bank. She co-leads the World Bank Center for Research on Women and Jobs. Her research focuses on the study of poverty, labor, economic shocks, and methodological studies on household survey data collection in low-income contexts. She is a regular contributor to the World Bank's Development Impact blog. Before returning to the Development Research Group, she was the Human Development Program Leader for Ghana, Liberia, and Sierra Leone and a Lead Economist with the Gender Group. Kathleen holds a PhD in economics from Michigan State University.

**Maurizio Bussolo is a Lead Economist in the Distributional Impact of Policies Unit at the**

World Bank. He specializes in quantitative analyses of economic policy and development with research interests spanning both micro- and macroeconomic topics. He led operational teams in the aftermath of the 2008-09 crisis, negotiating with Latin American and Caribbean governments on the implementation of reforms to shield the most vulnerable. He previously worked at the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development, the Overseas Development Institute in London, and Fedesarrollo and Los Andes University in Colombia. He has extensively published in peer-reviewed journals on trade, growth, poverty, and income distribution. Maurizio holds a PhD in economics from the University of Warwick.

## Core team

**Alejandro De la Fuente is a Senior Economist and the Global Program Lead on Climate**

Resilience in the Distributional Impact of Policies Unit at the World Bank, with extensive experience across low- and middle-income countries, including fragile settings. He specializes in poverty analysis, program evaluation, and risk and vulnerability and leads statistics and social protection operations. Previously, he held roles at the United Nations Development Programme, the UN Office for Disaster Risk Reduction, the Inter-American Development

**xvii**

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026 Bank, and the Ministry of Social Development and the Office of the President in Mexico. Alejandro has authored numerous peer-reviewed publications and six books and holds a PhD in development studies/development economics from the University of Oxford.

**Miki Khanh Doan is an Economist in the Distributional Impact of Policies Unit at the World**

Bank. Her work uses household data and applied microeconomic methods to examine how policies and shocks affect poverty, welfare, and resilience. At the World Bank, she focuses on the distributional impacts of climate shocks, agricultural policy, and climate-resilient development. Her broader research covers rural livelihoods, intrahousehold dynamics, food security, and human capital programs. Miki holds a PhD in agricultural and resource economics from the University of California, Davis, and a BA from the University of Richmond.

**Ebad Ebadi is an Economist in the Development Research Group at the World Bank. His**

research examines the intersection of development, environmental, and labor economics, with a focus on sustainable growth and labor market outcomes. His work examines topics such as environmental degradation, climate change, jobs, and the distributional impacts of environmental and climate policies. Through empirical analysis, his research aims to generate evidence that informs policies promoting sustainable and inclusive development; bridges research and policy; and contributes to debates on environmental sustainability, labor markets, and economic development. Ebad holds a PhD in economics from George Washington University and an MS in economics from the London School of Economics and Political Science.

**David Groves is a Lead Climate Change Specialist in the Chief Economic Advisor Unit of**

the Climate Department at the World Bank, where he manages the Climate Support Facility's Country Climate Strategies Program, helping countries develop and operationalize long-term, low-emissions development strategies. He also co-leads the Country Climate and Development Reports Program. Before joining the World Bank in 2021, he was a Senior Policy Researcher at the RAND Corporation, where he codirected the Climate Resilience Center and the Center for Decision Making Under Uncertainty. David holds a PhD in policy analysis from the Pardee RAND Graduate School, an MS in atmospheric sciences from the University of Washington, and an MS in earth systems from Stanford University.

###### Christoph Lakner is a Program Manager in the Development Data Group at the World

Bank. He leads the Global Poverty and Inequality Data team, which manages the Poverty and Inequality Platform, the home of the World Bank's global poverty numbers. His research interests include poverty and inequality measurement, especially from a global perspective. Christoph holds a DPhil, a MPhil, and a BA in economics from the University of Oxford.

**Gabriel Lara Ibarra is a Senior Economist in the Fiscal Policy and Growth Global Practice**

at the World Bank. Since joining the World Bank in 2012, he has led analytical work, policy dialogue, lending operations, primary data collection projects, and capacity-building activities across Latin America and the Caribbean and the Middle East and North Africa. His work

**xviii**

-----

About the Team

focuses on poverty measurement, inequality, social mobility, gender disparities, and the distributional impacts of public policies. Gabriel holds a PhD in economics and an MA from the University of Maryland, College Park.

**Jonas Helth Lønborg is a Senior Economist on the Global Poverty and Inequality Data**

team in the Development Data Group at the World Bank. His work focuses on analyzing the intersections between poverty and fragility, gender, and climate change. He is part of the Donor Funded Staffing Program, and his position is funded by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Denmark, where he also worked as a thematic Chief Counsellor prior to joining the World Bank. Jonas holds a PhD in economics from the University of Southern Denmark.

**David Newhouse is a Senior Economist in the Development Economics Data Group at the**

World Bank and an elected fellow of the Institute of Labor Economics and the International Statistical Institute. His work has focused on economic measurement, including integrating survey data with nontraditional data, and analysis related to poverty and jobs in developing countries. He has published approximately 30 journal articles on statistics, labor, poverty, health, and education in developing countries. David holds a PhD in economics from Cornell University.

**Minh Cong Nguyen is a Senior Economist in the Data Analytics Unit of the Development**

Economics Vice Presidency at the World Bank. His work focuses on poverty and inequality measurement, household survey harmonization, data imputation, small area estimation, geospatial analytics, and the development of global data systems. He has contributed to analytical and statistical initiatives across several regions and previously co-led the Middle East and North Africa Data Lab, the Europe and Central Asia Team for Statistical Development, and the Data for Goals team in the Poverty and Equity Global Practice. His current research examines global small area poverty estimation, the distributional effects of climate change, the spatial dimensions of poverty and employment, and methods for integrating and fusing data from multiple sources. Minh holds a PhD in economics, with a specialization in applied microeconometrics, from American University in Washington, DC.

**Aissatou Ouedraogo is an Economist in the Distributional Impact of Policies Unit for the**

Eastern Europe and Central Asia region at the World Bank. She has led and supported key analytical and country engagement products, including poverty assessments, development policy operations, fiscal incidence analyses with capacity building, country economic memorandums, country economic updates, country climate and development reports, and systematic country diagnostics in the Western and Central Africa and Europe and Central Asia regions. Before joining the World Bank, she held a postdoctoral position at Tufts University and worked with cabinet-level officials on large-scale development programs, including the $461 million Millennium Challenge Corporation initiative between the United States and Mali. Aissatou holds a PhD in agricultural economics from Michigan State University, an MA in policy economics from Williams College, and an MS in applied economics from Virginia Tech.

**xix**

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026

**Henry Stemmler is a Consultant in the Distributional Impact of Policies Unit at the World**

Bank. His work spans poverty and welfare measurement, applied microeconomic analysis, and the integration and analysis of geospatial and survey data. Previously, he was a postdoctoral researcher at the Food Systems Economics and Policy Group at ETH Zürich and worked as an Economist at the International Labour Organization. Henry holds a PhD in economics from the University of Göttingen.

**Samuel Kofi Tetteh Baah is an Economist in the Development Data Group at the World**

Bank, where he works on global poverty and inequality measurement and on the Poverty and Inequality Platform. His research focuses on improving the quality and comparability of poverty and inequality estimates. He holds a PhD in development economics from ETH Zürich, an MA in development economics from the University of Göttingen, and a BA in economics from the University of Ghana.

**Hernán Winkler is a Senior Economist and the Global Lead on Jobs in the Poverty and Equity**

Global Practice for Latin America and the Caribbean at the World Bank. He has led analytical work and policy dialogue on labor markets, the impacts of technology adoption and policy reforms, and the drivers and consequences of poverty and income inequality. Between tenures at the World Bank, he worked as a Senior Economist at Amazon. He holds a PhD in economics from the University of California, Los Angeles, and an MA in economics from the Universidad Nacional de La Plata, Argentina.

# Main Messages

## Ten facts on poverty, prosperity, planet, and jobs

### Poverty

1. ***The decline in extreme poverty has stalled. In 2026, 811 million people, 1 in 10, are living***

on less than $3.00 a day, a rate unchanged since 2019. Had the 2000-13 pace been maintained, 243 million additional people would have escaped poverty. The stagnation is confined to the extreme line; at $4.20 and $8.30, poverty reduction has kept the earlier pace (refer to figure 1, panel a).

2. ***Extreme poverty is concentrated, and demographic patterns will concentrate it further.***

More than half the extreme poor are living in nine economies, and, by 2030, 87 percent will be living in Sub-Saharan Africa, in a fragile and conflict-affected situation, or both (refer to figure 1, panel b). The population is growing most quickly where poverty rates are the highest; so, at the 2015-24 pace of economic growth, the global rate and the number of the extreme poor will be higher in 2030 than today.

3. ***Escaping extreme poverty has not meant reaching the middle class. Nearly half of humanity,***

3.7 billion people, is still living on less than $8.30 a day, the poverty line in upper-middleincome economies. Most people who have left extreme poverty since 2000 have moved only into the $3.00-$8.30 band: no longer extremely poor, but not economically secure, and one shock away from falling back into extreme poverty. The size of the global middle class-people living on between $8.30 a day and the $28.00 prosperity standard-has roughly doubled as a share of the world's population over the same period, but its expansion has been concentrated in today's upper-middle-income economies, where the middle class is now the majority. In lower-middle-income economies, the vulnerable group living on between $3.00 and $8.30 a day is still the largest, and, in low-income economies, the middle class remains a small minority (refer to figure 1, panel c).

### Prosperity

4. ***High inequality is retreating across economies but persists across people. The number of***

economies with a Gini index above 40 fell from 70 in 2000 to 42 in 2024, and those economies still above the threshold cut their average Gini from 51 to 46. Nonetheless, one in five people worldwide is still living in a high-inequality economy, a share unchanged since 2013 (refer to figure 2, panel a), and 37 economies have never left the above-40 group in 25 years. The concentration of the extreme poor is happening here, too. The share in high-inequality economies nearly doubled, to 36 percent.

5. ***The Prosperity Gap halved and then stalled. In 1990, incomes worldwide needed to rise***

10.5-fold, on average, to reach the $28.00-a-person-a-day prosperity standard. By 2013, the Prosperity Gap had fallen to 5.5, and it stood at 4.5 in 2026 (refer to figure 2, panel b, left panel). Almost all the progress occurred before 2013. Over the latest decade, the gap has narrowed by only about 1.2 percent a year (refer to figure 2, panel b, right panel). The gap in Sub-Saharan Africa is at 11.1, twice the global average and roughly where the world stood in 1990.

### Planet

6. ***Climate risk reaches well beyond the poor. Of the 4.5 billion people exposed to a major***

climate hazard, 1.5 billion are also highly vulnerable because they lack social protection or access to basic infrastructure, and most of the highly vulnerable live above the extreme poverty line. The risk is concentrated in the Middle East, North Africa, Afghanistan, and Pakistan and the Sub-Saharan Africa regions, where nearly half and over a third of the population, respectively, are at high risk, and almost everyone who is exposed is also vulnerable. In South Asia, by contrast, the share of the population at risk has been brought below 20 percent by reducing vulnerability (refer to figure 3, panel a). Globally, the share of people at high risk fell from one in three in 2010 to one in five in 2021, although the number of such people rose by 461 million.

excluding those from land use, land use change, and forestry, reached 52.8 metric gigatons of carbon dioxide equivalent (GtCO₂e) in 2024, 48 percent above the levels in 2000. Nearly all the increase is accounted for by upper-middle-income economies, where emissions more than doubled, while emissions in high-income economies edged down as the economies grew (refer to figure 3, panel b). ### Jobs

8. ***Labor is the main and often only asset of the poor, but their work yields less income per*** ***household member than the work of the nonpoor. Working-age adults in poor households***

are employed at rates only 6 percentage points below the corresponding rates in nonpoor households, but poor workers put in three to four fewer hours a week and support three times as many dependents as workers in the richest households (refer to figure 4, panels a and b). The gap is widening. Employment among the poor fell in 88 of 138 economies over 2003-24, and earnings growth, not employment, delivered all growth in consumption per capita at the bottom of the distribution.

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026

9. ***More jobs and better-paying jobs both reduce poverty, but they reach different people.***

Raising employment in poor households to 75 percent, the rate poor men already attain, cuts extreme poverty by a fifth; raising education to the nonpoor average cuts extreme poverty by nearly half; and switching poor workers into nonagricultural employment or into wage work cuts it by a quarter to a third. All these simulations reduce the depth of poverty as well (refer to figure 4, panel c). The gains from better pay accrue mainly to the poorest. The gains from more work accrue mainly to the near-poor, among whom the nonworking adults are mostly women.

10. ***Poverty falls as the work of the poor becomes more productive, and, in the poorest*** ***economies, the work is mostly on farms and in household enterprises, not in wage jobs. In***

richer economies, households that move into wage employment are much more likely to escape poverty, while those that move into self-employment are not. In the poorest economies, the gain from wage employment is about a third as large, and the gain from self-employment, on the farm or off the farm, is not far below (refer to figure 4, panel d). The lowest rung of the ladder out of poverty is work on the farm, where higher yields raise earnings. Each step up represents a higher-productivity activity. The reach of the ladder is limited only by the ability of firms on the upper rungs to create jobs. So far, in the poorest countries, most of the movement upward has ended in microenterprise or self-employment activities rather than higher-earning wage jobs. Jobs matter in a second way as well: They keep households from falling back into poverty. It is this protection that transforms the exits from extreme poverty into a growing middle class.

# Overview

The reduction in global poverty over the past generation ranks among the great global achievements of economic development in the postwar era. In 1990, 43 percent of the world's population was living in extreme poverty. By 2020, the share was about 11 percent, and, now, more than two-thirds of humanity is living in economies in which extreme poverty has effectively ended, up from a fifth in 2000. This achievement is the standard against which the present situation must be measured, and the present falls short. Progress against extreme poverty has come to a standstill. After rising during the COVID pandemic and then recovering, the extreme poverty rate has remained essentially unchanged. In 2026, an estimated 811 million people-one person in 10-were living below the international poverty line of $3.00 a day. The slowdown predates the pandemic and has persisted since. Had poverty continued to fall at the 2000-13 pace, roughly 240 million fewer people would be poor today. The picture is different at higher poverty lines. At $4.20 and $8.30 a day (the standards typical of lower-middle- and upper-middle-income economies), poverty reduction has broadly maintained the earlier pace. Even so, nearly half of humanity-3.7 billion people-are still living on less than $8.30 a day. The share has fallen modestly, but population growth has offset much of the gain, keeping the absolute number stubbornly high. Many people who have escaped extreme poverty are also living only slightly above the $3.00 line and are therefore vulnerable to falling back after a major economic or climate shock. El Niño illustrates this risk in real time. Still intensifying toward a projected peak in late 2026 and already approaching record levels in the modern period, it is already driving emerging impacts on food security, incomes, and livelihoods across the globe, with effects on nutrition and human capital that could persist for years. The conflict in the Middle East is a second source of pressure, raising energy prices and, with them, the cost of fertilizer and of transporting food. Before the conflict, extreme poverty in 2026 was projected to affect 808 million people rather than the 811 million people now estimated (refer to box 1.2). With a global median income of $9.20 a day, most of the world's population is living below or only slightly above the $8.30 line. The world's middle class-the population between this line and the prosperity standard of $28.00 a day-remains a minority of humanity (refer to box 1.3). It has roughly doubled as a share of the world's population since 2000, with almost all of the

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026 expansion in middle-income economies, but the group that has escaped extreme poverty without reaching security has remained close to a third of humanity throughout. Without a change in the trajectory of poverty reduction, the 2020s will close as the first decade in more than a generation without meaningful progress against extreme poverty. Read together, the findings of this report form a single argument, summarized as 9 facts in the main messages section and developed in the sections that follow. On poverty, the stagnation at the extreme line is compounded by concentration: The extreme poor live increasingly in a small group of economies, in Sub-Saharan Africa, and in fragile and conflict-affected situations, where populations are growing most quickly, so the global poverty rate will rise even as most national rates edge down. On prosperity, most people who have escaped extreme poverty have not reached the middle class, and nearly half of humanity is still living on less than $8.30 a day and remain vulnerable to falling back. Fewer economies are now highly unequal, but the share of the world's people living in these economies has not fallen, and the closing of the Global Prosperity Gap has slowed to a fraction of the earlier pace. On the planet, climate risk is reaching people far above the poverty line: while emissions are growing in economies where the poor do not live, ending extreme poverty would add little to global emissions. On jobs, the poor rely almost entirely on their labor; work nearly as much as the nonpoor, but earn less per household member; and their employment has been eroding. More jobs and better-paying jobs both reduce poverty, but the gains from better pay accrue mainly to the extreme poor, and the gains from more work accrue mainly to the near-poor. In the poorest economies, the route out of poverty runs through leaving low-return agriculture rather more than through wage work. In poorer and richer economies alike, the source of the exit is productivity growth on farms, where higher yields allow workers to move into nonfarm work, and in firms, which expand and hire wage workers.

## Tracking the global path to ending

### extreme poverty, with jobs at the center

The Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report tracks the progress in the effort to reduce extreme poverty and boost shared prosperity on a livable planet through eight vision indicators: the global poverty rates at the $3.00- and $8.30-a-person-a-day poverty lines, the Global Prosperity Gap, the count of high-inequality economies, and five planetary indicators-greenhouse gas emissions, people at high risk from climate hazards, the health of key ecosystems, food and nutrition insecurity, and access to water and sanitation-that track the natural conditions on which well-being depends. In the spirit of the earlier reports, this edition has a thematic focus: jobs and their role in poverty reduction.1 Most of the eight indicators change slowly year-on-year. Each edition of the report strives to dig more deeply than the often slowly changing headline statistics. This edition confirms well-established findings on education and access to better jobs, the vulnerability of children,

-----

Overview

the persistence of inequality, the exposure to climate risk, the difficulty of creating better jobs in rural areas, and the concentration of poor workers in subsistence agriculture and nonfarm self-employment. At the same time, this report has three new elements worth highlighting. First, it presents new data. The coverage of household surveys has been expanded in regions that earlier survey editions could not observe; the five planetary indicators are included for the first time; and a global database merging labor market indicators and poverty status-a combination no earlier collection provided-has been assembled to unpack the links between poverty and jobs. Second, this report offers new analysis: decompositions that separate progress within countries from the changing composition of the world; duration analysis of inequality; subnational trajectories and household typologies; the first global jobs profile of the poor based on a comparison of working hours by poverty status; panel survey evidence on labor transitions; and counterfactual simulations that assess the employment and earnings barriers the poor face. Third, it offers new findings, including that poor workers average fewer rather than more weekly hours on the job relative to nonpoor workers and that shifts into wage employment yield less poverty reduction in low-income economies than in richer economies. This report opens with a sobering projection. If economies grow over the coming years at the per capita economic growth rates experienced from 2015 to 2024, both the global extreme poverty rate and the number of people living in extreme poverty will be higher in 2030 than today. This would not represent a mere pause, but a stunning reversal of the improvement in well-being of the preceding decades. Only sustained annual global economic growth of around 4 percent per capita would bring the global extreme poverty rate down to near the 3 percent target by 2050. However, this rate would be double the average country growth rate observed over the past 25 years. The mechanism behind the projected reversal is linked to demographic trends (specifically population growth) and insufficient economic growth. Poverty rates in most high-poverty economies are projected to decline, though only marginally. The weight of these economies as a share of the world's population is expected to rise. The population is growing most quickly precisely in the locations that exhibit the highest poverty rates and that also often face conflict, fragility, and climate vulnerability. Thus, the global extreme poverty rate would be pulled upward even as the extreme poverty rates in most economies would hold steady or improve. The distinction matters for policy: The challenge is not a diffuse global failure of development, but the clustering of clear needs in an identifiable group of economies.

## Concentration is a key fact of global

### extreme poverty and inequality

Concentration recurs across the findings of this report. More than half the world's extreme poor live in only 9 economies, while three-quarters live in 20 economies. Global poverty is concentrated both from a regional perspective and in terms of fragility. By 2030, 87 percent of

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026 the world's extreme poor will be living in Sub-Saharan Africa or in fragile and conflict-affected states in Sub-Saharan Africa or elsewhere. The places where extreme poverty persists are also increasingly places characterized by high inequality. One in five people in the world are living in economies classified as highly unequal. Among the world's extreme poor, the share is two in five, and this share has nearly doubled since 2000. The overlap compounds the challenge because high inequality weakens the povertyreducing power of growth: The same rate of economic growth produces less poverty reduction in highly unequal economies. The report's count of high-inequality economies illustrates what a headline figure may conceal. The count fell from 70 in 2000 to 42 in 2024, a decline of more than one-third. Underlying this trend, the report's fresh duration analysis reveals two findings that emphasize the stubborn persistence of inequality. In 2000-24, the Gini index never rose above 40 in 79 economies and never fell below 40 in 37 economies. The middle is thin. Few economies spent between 5 and 20 years above the threshold before falling below the threshold. High inequality is apparently not a transitory state through which economies pass. It is a chronic condition either avoided or endured for decades. The fall in the count of high-inequality economies should thus not be read as evidence that escape from high-inequality status is easy. More encouraging is the fact that, among the 37 economies in which high inequality has endured, the average Gini index has declined from about 51 to about 46, and the decline has been broad across the distribution. Roughly a quarter of these economies now stand within three points of the threshold of 40. Transitions from high to moderate inequality are rare, but the progress that precedes an exit from high inequality accumulates across years before it is registered in a headline figure. Read together, these two findings demonstrate that it is more difficult to exit a situation of high inequality than the falling count suggests and that more exiting is under way than the count reveals. The report's second prosperity indicator corroborates the diagnosis reached through the poverty data. The Global Prosperity Gap-the average factor by which incomes must be multiplied to reach a dignified and secure daily standard of living-has narrowed by half since 1990, but the narrowing in the gap has slowed appreciably since 2013. Of note, the countries in the East Asia and Pacific region have achieved remarkable improvements toward closing the gap by a 4.4 percent reduction per year. China accounts for much of the progress. In Sub-Saharan Africa, consistent with the stalling poverty reduction, the pace is 0.7 percent a year. The region's three most populous economies, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Ethiopia, and Nigeria, account for 40 percent of the regional Prosperity Gap. All three are currently classified as fragile and conflict-affected states. Larger populations are only part of the story. Nigeria accounts for 18 percent of the population and 16 percent of the gap, and Ethiopia, for 10 percent and 8 percent, respectively. Yet, the Democratic Republic of Congo makes up 9 percent of the region's population, but accounts for 16 percent of its Prosperity Gap.

-----

Overview

## Deprivations extend beyond monetary

### measures: A deteriorating planet threatens the gains against poverty

Measured across 103 economies, 17 percent of people are multidimensionally poor, that is, they are deprived in at least one of three dimensions (monetary welfare, education, and access to basic infrastructure), while 11 percent are deprived according to the monetary welfare dimension alone. This difference between multidimensional and monetary poverty is widest in Sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia, where it is driven by deprivations in education and sanitation. The five planetary indicators tracked in this report reveal a further source of vulnerability. On balance, the situation is deteriorating: Greenhouse gas emissions are at record levels; ecosystems are shrinking; and food insecurity remains above prepandemic levels in many regions. Access to water and sanitation has improved steadily, and the share of people at high risk from climate hazards has fallen even as the absolute number of people facing such risk has grown. But progress is uneven; the gaps that remain are vast; and the pressures are affecting disproportionately the poor and those people who have only recently climbed out of poverty, thereby eroding the foundations on which durable poverty reduction depends. Climate risk is an important direct channel through which a deteriorating planet impacts poor households. The report's climate risk indicator combines exposure to damaging climate hazards-such as flooding, drought, tropical cyclones, or extreme heat expected over a lifetime-with vulnerability, that is, a lack of capacity to cope if a hazard strikes. The vulnerability can be physical (limited access to water and electricity or limited mobility) or economic (low income, little education, no social protection, no access to financial services). Around 4.5 billion people live in exposed areas, and 2.8 billion live in highly vulnerable conditions; about 1.5 billion people experience both types of vulnerability. The drivers of multidimensional poverty also drive climate vulnerability. The same lack of schooling and missing infrastructure that identify a household as multidimensionally poor can convert a flood or a drought from a hazard into a catastrophe. Globally, greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions continue to increase year on year. GHG emissions, excluding those from land use, forestry, and changes in land use, reached 52.8 metric gigatons of carbon dioxide equivalent (GtCO₂e) in 2024, about 48.0 percent above the levels in 2000, although they rose by only 0.6 percent in 2024. While the rate of increase seems to be declining since 2000, emissions have yet to peak and decline. This will lead to continued global temperature increases and global impacts, including increased damages from weather events, rising costs of adaptation, and exposure of more people to risks they may not be able to absorb. Emissions per unit of gross domestic product are nonetheless declining. Almost all major emitters have reduced emissions intensity in recent years, and several of the largest emitters

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026 (Japan, the United States, and several European Union countries) have been reducing the level of absolute emissions over the past decade. The health of key ecosystems is also deteriorating. Tropical primary forest loss reached 6.7 million hectares in 2024, nearly twice the level of 2023. Fires overtook agriculture as the leading driver of loss. Forests, wetlands, and other ecosystems support livelihoods and economic activity by regulating water, sustaining agricultural production, storing carbon, and reducing exposure to environmental hazards. These services are especially important in poorer economies, where renewable natural capital represents a large share of national wealth. If ecosystems are degraded, poor households lose resources and forms of protection that are difficult to replace. Food and nutrition insecurity remain widespread. In 2024, 2.3 billion people experienced moderate or severe food insecurity, while around 150 million children under 5 years of age were stunted, which is associated with consequences that can persist throughout life. Climate shocks, ecosystem degradation, and food price volatility can deepen these deprivations, especially among households that spend a large share of their incomes on food and have few assets available to cope. Progress on water and sanitation is also too slow. Around 2.1 billion people still lack access to safely managed drinking water. Rural populations and people in Sub-Saharan Africa face the largest gaps. These deficits are a form of deprivation, and they also raise the vulnerability to climate shocks. Droughts, floods, and extreme heat place the greatest strain on communities where water and sanitation systems are already weak. The planetary indicators contradict the widely held presumption that there is a conflict between the goal of ending extreme poverty and the goal of protecting the climate. The effort to eradicate extreme poverty is estimated to add less than 5 percent to global emissions. The extreme poor contribute the least to global emissions, but are the most vulnerable to the consequences of climate change. A benefit-cost comparison makes the asymmetry explicit. Weighing the people whom a percentage point of economic growth lifts out of poverty on a scale against the people who may be pushed into poverty by the emissions associated with the economic growth yields a benefit-cost ratio above 100 in the poorest countries and a corresponding ratio below 1 in many of the richest countries. Economic growth in places where the poor are concentrated has poverty reduction benefits that dwarf the near-term costs of the associated emissions.

## More and better jobs are needed to tackle

### global poverty

Poor households have-almost without exception-only a single productive asset: human capital. Land, physical capital, and savings are scarce at the bottom of the distribution, and the routes to acquiring these assets are limited. Since it was initiated in 2016, this report series has

-----

Overview

recorded the traits in the global profile of the poor, such as rural residence, low educational attainment, and the high share of children in the household, but has not detailed the link to jobs. This shortcoming has been caused by the lack of a sufficient database combining information on poverty and information on jobs at a global scale. This report has relied on the construction of such a database, which covers 96 percent of the world's population. The jobs profile complements the familiar profile of the traits of poverty. Rural poverty is associated with agricultural work. Two-thirds of extremely poor workers are in farm jobs and are among the most exposed to climate variability and ecosystem stress. The education deficit becomes an earnings deficit among these workers. Poor workers earn little because the skills and knowledge they have are poorly matched to the demands of higherpaying labor markets. The demographic profile illustrates a dependency burden. A working adult in a poor household supports roughly three times as many dependents as a working adult in a rich household. The missing second earner in poor households is most often a woman. Employment among women is lowest globally among households in the lower middle of the distribution. Low female labor market participation is a structural driver of household poverty. Labor income is the product of two factors: the amount of work a household supplies and the amount of earnings people receive if they work. This report examines poverty through each of these factors. It finds that neither factor alone accounts for the persistence of poverty and that addressing only one of these factors is insufficient to end poverty. On employment rates, the poor fall only slightly short of the nonpoor. About three in five working-age adults in poor households are employed, some 6 percentage points below the rate among adults in nonpoor households. Joblessness thus accounts for only part of global poverty. The near parity in employment rates is a global average. The employment gap between the poor and the nonpoor is small or absent in poorer economies and wide in richer economies. It also conceals two shortfalls. Poor workers put in about three to four fewer hours a week relative to nonpoor workers, whether wage earners or self-employed, and the gap is largest in the poorest countries and among the self-employed. So, more work means not only a job but sufficient hours in the job. In poor households, the shortfall in employment is concentrated among women. Men are employed at rates of about 75 percent; women at far lower rates. Over the past two decades, the gap between the poor and the nonpoor has widened. From 2003 to 2024, employment rates among the poor fell in 88 of 138 countries, and, in 99 of 137 countries, they grew more slowly or fell more quickly than the rates among the nonpoor at the $8.30-a-person-a-day poverty line (in 2021 purchasing power parity terms). Longer schooling among the young does not account for the decline, which is present among adults ages 25-64 as well. This report points to three mechanisms that it does not test directly. First, agriculture has shed workers more quickly than other sectors have absorbed them, and the poor, concentrated in agriculture, have been the first to be affected. Second, technical change may have lowered the demand for less well educated workers. Third, women leaving agriculture face constraints in taking up other work, ranging from care responsibilities and social norms to transport. Among the poor, the exit from farming may more often therefore end in nonemployment.

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026 Combining both employment rates and earnings, this report shows that, over these two decades, growth in earnings per worker accounted for essentially all the welfare growth among the poorest households, while declines in employment rates reduced welfare growth. Poverty continued to decline as long as the growth in earnings more than offset the shrinking employment rate among the poor. The past two decades thus represent a missed opportunity rather than a verdict on employment. Had the level of employment among the poor been maintained, the same earnings growth would have reduced poverty more quickly. How much can more jobs and better-paying jobs contribute to poverty reduction? Simulations undertaken for this report show that raising the employment rate among poor households to a benchmark of 75 percent of working-age adults would reduce the global poverty rate by a fifth. This is roughly the employment rate already observed among men in poor households, among poor households two decades ago, and in the richest quintile of the global population. Reaching this rate would therefore require closing much of the gender gap in employment because nearly all the shortfall is among women. Raising the earnings associated with the jobs held by the poor would reduce poverty further. Lifting the educational attainment of poor workers to the nonpoor average would cut the extreme poverty rate by nearly half. Labor movements out of agriculture and out of self-employment into wage work would contribute additional rate reductions. In these simulations, the additional employment and the higher earnings reach different populations. The earnings gains are proportionally greatest among the poorest. The simulations assign broadly similar absolute gains to workers with similar characteristics, and, so, the gain is proportionally largest among the poorest workers. Meanwhile, the employment gains are concentrated among the near-poor, among whom the nonworking adults are disproportionately women. Better pay or higher returns to work (such as among smallholders or microenterprises) benefits the destitute the most. More work benefits the near-poor the most.

## Better-paying work depends on

### productivity growth and the jobs that firms create

The simulations reported above draw on household surveys, which describe the labor market from the viewpoint of households. The employment and earnings the surveys record represent the joint product of the labor that households supply and the demand for this labor. Household surveys provide information on workers and their families, but only limited information on the firms that might employ these people or on the smallholder and microenterprise ecosystems

-----

Overview

in which the self-employed work. The simulations also hold wages fixed and displace no incumbents. So, the impacts they report represent upper bounds. What has happened over the past two decades shows why these limits matter. The contraction of subsistence farming has fed mostly into nonagricultural forms of microenterprise or selfemployment, mostly in services, rather than into higher-paying wage work. This represents a pattern of incomplete structural transformation. The distinction matters because a given rate of growth reduces poverty more quickly if it is accompanied by a movement of workers and earnings out of low-return agriculture than if the sectoral composition of the movement is neutral. Whether the poor can reach better-paying work depends on two factors: whether such work is created and whether workers have the skills and qualifications employers in higherpaying work require. Raising the productivity of the work the poor already do, especially on smallholder farms, is a route that does not depend on the creation of such jobs, and it is the route with the shortest horizon. The simulations are not the only evidence. Additional evidence is supplied by household surveys that track job transitions into wage jobs and self-employment, out of farming, or into or out of work altogether and that track whether households escaped poverty or fell into poverty during the same years. Longitudinal surveys in 41 economies confirm that both more work and better-paying work are associated with poverty reduction. However, the job transitions that drive this result vary. A transition into a wage job is associated with poverty reduction in richer economies, while a transition out of agriculture into nonagricultural work of any kind matters most in poorer economies. If the share of a poor household's working-age members in wage employment rises by 10 percentage points, the household's chance of escaping poverty increases by 2.4 percentage points in upper-middle-income economies and by 2.2 percentage points in high-income economies, but by only 0.8 percentage points in low- and lower-middle-income economies. A shift into nonwage work raises the chance of escaping poverty by 0.5 percentage points in the poorest economies, not far below the corresponding effect of wage employment in these same economies, while the estimate is not distinguishable from zero in upper-middleincome economies and negative in high-income economies. Poor households in the poorest economies are held back twice over. The wage work within their reach may not generate the income necessary to lift these households out of poverty, as these estimates show, and such work is also scarce. Over the period under observation, the share of working-age adults in wage employment rose by 3.4 percentage points among poor households in the poorest economies, against 7.7 percentage points in high-income economies. Access to a job and access to decent pay are both needed, and, in the poorest economies, both are in short supply. The longitudinal data also show that, in addition to lifting households out of poverty, employment helps keep households from sliding into poverty, though not always through the same type of work. Self-employment in agriculture and outside agriculture protects households against falling into poverty in rich and poor economies alike.

-----

Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026

## Concentrated poverty can be tackled, but

### progress is unlikely to be swift

The findings of this report support neither complacency nor despair. Most of the world has left extreme poverty behind, and the poverty that remains is concentrated geographically, socially, and across the dimensions of deprivation. Concentration establishes where the remaining poverty is located and who suffers from this poverty. Designing policies to reduce poverty depends on understanding both dimensions. The task will not be easy. This report documents that poverty is concentrated among the countries and the households that have been the hardest to reach; in fragile states; in regions vulnerable to climate risks; and in families with low education, low skills, and many dependents. The challenge of concentrated poverty cannot be separated from the condition of the planet on which the poor live. The coming decades will bring continued warming, mounting pressure on ecosystems, and growing food and water stress. At the same time, poverty reduction must accelerate. Investments in climate resilience can therefore also be investments in poverty reduction. Protecting ecosystems helps preserve the natural asset base on which many poor households depend. Limiting the peaks and duration of warming reduces the risks borne disproportionately by people who have contributed the least to the problem and possess the fewest resources to cope. Each route out of poverty examined above-employment restored to a level the poor attained two decades ago, schooling raised to the level the nonpoor already have, shifts into betterpaying jobs, and the economic growth needed for all three-can be quantified: how much poverty each would remove and which poor households each would reach. Such change rarely occurs quickly. Education among the poor typically yields impacts only after the poor cohorts successfully complete the transition from school to work. The barriers are great. Employment rates among the poor, especially poor women, are stagnant or falling. In some regions, a sizable share of the population remains vulnerable to climate hazards. Inequality is often persistent. Still, progress is possible even under such challenging circumstances, as demonstrated by the improvements in well-being over the last three decades. The broad focus of this report, to monitor this progress, is not meant to be a passive record of slowly moving numbers, but a road map that keeps the conditions and the opportunities for poverty reduction in view.

## Note

1. These are biennial reports. The Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2024 is the new edition of the *Poverty and Shared Prosperity Reports 2016, 2018, 2020, and 2022.*

# 1

## Global Poverty: Stalled Progress and Rising Concentration

## Summary

- *The global extreme poverty rate fell steadily for three decades, from 43 percent in 1990 to* *11 percent in 2020. This was an incredible accomplishment. However, progress has since come* *to a standstill. The extreme poverty rate has remained essentially unchanged after an increase* *during the COVID pandemic and the subsequent recovery. In 2026, 811 million people-1 in* *10 globally-were living in extreme poverty, defined as living on less than $3.00-a-person-a-day* *in 2021 purchasing power parity (PPP) terms.*
- *Extreme poverty is increasingly concentrated. More than half the extreme poor globally are* *living in only nine economies, and three-quarters in 20 economies. Of the nine economies, six* *are in Sub-Saharan Africa, where the extreme poverty rate has remained near 44 percent since* *2014. Only in 2026 did poverty rates in Sub-Saharan Africa return to prepandemic levels,* *while this is still not the case in the Middle East, North Africa, Afghanistan, and Pakistan* *(MENAAP) region.*
- *Fragility is the defining feature of global poverty. The share of the extreme poor living in a* *fragile and conflict-affected situation (FCS) rose from 12 percent in 2010 to 57 percent in* *2026, as more high-poverty economies became fragile and because extreme poverty fell more* *quickly in non-FCS economies. By 2030, 87 percent of the extreme poor globally will be living in* *Sub-Saharan Africa or in an FCS economy or both.*
- *If the per capita economic growth rates observed over 2015-24 persist, the global extreme* *poverty rate and the number of people living in extreme poverty will increase by 2030: an* *unprecedented reversal of the decades-long decline. Population growth in high-poverty* *economies is a key factor in the potential increase.*

- *People living in poor households are disproportionately young, rural, and less educated.* *Eighteen percent of children under the age of18 are living in extreme poverty, compared with* *6 percent among adults, and individuals with no formal education have a poverty rate of* *22 percent. Extreme poverty rates in rural areas are more than three times higher than urban* *rates. Although extreme poverty declined across most demographic groups between 2013 and* *2023, progress was slowest among households caring for children in which none or only one* *working-age adult was working.*
- *Subnational data reveal that poverty is concentrated not only in specific economies, but in* *specific regions within these economies. Poverty has been stagnating or rising in many regions* *within economies in Sub-Saharan Africa and MENAAP over the past decade.*

The largest differences are across poverty* *measures in South Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa.*

## Gains in global poverty reduction could be

### reversed in coming decades

Following the decades during which global extreme poverty fell at historic rates, the decline has come to a dramatic stop.1 Extreme poverty is increasingly concentrated in economies in Sub-Saharan Africa and in economies experiencing fragility, conflict, and violence. In these economies, poverty rates have generally stagnated or risen. Population growth in these economies, combined with rates of poverty reduction that are insufficient to sustain the progress achieved during the preceding years, means that the number of people living in extreme poverty globally is projected to increase in coming decades.

### After decades of progress, the reduction in extreme poverty has ground to a halt

After decades of significant and steady progress, the reduction of global poverty has reached a critical juncture. In 1990, nearly 2.3 billion people-43 percent of the global population-were living in extreme poverty, defined as living on less than $3.00-a-person-a-day (in 2021 PPP terms) (refer to box 1.1). By 2019, the number had fallen dramatically, to 837 million people, or 10.8 percent of the global population. But, since then, virtually no progress on reducing extreme poverty has been made. The COVID-19 pandemic triggered the largest setback in decades (Mahler et al. 2022). In 2026, 811 million people-1 in 10 globally-were living in extreme poverty, and this rate has remained essentially unchanged since 2020. Factoring in the economic growth and price impacts of the conflict in the Middle East suggests that the extreme poverty rate may be even higher (refer to box 1.2).

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

## BOX 1.1

###### Updated poverty lines carry important implications

This report relies on updated global poverty lines to incorporate the latest available data on purchasing power parities (PPPs), as compiled by the International Comparison Program. In June 2025, the base year for comparing price levels across economies was updated from 2017 to 2021 (Alfani et al. 2025). PPPs are used to convert welfare data and national poverty lines from local currency units into dollars.a As part of the update, the global poverty lines were revised to reflect updated national poverty lines set by national authorities around the world. The World Bank's global poverty lines are based on the national poverty lines that are used by governments to monitor poverty within national borders. The international poverty line is the median national poverty line among low-income economies. It thus reflects the typical standard by which governments in the poorest economies define poverty at the national level. Upward revisions in national poverty lines, especially in West Africa, have stemmed from improvements in household survey methodologies that have resulted in higher measured consumption and thus also upward revisions in national poverty lines. These higher national poverty lines do not reflect a more generous standard of what it means to be poor. They reflect better data to assess the cost of basic needs for a minimum food bundle and an allowance for essential nonfood consumption. Combined, the 2021 PPPs and the latest national poverty lines have contributed to higher global poverty lines (refer to table B1.1.1). They do not constitute a movement of the goalpost in poverty measurement. Rather, they reflect evolving standards of how the poorest countries measure consumption and evolving differences in the cost of living across countries as captured by an established methodology (World Bank 2025d).

## BOX 1.2

###### Tracking the impact on poverty of the conflict in the Middle East

The conflict in the Middle East and the associated increase in energy prices impacted projected economic growth in 2026. Nearly two in three countries were facing weaker growth prospects in June 2026 compared with the outlook in Global Economic *Prospects, January 2026 (World Bank 2026b, 2026c). According to the projections* of Global Economic Prospects, June 2026 (World Bank 2026c), commodity prices were expected to rise by 22 percent in 2026, while, according to earlier projections in January 2026, they had been expected to decline by 7 percent in 2026 (World Bank 2026b). The higher commodity prices led to a resurgence of inflationary pressures similar to the situation observed at the start of the Russian Federation's invasion of Ukraine, which resulted in a reduction in food production, particularly of wheat. What was different this time was that the shock has primarily affected energy prices, and these exert an impact on household budgets directly, but also indirectly through, for example, higher transport costs for food. Furthermore, there has been a significant rise in fertilizer prices, which has affected food production throughout 2026. More generally, energy intensity varies by economic sectors. So, household income can be affected differentially depending on the sector of employment. Record maize harvests in South Africa (Crop Estimates Committee 2026) fixed fertilizer prices in some countries, such as India (PIB 2026), and the low take-up of fertilizers in Sub-Saharan Africa (FAO 2025) generally could mitigate the impact on food prices. Furthermore, the overall effect of higher food prices on extreme poverty is complicated by the fact that many of the poor are food producers and could thus benefit from higher food prices (Ivanic and Martin 2008). It is thus crucial to understand what happens to input costs. If the increase in food prices is driven by input costs (for instance, fertilizer or transport costs), there are no net benefits among food producers, who may even be less well off if there is a reduction in food demand.

*(continued)*

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

###### BOX 1.2

###### Tracking the impact on poverty of the conflict in the Middle East (continued)

The 2026 nowcasts for poverty presented throughout this report use the growth rates in real per capita gross domestic product (GDP) of Global Economic *Prospects, June 2026 (World Bank 2026c).a The baseline estimates assume that* the incomes of everyone in the country grow at the same rate. However, higher prices for food and energy will affect households to a varying degree. Typically, poorer households spend a larger share of their budgets on food and energy and are thus more sensitive to price changes for these items. To investigate the disproportionate impact, two methods allow variation in the intensity of the shock faced by households (Kimura et al. 2026). In both cases, the average growth of the economy is maintained at the level indicated in Global *Economic Prospects, June 2026 (World Bank 2026c), which captures the aggregate* income and price shock. First, households are allocated sectoral growth rates based on their sector of employment. Second, inflation is allocated to households depending on their budget's exposure to food and energy. Poorer households typically spend a greater share on food and energy and thus receive lower real income if the prices of these items rise. A distribution-neutral assumption, which is used as the baseline throughout the report, would instead assume the equal distribution of inflationary shocks. The baseline using projections of Global Economic Prospects, June 2026 (World Bank 2026c) provides estimates of the number of people living in extreme poverty at 811 million people in 2026 (refer to figure B1.2.1). This is an increase of 2.5 million people relative to the preconflict growth projections of Global Economic Prospects, January 2026 (World Bank 2026b). While these differences are not solely attributable to the conflict, the numbers confirm a worsening outlook. Allowing for distributional impacts either from uneven income shocks or price shocks leads to a further increase compared with the preconflict projections and essentially a stagnation in extreme poverty relative to 2025 (when the number was 816.9 million people). Taking the differential impact of sectoral growth rates into consideration reveals that 816.6 million people were estimated to be living in extreme poverty in 2026. One of the main reasons is that projected agricultural growth is much lower than the growth projected in the service and industrial sectors in 2026. Because a larger share of the extreme poor work in the agricultural sector, poverty reduction is muted because of the low agricultural growth. This also highlights that it is important for the poor to engage in resilient high-growth sectors to favor poverty reduction (refer to chapter 4).

*(continued)*

Baseline Income shock Price shock Preconflict growth projections *Sources: Kimura et al. 2026; World Bank calculations based on data from the Poverty and Inequality Platform* (version September 2026), World Bank, https://pip.worldbank.org/home; World Bank 2026b, 2026c. *Note: The figure reports the trend in millions of poor people from 2023 to 2026, as assessed against the international* poverty line of $3.00-a-person-a-day. The baseline scenario relies on distribution-neutral growth forecasts in Global Economic *Prospects, June2026 (World Bank 2026c) to nowcast poverty for 2026. The income shock scenario allows for* distributional changes by combining household employment data with sectoral growth rates, while the price shock scenario captures disproportionate household impacts based on the household exposure to food and energy spending. The preconflict scenario applies the forecasts in Global Economic Prospects, January2026(World Bank 2026b). In both the income and price shock scenarios, the growth in average income or consumption matches the forecast of the World Bank in June 2026 (World Bank 2026c).

The differential price shock adjusts the real growth rates faced by households based on their food and energy expenditure. Growth is downgraded among households that have above average food and energy exposure, while it is revised upward for the rest to match the overall mean growth in the baseline. With these price shock assumptions, the number of people living in extreme poverty in 2026 is estimated at 813 million people, slightly below the 817 million in 2025.

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

###### BOX 1.2

###### Tracking the impact on poverty of the conflict in the Middle East (continued)

Since January 2026, the global economy has experienced a series of significant developments. There is currently great uncertainty regarding the trajectory of the conflict in the Middle East and its implications for the global economy in 2026, as well as any potential recovery beyond that. Furthermore, El Niño is still intensifying toward a projected peak in late 2026 and already approaching new records. This is driving impacts in food security, incomes, and livelihoods across the globe, including effects on nutrition and human capital that could persist for years.

a. In the case of economies that use a consumption aggregate, a pass-through rate of 0.7 is applied, compared with a rate of 1.0 (full pass-through) in the case of economies that rely on an income aggregates based on the empirical evidence provided by Mahler et al. (2022).

The speed of extreme poverty reduction has shifted markedly over time. Between 1990 and 2004, the global rate of extreme poverty fell from 43 percent to 30 percent, with hundreds of millions of people pulled out of extreme poverty. As the fight against extreme poverty gained momentum in populous economies, most notably China, the global rate of extreme poverty fell starkly in the years after 2004, delivering substantial progress toward the goal of reducing extreme poverty across the globe. However, the momentum shifted in the late 2010s, even before the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic. The slowdown reflected persistently low economic growth in low-income economies leading to an increasing concentration of the global poor in these economies (World Bank 2018, 2024a). After a rise in the incidence of extreme poverty in 2020, poverty rates began to decline, though at a slower pace than previously. Had poverty continued to decline at the pace observed between 2000 and 2013, the extreme poverty rate would have reached roughly 7 percent by 2026, some 3 percentage points below the actual rate (refer to figure 1.1). In absolute terms, this gap represents approximately 240 million more people living in extreme poverty than would have been the case under the earlier trajectory of poverty reduction. The slowdown relative to the 2000-13 trend is only observable at the $3.00 line. At the higher poverty lines (for example, $4.20), actual poverty reduction has broadly kept pace with the pre-2013 trend.

Despite this progress in reducing the poverty rates at higher poverty lines, nearly half the world's population in 2026, approximately 3.7 billion people, were living on less than $8.30-a-person-a-day, that is, the value of the poverty line typically associated with uppermiddle-income economies (refer to figure 1.2). Thus, although the share of people living below this line dropped from 70 percent in 1990 to 44 percent in 2026, the number of people living below the line did not decline (World Bank 2024a). This stagnation reflects both the fact that the population grew in low- and middle-income economies over the period and the fact that the global progress in pushing incomes above the revised and higher poverty lines has been slower (refer to box 1.1). Many of the people living between the international poverty line (the $3.00-a-person-a-day line) and the $8.30 line have escaped extreme poverty without attaining economic security. The world's middle class is concentrated in middle-income economies (refer to box 1.3).

## BOX 1.3

**The global middle class has grown, but economic security remains out of reach for many**

Living above the poverty line does not necessarily mean one has achieved middle-class status. A household that has escaped poverty may still face a substantial risk of falling back into poverty after an illness, a job loss, or a bad harvest, and this risk is what distinguishes the vulnerable from the middle class. In the approach developed by López-Calva and Ortiz-Juárez (2014), a household is middle class if the probability it will fall into poverty is low. The income level at which this probability becomes low is the economic security threshold, which marks the lower boundary of the middle class. Between the poor and the middle class lies a third group that is living above the poverty line, but does not enjoy sufficient security to have middle-class status. A large middle class is widely regarded as a foundation of stable growth because it helps sustain

*(continued)*

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

###### BOX 1.3

**The global middle class has grown, but economic security remains out of reach for many**

*(continued)*

domestic demand, investment in education, and support for institutions that mediate distributional tensions (Banerjee and Duflo 2008; Easterly 2001). It is also a product of growth. As an economy's average income rises, so does the share of the population that has attained economic security. The middle-income economies, home to about three-quarters of the world's population (World Bank 2024b), are precisely the ones in which this transition is under way and far enough along that extreme poverty has receded, but not so far that security is the norm. The thresholds used in this report can serve as approximate class boundaries. Each of the three poverty lines is the typical national poverty line of an income group: $3.00 a day for low-income economies, $4.20 for lower-middle-income economies, and $8.30 for upper-middle-income economies (refer to box 1.1). Households living on less than $3.00 a day are extremely poor by any standard. Those living on between $3.00 and $8.30 are above the extreme poverty line, but would count as poor in a middle-income economy and, for the most part, remain short of economic security. Those living on between $8.30 and $28.00 a day are above the poverty line of every developing economy and constitute a global middle class; and those above $28.00 a day exceed the prosperity standard this report uses to gauge shared prosperity (refer to chapter 2). These thresholds are proxies. Ongoing work indicates that the income required for security is higher than any poverty line. Applying the vulnerability approach to the World Bank's harmonized household surveys-estimating, for each economy, the probability that a household is living on less than the poverty line of the relevant income group over a few years, and identifying the consumption level at which this probability becomes low-yields economic security lines of about $6.00, $7.00, and $14.00 a day for the three income groups. This is roughly twice the corresponding poverty lines, but with wide variation across economies (Bolch et al. 2026). The threshold also shifts with the risks households face. In Europe and Central Asia, the income needed to maintain a low probability of falling into poverty rose by about 10 percent in real terms between 2008 and 2018 as the shocks pushing households into poverty grew larger (Bussolo et al. 2025). There is no fixed income at which security begins. The boundary of the middle class shifts with the environment in which households live. Measured against these thresholds, the global middle class has grown substantially since 2000, roughly doubling as a share of the world's population. The group living on between $3.00 and $8.30 a day, however, has remained close to a third of the world's population throughout the period. Among large numbers of people, the exit from extreme poverty has thus represented an exit into vulnerability rather than into security.

###### BOX 1.3

**The global middle class has grown, but economic security remains out of reach for many**

*(continued)*

in 2000 to a majority by 2026, and extreme poverty has all but disappeared. In today's lower-middle-income economies, the group living on between $3.00 and $8.30 a day is now the largest, and the middle class, though growing, remains a minority. In low-income economies, the distribution has barely shifted. About half the population is still living on less than $3.00 a day, and the middle class is negligible. High-income economies, by contrast, are composed almost entirely of households living on more than $8.30 a day, and the majority are above the prosperity standard. These patterns connect the report's monitoring to the concerns of middle-income economies. More work and better-paying work help people escape poverty (refer to chapter 4). Stable, well-paid jobs also protect against falling back into poverty, and they therefore help households climb from vulnerability to security, an agenda that has particular force in locations where the vulnerable group is largest. Vulnerability to climate-related hazards-a distinct concept, reflecting the lack of the means to cope if a hazard strikes rather than the risk of falling back into poverty-extends well above the poverty line (refer to chapter 3). Vulnerability in this case means that middle-class households are also exposed to risk if social protection and financial services are lacking. The $8.30 line and the $28.00 prosperity standard are precisely the boundaries within which the middle class is found.

The upward shift in the global distribution of welfare between 1990 and 2024 that resulted in poverty declines was highly uneven across income groups (refer to figure 1.3). In 1990, the global distribution was heavily concentrated at the lowest income levels. Half the world was living on less than $3.55 a day. By 2024, however, many people had moved above the international poverty line, and the median income had risen to $9.20 a day, reflecting substantive improvements in living standards across much of the globe. Yet, in today's lowincome economies, the welfare distribution has shifted only slightly in a more positive direction over the past three decades. Meanwhile, in the lower- and upper-middle-income economies of today, populations that were clustered around or even below the $3.00 thresholds in 1990 have, since then, shifted substantially in a positive direction. This partly reflects the dramatic welfare increases in China and India.

Overlapping crises and stagnation in some regions may explain these patterns and the global slowdown in poverty reduction (Bussolo et al. 2008; Imam and Temple 2026). In previous decades, rapid poverty reduction in East Asia and Pacific and, more recently, in South Asia, where rates of extreme poverty fell from above 50 percent to low single digits, largely drove progress in reducing global rates of extreme poverty (refer to figure 1.4). Latin America and the Caribbean also experienced substantial declines, though progress in that region has stagnated in the most recent decade.

Sub-Saharan Africa, in contrast, experienced much more modest improvement.2 There, extreme poverty rates declined from 62 percent in 1990 to 43 percent in 2026. The extreme poverty rate has declined little in the region since 2014. In the Middle East, North Africa, Afghanistan, and Pakistan region (refer to box 1.4 for information on World Bank regional classifications), extreme poverty has been increasing in recent years. In both regions, low rates of per capita economic growth have undermined efforts to reduce poverty (Beegle et al. 2016). The COVID-19 pandemic has had a particularly persistent impact in these regions, where low rates of economic growth make recovery from such shocks a lengthy prospect. In MENAAP, poverty rates at the $3.00 and $4.20 lines have not yet returned to prepandemic levels, while 2026 is the first year in which poverty rates at the $3.00 line are projected to be back at the prepandemic level in Sub-Saharan Africa (World Bank 2024a) (refer to figure 1.4). The world's low-income economies are overwhelmingly concentrated in these two regions.3 Meanwhile, the conflict in the Middle East has generated new economic strains. Rising energy, food, and fertilizer prices are projected to have slowed the pace of poverty reduction additionally between 2025 and 2026 (refer to box 1.2).
