Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

###### BOX 1.4

###### The Poverty and Inequality Platform has been updated with new data and revised regional classifications

The data on poverty and inequality used throughout the report are available through the World Bank's online tool for statistics on poverty, inequality, and shared prosperity: the Poverty and Inequality Platform (PIP). The PIP data build primarily on national household surveys and are usually updated twice a year as national statistical offices around the world make new data available. The availability of recent survey data is crucial for the accuracy and reliability of the platform's estimates of poverty and shared prosperity. Any specific region is considered covered by data in a given year if the economies with at least one survey within a three-year window on either side of the year account for at least 50 percent of the region's population. Because of a lack of data, neither the Middle East and North Africa nor Sub-Saharan Africa were included among the post-2020 data in Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2024 (World Bank 2024a). Since then, more recent data have become available for a number of populous economies, greatly increasing the regional and global coverage in the post-COVID years. The updates include data on Ethiopia (2021) and Nigeria (2022/23), which results in coverage for Sub-Saharan Africa up to 2024, as well as new data for the Arab Republic of Egypt (2021/22), India (2022/23, 2023/24), and Pakistan (2024/25). As part of the September 2025 PIP update, new regional groupings were implemented to align with the revised World Bank regional groups. First, until then, some high-income economies had been excluded from the geographic regions, but included as a separate group referred to as Other High Income or Rest of the World. Now, all high-income economies are included in the corresponding geographic regions, which means that a new region, North America, has been added. Second, the World Bank recently moved Afghanistan and Pakistan from the South Asia region to the Middle East and North Africa region, now labeled Middle East, North Africa, Afghanistan, and Pakistan (World Bank 2025d). The only World Bank region in which the composition did not change in the revision is Sub-Saharan Africa. Up-to-date estimates for the previously used regional classifications are available on the PIP website to allow for comparisons with previous editions of the report. Annex tables 1C.1-1C.3 provide an overview of poverty rates, numbers, and shares by region.

The disparities are even more pronounced at higher global poverty lines, which show that poverty rates are stagnating or rising in MENAAP and Sub-Saharan Africa, indicating that, even if households escape extreme poverty, they remain vulnerable to falling back into extreme poverty (refer to box 1.3 and annex 1B, figure 1B.1). These higher poverty lines highlight significant challenges in other regions as well. In South Asia, for instance, more than 70 percent of the population is living on less than $8.30 a day.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 The growth patterns underlying these trends reveal why progress has stalled. Growth incidence curves show that, between 2000 and 2019, most regions experienced positive welfare growth across the distribution. East Asia and Pacific recorded the strongest and most broad-based gains, followed by the Europe and Central Asia region and the Latin America and Caribbean region (refer to figure 1.5). In Sub-Saharan Africa, welfare grew at around 2 percent annually across the distribution. Since 2019, welfare growth in the region has averaged near zero among the poorest and turned negative across the upper half of the distribution (Wu et al. 2024).

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

Welfare growth in MENAAP has shown a concerning pattern. Between 2000 and 2019, welfare grew modestly, at around 1.5 percent annually, across most of the distribution. However, welfare growth among the poorest percentiles has turned negative since 2020, even as incomes higher in the distribution have continued to grow modestly. This means that the poorest are not only failing to escape poverty, but are falling farther behind. Both insufficient growth and high levels of inequality limit how effectively growth translates into poverty reduction. Since 2010, per capita incomes in low-income economies have barely risen, averaging less than 0.1 percent growth annually (World Bank 2025a). The sustained growth accelerations that have historically driven large poverty reductions in low-income economies have largely disappeared (World Bank 2025b). Global per capita income growth in low-income countries is projected to slow to its weakest pace since the COVID-19 pandemic, amid renewed energy-price shocks from the conflict in the Middle East (World Bank 2026c). Elsewhere, growth has remained stronger and more inclusive. South Asia, in particular, has shown strong, broad-based welfare gains across the distribution.4

### Poverty is now concentrated in economies where progress has stopped

Extreme poverty is highly concentrated geographically. More than half the extreme poor across the globe are concentrated in only 9 economies, and three-quarters are living in 20 economies (refer to figure 1.6).5 Of the nine economies, six are in Sub-Saharan Africa; the others are India, Pakistan, and the Republic of Yemen. The countries with high populations of poor people are on the list because of large populations or because of high poverty rates, or both. For example, India is among the top nine economies by poor population not because of a high extreme poverty rate, but because it is the most populous country in the world. Whereas India's extreme poverty rate has decreased substantially over the last 15 years, poverty rates have either stagnated or increased in the remaining eight economies. These statistics are subject to uncertainty, however. In some of these economies, such as Afghanistan and the Federal Republic of Somalia, no survey data exist, and poverty estimates must therefore be imputed (Mahler et al. 2026). In others, such as the Republic of Yemen, the last survey with relevant data is several years old, and estimates must be extrapolated forward. The concentration of poverty shifts at higher poverty lines (refer to annex 1B, figure 1B.2), and Sub-Saharan Africa becomes less dominant. More populous economies, notably India, make up a larger share of the global poor if poverty is measured at $4.20- or $8.30-a-person-a-day.

This unevenness in the location of the poor partly reflects the size of populations across economies. A dissimilarity index has been developed to measure the extent to which the concentration of poverty exceeds estimates based on population size alone if poverty rates were evenly distributed across economies by population size (refer to annex 1D). If an economy has a share of the global poor that is equal to its share of the global population, the index is 0. The closer the index is to 1, the more skewed poverty is relative to an economy's population size. Globally, the index rose from 0.31 in 2000 to 0.33 in 2010 and 0.65 in 2024, more than doubling in a quarter century. The 20 percent of the global population living in the economies with the

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration highest rates of poverty accounted for roughly 37 percent of the extreme poor in 2000; by 2024, the share had risen to around 85 percent. Today's low-income economies face greater obstacles than their predecessors. In 2000, today's low-income economies had per capita incomes around 60 percent of the mean per capita income in economies that have since graduated to middle-income status. By 2023, the ratio had fallen to 30 percent, as incomes in low-income economies stagnated, while those in economies that have since graduated continued to grow (World Bank 2025a). Today's low-income economies are also doing worse than they did in the past. The 26 economies classified as low-income economies today reduced extreme poverty at more than 2 percent annually during the 2000s, with average gross domestic product (GDP) per capita in each growing at a rate close to 3 percent (refer to figure 1.7). Progress has since slowed sharply. Poverty reduction nearly stalled between 2010 and 2019, and, in the decade ending in 2024, extreme poverty in these economies rose, while GDP per capita declined. The progress of the 2000s has thus not been sustained. While extreme poverty is increasingly concentrated in low-income economies, half the world's poor continue to live in middle-income economies. In 2025, low-income economies accounted for 49 percent of the world's extreme poor at the $3.00 line, up from 18 percent in 2010. This shift was driven largely by rapid poverty reduction in large middle-income economies, particularly China and India. The share of the extreme poor in today's lowermiddle-income economies declined by 8 percentage points, to 44 percent, in 2010-25 (refer to figure 1.8). At higher poverty lines, poverty is even more highly concentrated in middle-income

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

(refer to annex 1B, figure 1B.2). Across all poverty lines, the shares of the poor have risen in low-income economies, while they have fallen in upper-middle-income economies. Continued progress in middle-income economies, where structural growth challenges can impede sustained poverty reduction (World Bank 2024b), therefore remains pivotal to reducing global poverty. Many of the people who have escaped extreme poverty in middle-income economies are still living close to the $3.00 line. Incomes are clustered between $3.00 and $4.20 a day, well short of the economic security that defines the middle class (refer to box 1.3 and figure 1.3). This concentration means that the recent gains are fragile. Economic downturns, climate shocks, or conflict could push large numbers of people back into poverty, as the COVID-19 pandemic demonstrated (Mahler et al. 2022).

### After decades of progress, global extreme poverty is projected to rise

That poverty is now concentrated in economies where progress has stalled has profound implications for the outlook on extreme poverty. Because the population is also growing most rapidly in these same economies, the rising share of the global population in these economies is pulling the global poverty rate upward, even if national rates do not worsen. The stalled reduction in poverty may not merely be a temporary setback.6 If per capita growth in these economies continues at the pace observed over 2015-24, the global extreme poverty rate and the number of people living in extreme poverty will increase by 2030 (refer to figure 1.9). This would represent an unprecedented reversal of the decades-long decline in the global number of the extreme poor. If these economies were to achieve the growth rates they experienced during 2000-09, the global poverty rate would still remain above 8 percent in 2030 even under these more optimistic assumptions. Current GDP forecasts show slower poverty reduction, resulting in a poverty rate above 9 percent in 2030. Only under sustained annual global GDP growth of 4 percent would the extreme poverty rate approach the ambitious 3 percent goal by 2050, two decades later than the initial target year. These simulations hold within-country inequality constant at today's level. If economies manage to achieve pro-poor growth, the projected poverty rates would be lower.7 For example, growth in agriculture has been shown to benefit the poorest relatively more, although this effect dissipates as economies become richer (Beegle and Christiaensen 2019). Poverty rates in many low-income economies, particularly those in a fragile and conflictaffected situation, are projected to decline only marginally.8 These same economies are projected to experience substantial population growth (refer to figure 1.10).9 Thus, it is the growing population in economies with high poverty rates, not the rising poverty rates, that give these economies greater weight in the aggregate poverty rate and that is driving the projections of rising global poverty rates. Fragile economies that are involved in conflict are at the center of this pattern. Economies such as the Central African Republic, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Mozambique, Niger, Sudan, and the Federal Republic of Somalia combine some of the highest current poverty rates with some of the most rapid projected population growth.

Poverty reduction is especially difficult in economies affected by conflict and institutional weakness, which typically face compounding challenges even after the conflict has subsided (Corral et al. 2020; Federle et al. 2024; Korn and Stemmler 2025; Novta and Pugacheva 2021; World Bank 2025b). Conflict destroys assets and disrupts livelihoods, whereas poverty fuels grievances and weakens the capacity of the state to prevent violence (Corral et al. 2020). These challenges are compounded by the current global environment, in which economies dependent on energy imports and economies directly affected by conflict face the weakest growth prospects (World Bank 2026c). Furthermore, when a population grows quickly, the economy must expand even more quickly to deliver the same level of improvement in per capita welfare.

The global population living in FCS economies more than doubled between 2010 and 2025, rising from 6.0 percent to 13.5 percent. Over this period, a growing number of economies with large populations and high poverty rates became fragile or affected by conflict and violence.10 At the same time, poverty rates fell more rapidly in non-FCS economies, thereby increasing the share of FCS economies in the global total. As of 2026, more than half the extreme poor globally, 57 percent, were living in an FCS economy, up from 12 percent in 2010 (refer to figure 1.11, panel a). The extreme poverty rate in Sub-Saharan Africa is projected to decline by about 3 percentage points by 2030. Yet, the region's share of the global population is projected to rise over the period, thereby pushing the global poverty rate upward. The same holds for the MENAAP region, where poverty rates are projected to increase and the population continues to expand (refer to figure 1.11, panel b). By 2030, 87 percent of the extreme poor globally will be living either in Sub-Saharan Africa or in an FCS economy (refer to figure 1.11, panel a).

### Poverty persists unevenly across people and places

This section examines who the poor are by education, age, residency in urban or rural areas, and subregions within economies. It relies on investments in expanded databases to explore these links. The poverty estimates are constructed from household measures of consumption or income and assume an equal allocation of resources within households, regardless of age and gender.11

Poverty is highest among young people, people with low educational attainment, and in households caring for children **without two working-age adults**

Globally, populations are becoming more educated, and age composition is changing only slightly (refer to figure 1.12, panel a).12 As of 2024, almost three in four people globally were living in households with children, 39 percent in multiple-adult households, and 28 percent in two-adult households; only 2 percent were living in households with one female adult and children, and fewer than 1 percent in households in which senior adults alone cared for children (refer to figure 1.12, panel b).

Poverty varies sharply across these groups, even within economies at similar income levels (World Bank 2018). Children under age 18 face the highest rates of extreme poverty of any age-group. Individuals with no formal education face the highest rates of any educational group. Across these groups, rates of extreme poverty vary substantially, though all groups experienced declining poverty rates between 2013 and 2024. Age represents a stark divide. Children under age 18 were the most likely age-group to live in poor households in 2024, at 18 percent, compared with only 6 percent among adults (refer to figure 1.13, panel a). Poverty rates declined steadily with age, reflecting the vulnerability of households with many young dependents. The high share of children among the extreme poor reflects and reinforces the population growth challenge, that is, high child dependency ratios mean that the same level of economic growth translates into smaller gains in per capita welfare, requiring more rapid aggregate growth merely to keep pace with population expansion (Cruz and Ahmed 2018; World Bank 2016).

Rate of extreme poverty (%) Rate of extreme poverty (%) 2013 2024 *Sources: Global Monitoring Database (version September 2026), internal database, World Bank; Luxembourg Income* Study (May 2026 vintage), LIS Cross-National Data Center, https://www.lisdatacenter.org/our-data/lis-database/. *Note: The figure reports poverty rates for the $3.00-a-person-a-day poverty line (in 2021 purchasing power parity* terms). Educational attainment is strongly associated with poverty incidence. Individuals with no formal education in 2024 faced poverty rates of 22 percent, compared with only 6 percent among individuals with secondary education and 1 percent among individuals with tertiary education (refer to figure 1.13, panel b). Poverty declined by the greatest amount in absolute terms-12 percentage points-between 2013 and 2024 among individuals with no formal education. A Shapley decomposition of this decline reveals that, within economies, the poverty rate among individuals with no formal education fell by 15 percentage points (refer to annex 1B, figure 1B.3).13 This within-economy progress was highly concentrated. India alone accounted for roughly 70 percent of the progress, and five economies-Bangladesh, China, India, Indonesia, and Pakistan-accounted for nearly all the progress. The increase in the concentration of the people with no schooling globally in higher-poverty regions slightly countered this reduction. In Sub-Saharan Africa, the region's rising share of the global population with no schooling more than offset modest within-economy progress. The decline in poverty rates observed among individuals with no formal education would have been larger if not for this global shift toward Sub-Saharan Africa (which exhibits high poverty rates overall) in terms of the share of the global population with no schooling. A similar pattern holds in primary, secondary, and tertiary education. Thus, within-economy declines in poverty rates dominate, but are partly offset by compositional shifts across education levels. The profile of poverty in Sub-Saharan Africa differs markedly from the profiles in other regions. Although poverty rates are substantially higher across all groups in Sub-Saharan Africa relative to the same groups in other regions, children under age 18 living in poor households account for more than half the extreme poor in MENAAP and Sub-Saharan Africa, compared with

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

30 percent to 46 percent in other regions. A decomposition of changing demographic profiles into geographic and within-region components reveals how these regional differences are reshaping the global profile (refer to annex 1B, figure 1B.4). The geographic concentration of poverty in Sub-Saharan Africa, which has a younger population structure relative to other regions, is entirely driving the rising share of children among people living in poor households globally. Within regions, the child share of the poor population has actually declined slightly. Globally, households with children face substantially higher poverty rates than households without children (De la Flor Giuffra et al. 2025), and the relevant trends diverged across different household types in 2013-24 (refer to figure 1.14, panel a). Poverty rates fell across all household types globally, but the pace varied. Declines were most rapid among two-adult households with children (18 percent to 12 percent) and among multiple-adult households with children (22 percent to 14 percent), as well as among households without children, which already had low rates. By contrast, declines were slower among households with one woman adult and children (24 percent to 21 percent). As a result, the gap in poverty rates between these household types and two-adult households with children widened over the decade, underscoring how the absence of a second working-age adult is an increasingly important correlate of poverty. These patterns should be interpreted with caution. Because poverty is measured on a per capita basis, household needs increase one-for-one with the number of household members, ignoring any household economies of scale that arise from resource-sharing within the household. Jolliffe and Tetteh-Baah (2024) show that incorporating household economies of scale substantially changes the identification of who is poor and that the largest effects are in regions where the households are the largest. Trends in Sub-Saharan Africa differ slightly from the global pattern. There, households with children exhibit broadly similar poverty rates across household types, ranging from 46 percent to 50 percent in 2024, and barely changed over the preceding decade (refer to figure 1.14, panel b). The trends were mixed among households without children. Poverty among adultonly households was steady at near 14 percent, whereas rates among senior-only households without children fell from 21 percent to 16 percent. The conflict and fragility dimension reinforces the diverging progress. In non-FCS economies, poverty rates declined substantially between 2013 and 2024 across all household types (refer to figure 1.14, panel c). In FCS economies, by contrast, poverty rates rose among most household types with children (refer to figure 1.14, panel d). The increases were largest among households with one woman adult and children, in which poverty rose from 45 percent to 52 percent, and among multiple-adult households with children, from 43 to 51 percent. Households in which senior adults alone care for children also experienced a substantial rise, from 41 percent to 47 percent, whereas poverty in two-adult households with children was steady at 44 percent. In FCS economies, poverty among senior-only households without children, a category with few households, showed a substantive decline. As a result, household types with children in FCS economies now face poverty rates roughly four to seven times the rates in non-FCS economies.

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

### Subnational disparities reveal persistent pockets of extreme poverty

Although more people are now living in urban areas than in rural areas, poverty remains an overwhelmingly rural phenomenon (refer to figure 1.15). In 2024, 16 percent of rural residents were living in extreme poverty, compared with 5 percent of urban residents, a ratio of more than three to one. Both rates fell between 2013 and 2024, from 25 percent and 7 percent, respectively, but the gap remains wide. As a result, three-quarters of the global poor today reside in rural areas. However, because of increasing urbanization, the share of poor people living in urban areas is expanding globally. In Sub-Saharan Africa, the share rose from 18 percent in 2013 to 26 percent in 2024, and elsewhere, from 22 percent to 28 percent. The urbanization of poverty reflects changes within regions-urbanization is advancing more quickly than the reduction of urban poverty-rather than the geographic shift of poverty toward Sub-Saharan Africa (refer to annex 1B, figure 1B.4, panel b). This rural-urban disparity, however, is only the first layer of spatial concentration. Subnational poverty estimates reveal even sharper disparities within economies and regions (refer to figure 1.16 and annex 1B, figure 1B.5).14 In Sub-Saharan Africa, national averages mask enormous within-economy heterogeneity. In Nigeria, subregional rates range from 8 percent to 80 percent, and, in Kenya, from 5 percent to 85 percent, illustrating how much regions within an economy can differ. In the Democratic Republic of Congo, by contrast, poverty is pervasive. Even the least-poor province has an extreme poverty rate of nearly 50 percent, and, in most provinces, the rate exceeds 90 percent.

The East Asia and Pacific region and the Latin America and Caribbean region also display considerable within-region heterogeneity (refer to annex 1B, figure 1B.5). Although the median poverty rates there are modest (4 percent and 3 percent, respectively), both regions include subregions with rates comparable with those in Sub-Saharan Africa, about 60 percent or higher in parts of Haiti, the Solomon Islands, and Timor-Leste. South Asia exhibits a more compressed distribution. Poverty rates in most subregions are clustered below 10 percent. In Europe and Central Asia and in North America, rates are uniformly low. Medians are below 1 percent, and there are few outliers, except in Central Asia. Subnational data reveal diverging trajectories that mirror global patterns of uneven progress. The Kyrgyz Republic and the Philippines exemplify successful broad-based poverty reduction. In the Kyrgyz Republic, nearly all regions experienced dramatic declines in poverty in 2015-23 (refer to map 1.1, panel a). Rates fell to single digits in regions that had previously shown poverty rates exceeding 40 percent, and several regions nearly eliminated extreme poverty. The Philippines showed a similar pattern. Poverty rates declined throughout the country in

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

2015-23, though pockets of higher poverty persisted, especially in the south (refer to map 1.1, panel b).

Nigeria stands in contrast to these success stories (refer to map 1.1, panel c). Between 2018 and 2022, subnational poverty rates showed little improvement in the country. Though the gap was only four years, poverty rates worsened considerably in many states. The rates continued to climb in 2022 in the north, where poverty already exceeded 50 percent, and several states registered rates above 70 percent or 80 percent. This stagnation illustrates why Nigeria now hosts the largest population of the extreme poor globally. Guatemala also presents a more concerning picture of stagnation and reversal (refer to map 1.1, panel d). Between 2014 and 2023, the extreme poverty rate rose substantially in several departments, and only a handful achieved meaningful reductions. This mixed performance highlights that progress can stall even in middle-income economies.

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

## Poverty extends beyond absolute welfare

### thresholds

Poverty can be described along other dimensions besides poverty thresholds set according to monetary measures of welfare relying on consumption or income. Deprivation can be defined by considering a cutoff determined using basic needs, and it can be defined in relative terms through a comparison among peers. Measurement along nonmonetary dimensions can be useful and revealing. The $3.00 international poverty line measures deprivation with respect to the most basic needs as defined across low-income economies. As an economy's income level increases, the perception of the composition of these basic needs may evolve (Herforth et al. 2020; Jolliffe and Prydz 2016; World Bank 2024a). Measurement might shift to the application of relative poverty lines that establish the poverty threshold not only according to the requirements to satisfy basic needs, but typically relative to the average level of income or consumption. The societal poverty line, a relative measure of poverty, was developed to capture the shifting nature of deprivation across income levels. Following the recommendations of the Atkinson Commission on Global Poverty (World Bank 2017), the societal poverty line varies according to the living standards in an economy. It is set equal to $1.30, plus half the median consumption or income in the economy, with a floor at the $3.00 international poverty line (Foster et al. 2025) (refer to annex 1A). This (weakly) relative approach reflects a recognition that poverty partly involves exclusion from a normal life in a given society. It also allows the poverty line to be specific to an economy. Globally, the societal poverty line varies with average income in a way that approximately corresponds to the way national poverty lines vary with average income across countries (Foster et al. 2025; Jolliffe and Prydz 2021). Regional patterns in societal poverty, as measured by the societal poverty line, mirror the patterns observed in extreme poverty rates. Although the measure is only weakly relative (refer to figure 1.17, panel a), economies with high societal poverty lines tend to exhibit lower societal poverty rates. Sub-Saharan Africa accounts for most economies with high societal poverty rates, consistent with the concentration of extreme poverty in the region (refer to figure 1.17, panel b). Higher-income economies typically exhibit lower societal poverty rates, despite their higher societal poverty lines. These comparisons depend on the level of inequality in an economy and, in particular, the concentration of people at the bottom tail (refer to chapter 2).

### Multidimensional deprivations add depth to the poverty profile

The World Bank's absolute and societal poverty lines rely on the measurement of household monetary welfare through the level of consumption or income. The World Bank Multidimensional Poverty Measure (MPM), by contrast, incorporates measures of nonmonetary deprivation (World Bank 2026e). In addition to extreme poverty, which is measured in monetary terms, the MPM includes deprivations in education and access to basic infrastructure services (Lara Ibarra et al. 2026) (refer to annex 1A). (The components of the MPM are among the indicators discussed in chapters 3 and 4.) The MPM defines a household that is deprived along at least one of these three dimensionsmonetary welfare, education, and access to infrastructure-as multidimensionally poor. Any person identified as poor using the international poverty line is also identified as poor using the MPM. In education, household deprivation is defined according to whether any children up to the age of grade 8 in the household is not enrolled in school or whether no adult in the household has completed primary education. In the case of infrastructure, household

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

deprivation is defined according to whether a household lacks access to one or more of the following: clean drinking water, sanitation, or electricity. To calculate the measure, the MPM requires more data than the monetary poverty measure. Data on the MPM are therefore available only for a smaller sample of economies. If economies with information available for any year after the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic are considered (2020 or more recently), the MPM covered 103 economies as of September 2026. Across these 103 economies, 16.7 percent have been identified as multidimensionally poor, compared with 11.3 percent using the international poverty line of $3.00-a-person-a-day (refer to table 1.1). Extreme poverty rates generally coincide with deprivations in other dimensions (World Bank 2026e) (refer to figure 1.18). There may be wide variations, however, whereby economies associated with high monetary extreme poverty rates nonetheless exhibit low rates of nonmonetary deprivation.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

### The ongoing mission to end extreme poverty

This chapter tracks the progress and setbacks on the path to ending extreme poverty. After falling steadily for three decades, from 43 percent in 1990 to 11 percent in 2020, and then bouncing back after the COVID-19 pandemic, the extreme poverty rate has stalled. In 2026, 811 million people-1 in 10 globally-were living in extreme poverty, defined as living on less than the international poverty line of $3.00-a-person-a-day, expressed in 2021 PPP terms. This rate has remained essentially unchanged over the past six years (refer to figure 1.1). However, extreme poverty has become increasingly concentrated geographically. More than half the extreme poor globally are living in only 9 economies, and three-quarters in 20 economies. Of the nine economies, six are in Sub-Saharan Africa, where the extreme poverty rate has remained near 44 percent since 2014. Only in 2026 was the extreme poverty rate for Sub- Saharan Africa projected to return to its prepandemic level, while the rate for MENAAP remained above its prepandemic level. Fragility and conflict are additional key defining features of global poverty, driven by two factors: more high-poverty economies became fragile, and extreme poverty fell more quickly in non-FCS economies. The global poor are disproportionately young, rural, and less educated. Although the extreme poverty rate declined across most demographic groups between 2013 and 2023, progress was slowest among households caring for children, but without two working-age adults. The MPM reinforces the awareness of the severity of these patterns and suggests that the challenges are even more daunting. Across 103 economies, 16.7 percent of people are multidimensionally poor. Delivering more rapid and more inclusive economic growth is imperative to bringing poverty reduction back on track. This growth is most needed in contexts in which peace and stability are lacking, making growth more difficult to achieve. Rapid population growth presents an additional challenge in high-poverty economies, particularly if economic growth has been insufficient to generate sustained gains in income per capita.

## Annex 1A. Data and methodology

### Poverty and Inequality Platform data and the methodology of global poverty measurement

The data used to measure poverty in this report are taken from the World Bank's Poverty and Inequality Platform (PIP), which aggregates welfare data from household surveys. The platform includes data from more than 2,500 household surveys in 172 economies representing

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

97 percent of the world's population. Statistics from most of the surveys are included in the World Bank Global Monitoring Database, which is described below. This report relies on 2,588 survey-years of data, or an additional 204 surveys relative to the data available for the Poverty, *Prosperity, and Planet Report 2024 (World Bank 2024a). This increase in the coverage of survey* data has made reporting possible on global poverty up to 2024, a two-year lag from 2026, the publication year of this report. The nowcasts presented for 2025 and 2026 require additional assumptions, as explained below. The report measures welfare using either household consumption or income data depending on which type of data is available for each economy. Slightly more than half the economies on which the platform reports rely on consumption data. Economies in Latin America and the Caribbean and the high-income economies account for the majority of the economies that rely on household income data. An extensive literature explores and compares consumption and income measures in household surveys and the respective benefits and limitations (World Bank 2016). Survey data are converted to internationally comparable welfare measures using 2021 PPP dollars (refer to box 1.1). As of the June 2025 update, PIP uses the 2021 PPPs, replacing the 2017 PPPs (Alfani et al. 2025; Foster et al. 2025). The global poverty lines have been updated accordingly. The international (extreme) poverty line is $3.00-a-person-a-day. The $4.20- and $8.30-a-person-a-day lines reflect the median national poverty lines of lower-middle-income and upper-middle-income countries, respectively (Foster et al. 2025). Welfare values of less than $0.28-a-person-a-day (in 2021 PPP terms) have been replaced with $0.28 because of the significant measurement errors at such low consumption or income levels (Yonzan et al. 2026). For economies on which no survey data are available for a particular year, PIP includes estimates of poverty rates on the basis of the most recent available survey data. If survey data are available through the platform only prior to a particular reference year, the most recent welfare distribution is extrapolated forward using growth rates from national accounts, either real GDP per capita or real household final consumption expenditure per capita. Because of its close conceptual relation with the welfare aggregate, the latter, if available, is generally the preferred national accounts variable in PIP. However, for lowand lower-middle-income economies, GDP per capita is preferred because household final consumption expenditure per capita is often calculated as the residual component of GDP in the case of economies with low statistical capacity (Mahler and Newhouse 2024). Not all economic growth feeds into growth in household income. Pass-through rates between national accounts data and household welfare are assumed in PIP based on estimations of Mahler et al. (2022) of 0.7 if the last household survey in an economy uses a consumption aggregate and 1.0 if the last survey uses an income aggregate (Mahler and Newhouse 2024). The extrapolation is distribution neutral, that is, no change in inequality is assumed.

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 For the years between two surveys, poverty is interpolated in PIP on the basis of the data from the two surveys using data from national accounts, while assuming the same pass-through rates. The World Bank (2026a) provides more technical details on the extrapolation and interpolation methods. Although PIP includes data from a large number of surveys, poverty cannot be estimated directly in the case of several economies (less than 3 percent of the global population) because of the lack of survey data, unreliable purchasing power parities, missing national accounts data, and so on. For these economies, an imputation approach developed by Mahler et al. (2026) is applied. The approach relies on a small set of variables on which data are widely available-GDP per capita, under-5 mortality rates, life expectancy, rural population share, income group, and region-to predict full welfare distributions. The model used in this case has two tiers. The choice of the tier to be used in the case of a particular economy depends on whether data on GDP per capita are available. The distributions predicted by the model are used on the platform solely to include data on the economy in regional and global aggregates. The resulting poverty rates are not reported individually on the platform's website because of the large possible errors involved in any imputation model. With the September 2025 update to the platform, this approach replaced the previous practice of assigning any economy on which data are missing the population-weighted average poverty rate for the region in which the economy is located. Regional poverty estimates for a particular reference year are presented in PIP only if survey data are available for that year on at least 50 percent of the regional population. A global poverty estimate for a particular reference year is reported in PIP only if survey data are available that cover at least 50 percent of the world's population and at least 50 percent of the population of low- and lower-middle-income economies in that year. An economy is considered covered (that is, relevant data are available) in a particular reference year if a nationally representative survey has been conducted within three years on either side of the reference year. As an exception, however, surveys conducted before the COVID-19 pandemic are not counted in PIP for coverage in 2020 or later. Likewise, surveys conducted after the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic are not counted for coverage in 2020 or earlier. Alongside the inclusion of new survey data on India, the survey source used for India in recent years has also changed relative to the previous edition of the report. Estimates previously based on data from the Consumer Pyramids Household Survey, conducted by a private survey firm, which covered the years 2015 to 2021, have been removed from PIP because of concerns regarding comparability with data from the newly incorporated Household Consumption Expenditure Survey released by the government of India. As a result, survey data on India are no longer available on the platform for 2015-21. This means that the coverage of South Asia in 2015-19 and globally for the reference year 2019 has been lost.

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

The societal poverty line is defined for an economy as max ($3.00, $1.30 + 0.5 × median), which means that it is the greater of either $3.00-a-person-a-day (that is, the international poverty line) or $1.30, plus half the median consumption or income of the economy (depending on the type of data used in the economy to calculate welfare measures). For economies with sufficiently high median consumption or income, this is a relative poverty measure, reflecting the fact that national poverty lines rise as economies grow. The societal poverty line approximately reflects how national poverty lines vary with average income around the world (Foster et al. 2025; Jolliffe and Prydz 2021). The Multidimensional Poverty Measure indicates the share of a population that is defined as multidimensionally poor based on the three separate dimensions of poverty it encompasses. Each of the three dimensions of poverty covered weighs equally (one-third) in the final measure, and each indicator within a dimension of poverty weighs equally within that dimension. Monetary poverty, based on one indicator (the $3.00-a-person-a-day international poverty line), accounts for one-third of the total score. Education poverty also contributes one-third. Each of the two education indicators (attainment and enrollment) account for onesixth of the total score. Basic infrastructure services contribute the final third. Each of the three associated infrastructure indicators (electricity, sanitation, and water) account for oneninth of the total score. A household is identified as multidimensionally poor if it is deprived in indicators whose weights sum to one-third or more of the total possible score across all dimensions.

### Projection methods

Sufficient survey data are currently available in the Poverty and Inequality Platform to support data coverage through 2024. The report uses projections to generate poverty numbers for 2025 and beyond. In this report, nowcasting refers to the method used to generate data for 2025 and 2026, and forecasting refers to the corresponding method for subsequent years, that is, the future at the time of publication of the data. Poverty is nowcast and forecast using growth rates of GDP per capita, computed using methods similar to those employed in earlier years in cases in which no survey data were available for a particular economy. Projections based on per capita GDP growth predict poverty nearly as well as projections using a combination of 1,000 economy-level development outcome indicators (Mahler et al. 2022). Projecting poverty rates on the basis of GDP per capita growth rates involves making two key assumptions. First, as in interpolation, distribution neutrality is assumed, meaning that all households in an economy benefit equally from economic growth in the economy. Each household's welfare is scaled by the GDP per capita growth rate, and, so, there is no change in inequality. Second, the report applies the same pass-through rates to the growth rate of an economy's GDP per capita as in the imputation of poverty rates in the previous

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 subsection: 0.7 if the most recent survey available for the economy has consumption data, and 1.0 if it has income data. The report applies the methodology used to nowcast and forecast consistently across all economies to ensure comparability; so, economy-specific adjustments are not made. Alternative nowcasts using a range of methods with assumptions specific to each economy are available in the World Bank's Macro Poverty Outlook (World Bank 2025c). To analyze the potential impact of the conflict in the Middle East, the report also includes estimates that relax the assumption of distribution neutrality (refer to box 1.2). In particular, sectoral growth rates and differential spending patterns are used to model household-specific income and price shocks. Kimura et al. (2026) provide more details. To extend the series through 2030, growth rates for GDP per capita are taken from Global *Economic Prospects, June 2026 (World Bank 2026c), supplemented with the April 2026 Macro* *Poverty Outlook (World Bank 2026d) or the April 2026 World Economic Outlook (IMF 2026).* If growth data are unavailable, average annual historical GDP per capita growth rates (over 2015-24) presented in the World Development Indicators of the World Bank are used.

### The Global Monitoring Database

The Global Monitoring Database is the World Bank repository of nationally representative household surveys conducted by national statistical offices. These surveys all include information on welfare (whether household income or consumption) and household size (to adjust to per capita levels). The database is the main source of data for the poverty and inequality estimates published in PIP. The database also includes other harmonized variables, such as the age, sex, and education levels of members, the rural or urban location of households, and household access to basic infrastructure services (for example, electricity and improved sanitation). Because survey instruments differ across economies, data are harmonized to the degree possible. Researchers can access the estimates through PIP, and they can access microdata through PIP's Statistics Online tool as long as agreements permit data sharing.15 Both the database and platform are updated periodically. This report uses the latest version of data available (September 2026).

### The Global Subnational Poverty Atlas

The World Bank's Global Subnational Poverty Atlas combines household survey data in the Global Monitoring Database and data on administrative boundaries to provide visualizations of extreme poverty at the subnational level. The Atlas includes custom shapefiles that can be linked to the subnational geographic units in the database. These shapefiles follow a harmonized spatial hierarchy across economies consisting of four administrative levels, from the whole economy to district and finer units.

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

### Annex 1D Measuring the geographic

### .

### concentration of global poverty

To measure the extent to which global poverty is concentrated in a small number of economies disproportionate to their population, this annex applies the dissimilarity index (Duncan and Duncan 1955), a standard measure of spatial concentration. The index compares the distribution of the extremely poor population across economies with the distribution of the total population. It is computed as follows:

*D* 1 ∑ i *p n*

= - *i* , (1D.1)

2 *i P N* where pi is the number of the extreme poor in economy i; P is the total population of the extreme poor globally; ni is the total population of economy i; and N is the total global population. The index ranges from 0 (the poor in a particular economy are distributed exactly in proportion to the population across all economies) to 1 (all poor people in the world are in one economy). The dissimilarity index measures the share of the global poor population who would need to relocate across economies to equalize the distribution of poverty to reflect the share of a economy's population to the global population. The index rose from 0.31 in 2000 to 0.33 in 2010 and 0.65 in 2024. The geographic concentration of poverty, not explained by population size, thus more than doubled over the quarter century between 2000 and 2024. Figure 1D.1 illustrates this shift. It ranks countries from lowest to highest according to poverty rates, plotting the cumulative share of the global population against the cumulative share of the global poor population. The farther the curve bows below the 45-degree diagonal, the more concentrated poverty is in fewer economies, independent of the population size of those economies. In 2000, the curve already bowed below the diagonal, but, by 2024, the concentration became extreme: The 2024 curve traces the horizontal axis for much of its length. This reflects the fact that 30 percent of the global population in that year were living in economies with effectively zero extreme poverty, up from 9 percent in 2000. The 2024 curve then rises steeply at the far right. Economies with the highest extreme poverty rates and a population share of 20 percent in the world accounted for roughly 37 percent of the extreme poor in 2000. By 2024, economies with the highest extreme poverty rates and 20 percent of the global population accounted for roughly 85 percent of the extreme poor.

## Notes

1. Poverty is measured using household consumption or income per capita from household surveys, depending on the type of data that are available for each economy. Refer to annex 1A for details.
2. Figure 1.4 reports regional aggregates drawn from the Poverty and Inequality Platform (PIP). When no survey is available for a country in a particular year, PIP interpolates and extrapolates the existing surveys (refer to annex 1A for further details). PIP uses a method that is common across countries and uses national accounts growth adjusted by a pass-through rate. The World Bank publishes an alternative set of poverty nowcasts through the Macro Poverty Outlooks (MPOs). The MPOs are the preferred estimates for country-specific analyses, since they follow country-specific methodologies. As a result of the distinct projection methods, the nowcasts can differ between PIP and the MPOs. For example, in the case of Nigeria, the MPO uses a Nigeria-specific microsimulation that projects nominal consumption by households' sectoral income sources and applies household-specific inflation based on purchased food, nonfood, and own-produced food. It therefore captures uneven sectoral growth and inflation-driven losses in purchasing power, resulting in a different poverty trajectory.
3. As of the end of fiscal year 2026, the 26 economies classified as low-income are, in Sub-Saharan Africa, Burkina Faso, Burundi, the Central African Republic, Chad, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Eritrea, Ethiopia, The Gambia, Guinea-Bissau, Liberia, Madagascar, Malawi, Mali, Mozambique, Niger, Rwanda, Sierra Leone, the Federal Republic of Somalia, South Sudan, Sudan, Togo, and Uganda; in MENAAP, Afghanistan, the Syrian Arab Republic, and the Republic of Yemen; and the Democratic People's Republic of Korea. Note that 22 of the 26 are in Sub-Saharan Africa.

-----

Global Poverty: Stalled Prog ress and Rising Concentration

4. In Europe and Central Asia, the pronounced growth at the lower percentiles in 2019-25 was broadbased across the region, whereas Central Asian economies also experienced marked growth at upper percentiles.
5. The 9 economies with the most poor (poverty percentages at the $3.00-a-person-a-day line in 2024 in parentheses) are the Democratic Republic of Congo (82 percent), Ethiopia (32 percent), India (2 percent), Mozambique (81 percent), Nigeria (41 percent), Pakistan (22 percent), Tanzania (47 percent), Uganda (53 percent), and the Republic of Yemen (72 percent). The other 11 making up the top 20 are Afghanistan (47 percent), Angola (42 percent), Indonesia (5 percent), Kenya (43 percent), Madagascar (68 percent), Malawi (76 percent), Niger (55 percent), the Philippines (10 percent), the Federal Republic of Somalia (63 percent), Sudan (41 percent), and Zambia (71 percent). The estimates reported in this footnote are the poverty rates that underlie the global and regional aggregates. These have been extrapolated or interpolated to a common year if a survey is unavailable in 2024 (refer to annex 1A).
6. Of the 26 economies classified as low income in fiscal year 2026, only 6 are projected to transition to middle-income status by 2050 (World Bank 2025a).
7. Earlier versions of this report have also included scenarios that modeled reductions in inequality (for instance, refer to World Bank 2024a, figure 1.3). Lakner et al. (2022) model the complementary effects of growth and changes in inequality on poverty.
8. There is a complex bidirectional relationship between poverty and conflict (Fuseini et al 2025). For a more detailed discussion of the topic, refer also to Corral et al. (2020).
9. For more discussion of the evidence on the slow or, sometimes, stalled fertility transition in Sub-Saharan Africa in particular, refer to Büttner et al (2024).
10. Thus, for example, Nigeria was classified as FCS until the 2008 fiscal year and again from 2020 onward, and Ethiopia became classified as FCS in fiscal year 2022.
11. All members of a given household therefore have the same poverty status. Differences in poverty rates across men and women will thus necessarily reflect household composition patterns related to the extent to which women (men) reside in households with no men (women). Research has shown that differences in poverty rates between men and women are sensitive to the life cycle and to household composition patterns, especially among adults ages 25-34 (refer to Muñoz-Boudet et al. 2021). In this report, poverty rates by age and by household composition are reported, but not poverty rates among men and women. This might be misleading given the strong assumption of the equal sharing of resources within households.
12. The analysis captures changes over the last decade. This is too short of a period to pick up population aging effects, although the direction of the observed changes is consistent: The share of children falls, while the share of adults (25+) increases.
13. A Shapley decomposition can be used to break down the total change in the global poverty rate into two components: changes in poverty rates within economies and changes in the distribution of the population across economies and regions. The contributions of the two components sum to the total observed change.
14. The World Bank's Global Subnational Poverty Atlas provides poverty estimates for 1,900 subregions across more than 170 economies based on representative subregions from official household surveys. In many countries, these subregions correspond to the province or state level, that is the first or second government administrative levels. Refer to Global Subnational Poverty Atlas (version April 2026), World Bank, https://datacatalog.worldbank.org/search/dataset/0042041 [/global-subnational-poverty-atlas-gsap.](https://datacatalog.worldbank.org/search/dataset/0042041/global-subnational-poverty-atlas-gsap)
15. Refer to SOL (Statistics On-Line), World Bank, https://pip.worldbank.org/sol/sol-landing.

# 2

## The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bumpy and Uneven

## Summary

- *The shared prosperity measures used in this chapter focus on the distribution of welfare in an* *economy (as captured by the Gini index) and on the average gap between the welfare of individuals* *and a global prosperity standard of $28.00-a-person-a-day (the Global Prosperity Gap).*
- *The number of economies classified as highly unequal (Gini above 40) has fallen substantially* *since 2000, from 70 to 42 economies. Even the economies that remained above the high-* *inequality threshold throughout 2000-24 narrowed inequality. Their average Gini index fell* *from about 51 to about 46.*
- *Since 2013, at least one in five people worldwide have been living in economies characterized by* *high inequality. The number jumps to nearly one in three people in the world without the large* *populations of China and India.*
- *The overlap between high inequality and poverty has deepened. Between 2000 and 2024, the* *share of the world's extreme poor-defined as the people living below the $3.00-a-day poverty* *line measured in 2021 purchasing power parity (PPP) terms-who are living in high-inequality* *economies nearly doubled. In 2000, slightly more of the extreme poor were living in low-inequality* *economies than in high-inequality economies. By 2024, more than six of the extreme poor were* *living in a high-inequality economy for every one person living in a low-inequality economy.*
- *The Global Prosperity Gap narrowed by more than half between 1990 (when it was 10.5) and* *2015. Incomes around the world in 2015 would have had to increase by fivefold, on average, to* *reach the standard of$28.00-a-person-a-day.*

A reproducibility package is available for this book in the Reproducible Research Repository at

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

- *Progress has slowed in the most recent decade. The Global Prosperity Gap fell only slightly, to* *4.5, between 2015 and 2026. Incomes would still have had to increase by a factor of4.5, on* *average, if everyone in the world were to reach the threshold of$28.00-a-person-a-day.*
- *Progress in narrowing the Global Prosperity Gap has been uneven across regions. East Asia and* *Pacific made the largest gains, reducing the average gap by more than 80 percent between 1990* *and 2026. In other regions, the gap has been far more stagnant. The Middle East, North Africa,* *Afghanistan, and Pakistan (MENAAP) region exhibited little improvement after 2015 when* *the gap stood at around 5, while Sub-Saharan Africa remained farthest from the prosperity* *standard in 2026, with a gap of11.1, more than twice the global average in 2026 and about the* *same level as the Global Prosperity Gap in 1980.*

## Inequality matters for development

The World Bank has a dual mission: to end extreme poverty and to boost shared prosperity on a livable planet. The objectives are closely intertwined, but also possess intrinsic value each on their own. The importance of shared prosperity in development is thus the subject of several World Bank publications (World Bank 2005, 2016, 2022, 2024). To monitor the progress in shared prosperity, the World Bank uses two indicators: the Global Prosperity Gap and the number of economies exhibiting high inequality. The Global Prosperity Gap is a distribution-sensitive measure that captures the average global shortfall with respect to a global prosperity standard of $28.00-a-person-a-day. The measure captures both average welfare (measured as household consumption or income, depending on the survey source) and the distribution of welfare, placing greater weight on poorer individuals. The number of economies exhibiting high inequality is measured by using a threshold of 40 for the Gini index. This means an economy with a Gini of above 40 is considered a high-inequality economy. The number directs the focus toward the distribution of welfare within economies. The case for monitoring inequality has deepened over the past three decades, shaped by three shifts in economic thinking. Early thinking often treated inequality as a temporary by-product of development, but later work showed that high inequality can become self-reinforcing and can slow progress in reducing poverty. If poor households cannot invest in education or productive opportunities, inequality can trap economies in low-human-capital, low-growth equilibriums. More broadly, growth tends to reduce poverty less if inequality is greater, meaning that poverty reduction and inequality reduction are closely linked rather than separate policy agendas. The first major shift challenged the idea that inequality naturally rises and then falls because of development. Kuznets (1955) proposes the well-known inverted-U, suggesting that inequality would increase as economies industrialize before declining as they mature. For decades, this framing implied that inequality would be transitional and likely to self-correct over time.

-----

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

Subsequent work has cast doubt on this view. Galor and Zeira (1993) show how inequality can persist if credit constraints prevent poor households from investing in education, leading to underinvestment in human capital and low-growth traps. Bourguignon (2003) clarifies that poverty reduction depends on both mean income growth and the distribution of income. So, growth reduces poverty less if inequality is greater, a relationship formalized through the decomposition of changes in poverty into growth and redistribution components first proposed by Datt and Ravallion (1992). In practical terms, poverty reduction and inequality reduction are closely linked. Cross-country evidence supports this finding. Economies exhibiting greater initial inequality tend to reduce poverty less at the same rate of growth in gross domestic product (Fosu 2009, 2017). More recent calibrations suggest the existence of a double dividend produced by lower inequality. Lower inequality reduces poverty directly and also increases the poverty-reducing effect of growth (Bergstrom 2022). The second major shift relocated the analysis from the country to the global level. Inequality may be reframed by treating the world as a single distribution of individuals rather than a collection of national averages (Milanović 2016). This approach is able to probe the uneven development path globally in terms of household welfare, thereby highlighting the large gains for much of emerging Asia, the weaker gains for parts of the lower-middle and middle classes in richer economies, and the large gains at the top of the global distribution (Lakner and Milanović 2016). Related work shows that wealth can become more highly concentrated if the returns to capital outpace economic growth (Piketty 2014). This perspective also broadens what should be measured. Focusing only on the extreme poor or only on the bottom 40 percent of the income distribution can generate analyses that miss both the hollowing out of the middle and the concentration of gains at the top. The third major shift was the recognition that inequality has consequences beyond income. Acemoglu and Robinson (2008) show how economic inequality and political power can reinforce one another through institutions, while Stiglitz (2012) links tighter concentration to weaker aggregate demand and greater financial fragility. Corak (2013) documents the Great Gatsby curve, showing that higher inequality tends to go hand in hand with lower intergenerational mobility. The policy question is therefore not whether inequalities arise, but whether they are managed in ways that allow more people and places to benefit from growth over time. If they are not so managed, Hirschman and Rothschild's (1973) tunnel effect suggests that an initial tolerance of inequality can turn into frustration and resentment, undermining the social foundations of development. This is the logic behind a progrowth equity agenda that corrects distortions in access to education, credit, and productive opportunities so that structural transformation becomes more inclusive (López-Calva and Rodríguez-Castelán 2016). This chapter tracks two complementary dimensions of shared prosperity. The first dimension is high inequality, defined as a Gini index greater than 40 in an economy. The Gini index is one of the most familiar measures of within-economy inequality. The second dimension is

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 the Global Prosperity Gap, which captures the distance separating people from a globally defined standard of secure and resilient living. Understanding the source of deprivation and of unequal opportunities is a crucial first step toward addressing such issues in the pursuit of shared prosperity.

### High inequality still fects at least one in five people worldwide

### Inequality is high in 42 economies

Based on the most recently available household survey for the 169 economies for which data are available, the Gini index in 42 economies worldwide as of 2024 was greater than 40, the World Bank's threshold for high inequality (Haddad et al. 2024) (refer to boxes 2.1 and 2.2).1 Of the remaining 127 economies, 94 were classified at moderate inequality (a Gini index above 30 and up to 40), and 33 economies were classified at low inequality (a Gini index of 30 or less).

## BOX 2.1

###### The Gini is the basis of the World Bank measure of high inequality

The World Bank tracks the number of economies with a Gini index greater than 40 as one of two indicators of shared prosperity, thereby providing a simple and widely comparable measure of the number of economies with high inequality (refer to annex 2A). The Gini index summarizes the distribution of welfare within an economy on a scale from 0 (a society in which everyone has the same income or welfare, that is, a society with perfect equality) to 100 (a society in which one person has all the income, that is, a society with perfect inequality). The Gini is one of the most familiar measures of inequality. It has a long history of use and recognition among the widest audience, and it satisfies standard axioms for inequality measures (Haddad et al. 2024). Most alternative inequality measures are also highly correlated with the Gini index, meaning that conclusions drawn from the Gini are unlikely to differ substantially from findings based on other commonly used measures (Haddad et al. 2024). There are no universally recognized standards for defining high levels of inequality. In the absence of predefined standards, the World Bank has chosen a Gini index of 40 as the threshold of high inequality. An economy with a Gini

*(continued)*

-----

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

###### BOX 2.1

###### The Gini is the basis of the World Bank measure of high inequality (continued)

above 40 is thus regarded as a high-inequality economy. This threshold was selected on the basis of several considerations, as follows. United Nations reports have long used this value to define high inequality; it separates approximately the top third of all Gini indexes observed across economies globally in 2000-22 from the bottom two-thirds; and expert consultations within the World Bank in 2024 found that the median expert regarded a Gini index greater than 40 to be an indicator of high inequality if consumptionbased measures of inequality were used (Haddad et al. 2024). The Gini index is calculated from household survey data. Such data are associated with several well-documented measurement limitations (refer to annex 2B). Four limitations matter most for purposes of this chapter. First, the Gini index is based on either consumption or income depending on the type of survey in each economy. Consumption-based values are systematically lower. Given that economies in Latin America and the Caribbean rely on income-based measures, while economies in Sub- Saharan Africa predominantly use consumption-based measures, part of the measured gap between the two regions reflects the welfare concept rather than the underlying distribution. Adjusting all economies to an income basis would lead to a classification of more economies as highly unequal. Most of these economies would be in Sub-Saharan Africa. This would mean that Sub-Saharan Africa is more unequal, on average, than Latin America and the Caribbean, which would reverse what unadjusted survey data suggest (Deudibe et al. 2026) (refer to annex 2B). Second, surveys underestimate the incomes at the top of the distribution. So, the true level of inequality is likely higher than the measurements indicate, and part of the observed decline could reflect growing difficulty in capturing top incomes as economies develop. Third, trends in income or consumption inequality may not align with trends in wealth inequality, which has risen in most economies since the 1990s even in cases in which income inequality has declined (Disslbacher et al. 2026). Fourth, welfare is measured at the household level, which assumes that income or consumption is shared equally among household members. Evidence on allocation by sex and age indicates that this is not so (Doss 1996; World Bank 2018). Measured inequality therefore understates inequality across individuals, and the understatement is not uniform across economies.

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 Map 2.1 reveals a striking geographic concentration. High-inequality economies are clustered predominantly in two regions: Latin America and the Caribbean and Sub-Saharan Africa. In contrast, the Europe and Central Asia region and South Asia exhibit predominantly low or moderate inequality. This regional pattern has been resistant to change over the past two and a half decades. There are important methodological caveats in making this global comparison (refer to box 2.1 and annex 2B). In particular, the data combine Gini indexes measured in terms of income and consumption, which is a function of the survey practice across economies. Inequality in income tends to be greater than inequality in consumption, and this may affect relative economy rankings.

## BOX 2.2

###### Tracking high inequality beyond the index threshold of 40

Identifying economies with values above a threshold on a particular measure offers simple, compelling messaging. The meaning of the sentence, 42 economies remain highly unequal, is more readily understandable than the sentence, the global unweighted mean Gini index is 37. But a threshold-based metric has limitations. First, it can obscure progress. A Gini index that has been reduced from 55 to 45 demonstrates that substantial progress has been achieved in reducing inequality even though the country might still be classified as highly unequal. Second, it can amplify the noise near the threshold. Small measurement changes or survey comparability issues can cause economies to switch classifications without any meaningful underlying change. To tell a more complete story, this chapter uses two complementary approaches. First, the chapter reports on threshold-based counts, such as the number of economies for which the Gini indexes are above 40 and the share of the global population living in high-inequality economies. Second, the chapter examines the level of the Gini index itself. Figure B2.2.1 shows that the decline in the share of economies exhibiting high inequality is robust to small adjustments around a Gini index of 40. The figure divides economies into four groups: those with Gini indexes of at least 38 and no greater than 40, those with values above 40 and no greater than 42, those with values greater than 42, and those with values less than 38. (Note that 38 and 42 represent plus or minus 5 percent of 40.) The share of economies with Gini indexes greater than 42 fell from 34 percent in 2000 to 19 percent in 2024, and the share with Gini indexes less than 38 rose from 49 percent to 63 percent.

### At least one in five people are living in economies classified as highly unequal

Counting economies tells only part of the story. A focus on people rather than economies reveals that, in 2024, one in five people globally, approximately 1.6 billion people, were living in economies in the high-inequality classification (refer to figure 2.1). Because the indicator classifies economies rather than people, the global population share is sensitive to the classification of populous economies. Specifically, China and India have large populations, but are not classified as highly unequal economies. If China is excluded from the calculation, the share of people living in high-inequality economies in the remainder of the world increases to about one in four, and, if India is also excluded, nearly one in three people in the remainder of the world were living in such economies in 2024.2

The latest* available survey (after 2000) is used for each economy.

### The number of high-inequality economies has fallen by more than one-third since 2000

The status of inequality today reflects the accumulation of decades of movement of economies in and out of the high-inequality classification. The number of high-inequality economies has fallen substantially over the past two and a half decades (refer to figure 2.2). In 2000, the Gini index was above 40 in 70 economies worldwide. The count rose in the early 2000s, peaking at 76 in 2004, before declining steadily thereafter. By 2024, the count had declined to 42, a reduction of more than one-third from the number in 2000. While the number of high-inequality economies fell by more than a third in 2000-24, the share of the world's population living in such economies has remained near 20 percent since 2013 (refer to figure 2.2). The steep decline from roughly 50 percent in the early 2000s to 25 percent by 2013 (and, similarly, the steep increase in 2002) was driven almost entirely by China. The shift of China to below (above) the threshold moved more than a billion people out of (into) the high-inequality population count in a single step.

If no survey is* available for a particular economy in a given year, data are used from the most recent survey in that economy prior to the given year. To ensure a balanced sample, data from the earliest survey in an economy are backcasted if the first available survey is after 2000.

The decline in the number of high-inequality economies is measured using the welfare concept available in each economy, that is, by combining consumption-based and incomebased Gini indexes. Such differences in welfare concepts affect measured inequality and thus the number of economies that are classified as highly unequal, particularly because consumption-based measures typically yield lower Gini index values relative to incomebased measures. However, economies generally rely on a consistent welfare concept over time. So, the observed decline is not driven by systematic swapping between income- and consumption-based measurement. Deudibe et al. (2026) show that expressing consumption distributions on a comparable income basis would raise measured inequality and increase the number of economies classified as highly unequal, particularly in Sub-Saharan Africa (refer to annex 2B).

### Average inequality has declined since the mid-2000s, whether economies or people are counted

Beyond the fall in the number of high-inequality economies, the average Gini index also improved (refer to box 2.2). There are at least two ways to view the Gini index across economies: the simple average across economies without taking population size into account (the simple average) or an average accounting for population size (the population-adjusted average). In either case, the average Gini index has declined substantially since the mid-2000s (refer to figure 2.3).

This is consistent with the trend in the number of economies and the number of people living in high-inequality economies. The simple average Gini index was relatively stable at between 39 and 40 from 2000 through 2005, then declined to about 36 by 2024. The population-adjusted average Gini index shows a slightly different pattern. It was stable at between 37 and 38 from 2000 to 2013, then fell to about 34 by 2024. This reflects the influence of large, populous economies in the global distribution. During the earlier period, rising inequality in economies, such as China, offset declines elsewhere, while, during the later period, declining inequality in the same economies (such as China) contributed to a fall in the population-adjusted average. The gap between the simple and population-adjusted average Gini indexes narrowed from 2000 until roughly the end of the decade. The gap between the two means remained relatively stable for much of the 2010s until approximately the start of the COVID-19 pandemic. The simple average Gini index remained consistently above the population-adjusted average because small high-inequality economies receive the same weight as large economies in the simple average, but exert little weight in the population-adjusted series. The early narrowing suggests that some reduction occurred in the influence of smaller high-inequality economies, while the generally parallel movement thereafter indicates that inequality was not skewed by country population.

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

### The share of the global extreme poor living in high-inequality economies nearly doubled in 2000-24

The World Bank goals of ending extreme poverty and boosting shared prosperity are intertwined. The global poor are increasingly concentrated in highly unequal economies. Between 2000 and 2024, the share of the extreme poor-that is, people living on less than $3.00 a day-in high-inequality economies nearly doubled, from about a fifth to about two-fifths (refer to figure 2.4). Over the same period, the share of the poor in low-inequality economies fell from roughly a quarter to under 6 percent, while the share in moderate-inequality economies remained stable at roughly more than half the global poor population. The shift reflects two dynamic factors: the movement of some economies out of the highinequality category and slower poverty reduction in the economies that remained in it. First, in several economies, rapid poverty reduction was accompanied by declining inequality, and the economies were no longer in the high-inequality category. China is the clearest example. The Gini index in China fell below 40 in 2013, and this transition coincided with an exceptionally large reduction in poverty. This meant that the number of poor people living in high-inequality economies overall declined sharply (World Bank and DRC 2022). Second, many economies

that remained above the high-inequality threshold throughout the period (concentrated in Sub-Saharan Africa) showed much slower poverty reduction or stagnant poverty rates (refer to chapter 1). Together, these trends mean that extreme poverty has become increasingly concentrated in economies in which inequality is high, even as the global population share of the extreme poor has shrunk. In 2000, the number of the extreme poor living in high-inequality economies was roughly matched by the number living in low-inequality economies. By 2024, more than six of the extreme poor were living in a high-inequality economy for every one person living in a lowinequality economy. The pattern is consistent with the more rapid progress in poverty reduction in economies with lower measured inequality, while also reflecting the passage of economies across the high-inequality threshold.

### The stickiness: Once inequality is high, it remains high for decades

In many economies, high inequality is not transitory, but chronic. The spell length of high inequality, that is, the duration of the high-inequality status of a particular economy, is defined here as the longest run of consecutive years within the 2000-24 window in which the Gini index for the economy was above 40. The distribution of spell lengths across the sample
