### Silver lining: Progress among persistently high-inequality economies

The stickiness in 2000-24 does not mean there was no progress among the persistently highinequality group. Among the 37 economies that remained above the high-inequality threshold throughout 2000-24, the average Gini index (not accounting for population size) fell from approximately 51 to approximately 46, a reduction of 5 Gini points (refer to figure 2.6).4 The decline in the average Gini was broad-based: The average Gini value among economies that were more unequal than 75 percent of the high-inequality economies fell from about

56 to about 50, and the average value among economies that were more unequal than only 25 percent of these economies fell from roughly 47 to roughly 43. By 2024, the Gini value in an economy at the 25th percentile was only 3 points above the threshold of 40, indicating that the lower end of the persistently high-inequality group had moved much closer to the exit point of the high-inequality category in the intervening years since 2000. This is consistent with the ongoing decline in the overall number of high-inequality economies, which fell from 62 in 2015 to 52 in 2020 and then to 42 in 2024. Even many economies that remain classified as highly unequal have thus continued to make gradual progress toward lower inequality. This progress represents an important caveat in any consideration of the count of highinequality economies, which is a headline indicator that tracks shared prosperity. An economy with a Gini index that declines from 55 to 45 has made substantial progress in reducing inequality, but contributes identically to the headline count as an economy that has stagnated at a Gini index of 55 (refer to box 2.2). Taken together, figures 2.5 and 2.6 tell a more complete story than the count alone. The global stock of high-inequality economies is shrinking slowly, and the economies that remain high in inequality are also making real progress toward exiting high-inequality status, even if the progress is not yet sufficient to exit (Alvaredo et al. 2026).

## The Global Prosperity Gap has been

### closing but remains wide

The Gini index is an indicator of the distribution of welfare within a population. However, it does not offer insights into the overall level of welfare among the population. Meanwhile, extreme poverty measures the extent to which a population is deprived of basic needs (refer to chapter 1), but yields no information on whether people are living only slightly above a specific poverty threshold or are experiencing a comfortable level of welfare. In 2024, for instance, Mexico and the United States had similar Gini indexes: 42.6 and 41.8, respectively. Extreme poverty had also been all but eradicated in both economies, at 1.7 percent in Mexico and 1.1 percent in the United States. Nonetheless, despite the similarities in the scores across the two metrics, the average level of welfare among the populations of the two economies differed substantially. In 2024, the average daily income per person amounted to $96.00 in the United States, almost five times the $20.00 average daily income per person in Mexico (both measured in 2021 PPP terms). The Prosperity Gap combines information on the level and on the distribution of welfare. It offers insights into the depth of vulnerability in a population and the status of households within the same population with respect to secure, resilient living standards. It accomplishes this by measuring the amount of income required among a population to reach an average, globally defined level of prosperity. The global prosperity standard is set at $28.00-a-persona-day (in 2021 PPP terms), which is the income of a typical person living in a country at the threshold of high-income status (refer to box 2.3).5

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

## BOX 2.3

###### The Global Prosperity Gap measures inequality and the level of welfare

The Global Prosperity Gap is one of two measures the World Bank uses to track shared prosperity. The Prosperity Gap represents the number of times an average person's income would need to be multiplied to reach a globally defined prosperity standard of $28.00-a-person-a-day (in 2021 purchasing power parity [PPP] terms). It is the average of these values among all individuals across the world. The Prosperity Gap measure therefore captures both the level of income in a population of interest and the level of inequality (Kraay et al. 2023). It is defined as follows:

##### 1 ∑ N z ,

*PG =* (B2.3.1) *N y i=* 1

*i*

where z is the prosperity standard; yi is the welfare of individual i; and i = 1, …, N indexes the N people living in the population of interest (for the Global Prosperity Gap, this would be the entire population of the world). An individual experiencing a welfare level above (below) the $28.00-a-persona-day standard would be associated with an individual Prosperity Gap below (above) a value of 1. The threshold of $28.00 reflects the income of a typical person living in an economy graduating from middle-income to high-income status. Seeking to raise everyone in the world to this level of prosperity is a substantially more ambitious target than seeking to support everyone in meeting or exceeding the poverty lines for low-income, lower-middleincome, and upper-middle-income economies-that is, $3.00-, $4.20-, and $8.30-a-person-a-day, respectively (in 2021 PPP terms)-which are used to assess global progress (refer to chapter 1). The World Bank introduced the Global Prosperity Gap in 2023 as a vision indicator to measure progress toward the World Bank's vision and mission of ending extreme poverty and boosting shared prosperity on a livable planet. The measure was developed to address several issues. First, the Prosperity Gap can be aggregated across economies to provide regional or global estimates of shared prosperity. This ability to aggregate and decompose the measure facilitates valuable insights on progress in shared prosperity across, for example, regions or demographic groups. Second, the Prosperity Gap assigns greater importance to the bottom of the income distribution, thereby emphasizing the part of the population of greatest importance to the World Bank mission.a Third, while other measures, including the growth of the bottom 40 percent of the welfare distribution, require at least two rounds of comparable

*(continued)*

-----

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

###### BOX 2.3

**The Global Prosperity Gap measures inequality and the level of welfare (continued)**

data over a specific time frame, the Prosperity Gap can be measured based on a single representative survey, thereby allowing for timelier monitoring on shared prosperity even in economies for which data are limited. Changes in the growth of the bottom 40 percent nonetheless align closely with changes in the Prosperity Gap. So, the new measure preserves comparability with the World Bank's previous shared prosperity indicator (World Bank 2024). a. The Prosperity Gap is pro-poor in the sense that the contribution to the measure increases proportionally to the reduction in income. For example, consider a person living on $7.00 a day and another on $14.00 a day. Their Prosperity Gaps are 4 and 2, respectively, meaning that the person with half the income contributes twice as much to the average Prosperity Gap of society. Similarly, people living below the prosperity standard ($28.00 a day) contribute relatively more than people living above the prosperity standard. For instance, consider two individuals, one living on $18.00 and another living on $38.00 a day. Both have welfare that is $10.00 from the prosperity standard. However, the Prosperity Gap of this two-person economy is 1.1, not 1, showcasing the pro-poor nature of the measure.

### The Global Prosperity Gap has narrowed by more than half since 1990

Significant progress has been made since 1990 in closing the Global Prosperity Gap. Starting from a level of 10.5 in 1990, the gap was cut by more than half during the following two and a half decades and reached 4.5 in 2026. Improvements were most prominent until 2013, when the Global Prosperity Gap stood at 5.5 (refer to figure 2.7, panel a). Although the Prosperity Gap has continued to narrow since then, it has done so at a slowing rate, with annual improvements of approximately 1.2 percent during the latest decade (refer to figure 2.7, panel b). The 2026 gap of 4.5 means that incomes would, on average, need to be multiplied by a factor of 4.5 to lift everyone in the world to the prosperity standard of $28.00-a-person-a-day. Because the Prosperity Gap reflects both inequality and the level of welfare within a population, changes in either or both components may drive changes in the gap over time. These changes may be decomposed between inequality effects and welfare effects to gain insight into the drivers of the changes over a period (World Bank 2024). Figure 2.8 shows the average annual change in the Global Prosperity Gap in each period, decomposed into these two factors. Growth in welfare has contributed to closing the Global Prosperity Gap in every decade since 1990, and its contribution has grown since 2000. Since 2020, it has been the sole driver of the decline.

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

The role of inequality may be separated into inequality between economies and inequality within economies (Bourguignon and Morrisson 2002; Lakner and Milanović 2016). Declines in inequality between economies have contributed substantially to the narrowing of the Global Prosperity Gap because more populous poorer economies have experienced years of growth rates above the global average, thus closing the gap between poor and rich economies. The growth experiences of China and other economies in the East Asia and Pacific region in particular have driven this pattern (Bussolo et al. 2012; Mahler et al. 2022).6 Changes in inequality levels within economies have contributed both positively and negatively to the narrowing of the Global Prosperity Gap in recent decades. The declining trend in the Global Prosperity Gap since 1990 can be divided into two main periods. In the decades up to 2010, average welfare across all economies and in the poorest economies was catching up relative to the average welfare in richer economies (declining between-economy inequality), contributing to a narrowing Global Prosperity Gap. However, within-economy inequality also widened during this period, that is, the richer parts of the population within economies grew at more rapid rates relative to the poorer parts of the population, which hindered additional narrowing in the gap. Since 2010, however, welfare growth has been the predominant driver of reductions in the gap. The contribution to the reduction in the gap has been small from poorer economies catching up to richer economies (which would show up as a reduction in between-economy inequality) and from poorer individuals within economies catching up to richer individuals in the same economies (which would show up as a reduction in within-economy inequality). This is despite the substantial decline in the number of economies considered as high-inequality economies during this period (refer to figure 2.2). Indeed, since 2021, between-economy inequality has driven the widening in the Global Prosperity Gap for the first time. This may be linked to the uneven recovery from COVID-19 whereby advanced economies have managed to rebound strongly, but many emerging markets and developing economies are still struggling to regain lost ground (Kose et al. 2026).

### Progress toward shared prosperity has varied across regions

The simplicity of the Prosperity Gap facilitates its usefulness in multiple types of analysis. In addition to the decomposition described above, the gap may easily be aggregated and disaggregated across different subpopulations within a particular population. For example, population-adjusted averages of national Prosperity Gaps provide regional Prosperity Gaps, which again may be averaged into the Global Prosperity Gap. Disaggregating the trends in the Global Prosperity Gap by region makes evident that positive developments in the East Asia and Pacific region largely account for narrowing in the global gap since 1990 (refer to figure 2.9, panel a). During the period, the Prosperity Gap in the region narrowed from the second widest in the world to one of the narrowest, behind only the North America region and the Europe and Central Asia region. In 1990, the East Asia and Pacific region accounted for half the Global Prosperity Gap (refer to figure 2.9, panel b).

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 Since then, the average Prosperity Gap in East Asia and Pacific has narrowed by more than 80 percent, from 15.0 in 1990 to 2.5 in 2026. Throughout the period, Sub-Saharan Africa has had the highest regional Prosperity Gap, despite a narrowing from 17.1 in 1990 to 11.1 in 2026. In 1990, the average Prosperity Gap was only slightly larger in Sub-Saharan Africa than in East Asia and Pacific. In addition to exhibiting the widest regional Prosperity Gap in 2026, Sub-Saharan Africa accounts for 41 percent of the Global Prosperity Gap. Although Latin America and the Caribbean has consistently been the region with the greatest inequality, progress toward closing the Prosperity Gap has been substantial (Alvaredo et al. 2023). Other regions, such as Europe and Central Asia, as well as North America, have had persistently narrow regional Prosperity Gaps throughout the period. This

is because high-income economies feature heavily in these latter regions, and the prosperity standard by which the Prosperity Gap is defined is the income of a typical person in an economy transitioning into the high-income classification. The high average welfare in these regions thereby ensures narrow regional Prosperity Gaps, irrespective of the levels of inequality in the regions. The Middle East, North Africa, Afghanistan, and Pakistan region stands out for its lack of recent progress. Its regional Prosperity Gap has hovered around 5 since 2015, making it the only region other than Sub-Saharan Africa where the past decade brought essentially no improvement (refer to figure 2.9, panel a). Although all regions have experienced progress in narrowing the Prosperity Gap, this does not mean all economies have made progress. Indeed, in 1990-2026, the Prosperity Gap widened in 18 economies, representing approximately 10 percent of all economies and home to around 10 percent of the global population. Most of these are in Sub-Saharan Africa (where the gap increased in 6 of 48 economies), Europe and Central Asia (an increase in 5 of 48 economies), and MENAAP (an increase in 4 of 17 economies). The gap in global prosperity has been concentrated in a few regions throughout the period, although the composition has changed. As of 2026, South Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa together accounted for 62.8 percent of the gap, but only 37.0 percent of the global population. In 1990, the size of the population and the average income meant that China dominated in the Global Prosperity Gap (refer to figure 2.10). China alone accounted for 37.5 percent of the global gap. Together, China and India accounted for more than half the global gap in 1990. However, following China's substantial economic progress since then, the share of China in the global gap has declined substantially. In 2026, seven economies accounted for more than half the global gap (India, China, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Nigeria, Pakistan, Indonesia, and Ethiopia, in order of the size of the contribution). Despite the significant progress toward the eradication of extreme poverty (refer to chapter 1), India still weighs heavily in the global gap. Of the 10 economies that contribute the most to the global gap relative to population size, 9 are in Sub-Saharan Africa.7 The tenth is the Republic of Yemen. These 10 economies account for 15.7 percent of the global gap, but are home to only 3.6 percent of the global population.

### Beyond national numbers: The subnational variations

As the availability and quality of the data improve, more highly granular estimates of many measures, including the Prosperity Gap, become possible. This not only provides better information on the reach of development issues, but also enables policies and interventions to be targeted more effectively. Map 2.2 provides insights on subnational Prosperity Gaps in economies in which subnational data are accessible.

Map 2.3 illustrates insights on recent trends in subnational Prosperity Gaps in the Democratic Republic of Congo and Peru. The subnational data indicate that Prosperity Gaps tend to be wider in areas of economies that are more distant from coastlines, particularly in Sub-Saharan Africa. The Prosperity Gap widened in some parts of the country.

### Why has the Prosperity Gap been narrowed in East Asia and Pacific, but not in Sub-Saharan Africa?

The economies of the East Asia and Pacific region have achieved remarkable progress in narrowing the Prosperity Gap (refer to figure 2.9). In 1990-2026, the average Prosperity Gap in the region has been reduced at an annualized rate of 4.4 percent. Indeed, the progress in China alone is key to explaining the trends in the Global Prosperity Gap since 1990. Because of the size of the population and the marked improvements in average welfare in China during the past few decades, the Global Prosperity Gap would show a much more modest decline since 1990 if data on China were not included (refer to figure 2.11). In the 1990s, the world would have experienced no progress in reducing the Global Prosperity Gap if data on China were excluded.

Compared with East Asia and Pacific, Sub-Saharan Africa has reduced its regional Prosperity Gap at a much more modest pace in 1990-2026, by approximately 0.7 percent annually. This is consistent with analysis that highlights the stagnation in poverty reduction in Africa. Structural inequalities and low and volatile economic growth in Africa are barely able to keep pace with population growth (Sinha et al. 2024).

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 The experiences of the two regions can be compared by decomposing the change in the respective regional Prosperity Gaps over the period. Whereas the decomposition illustrated in figure 2.8 breaks down the trends in the Prosperity Gap into growth in mean welfare and changes in inequality both within and between economies, figure 2.12 shows a simpler decomposition that allows for easier comparison across the two regions. It shows an index of trends in the Prosperity Gap in 1990-2026 and an index of trends in mean welfare. To allow for a comparison between growth in mean welfare and the resulting narrowing in the Prosperity Gap, the reciprocal index of mean welfare is shown, similar to the method applied in the previous decomposition. Additionally, figure 2.12 shows the index of trends in the prosperity head count defined as the number of people living below the prosperity standard of $28.00-a-person-a-day (in 2021 PPP terms). Substantial growth in mean welfare has contributed substantially to the reduction in the regional Prosperity Gap in East Asia and Pacific across the decades. However, in Sub-Saharan Africa, mean welfare growth has been an inconsistent contributor to such progress. In the 1990s, mean welfare decreased in the region. This was followed by a decade of positive trends in mean welfare in the 2000s. Since 2010, there has been little progress in mean welfare (refer to chapter 1, figure 1.5). Improvements in inequality contributed to the decline in the Prosperity Gap in both regions, but mean welfare advanced much more rapidly in East Asia and Pacific, thereby contributing to greater narrowing in the Prosperity Gap there.

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

The lack of improvement in the prosperity head count-that is, individuals and households crossing the $28.00 threshold-in Sub-Saharan Africa since 1990 reflects the fact that the $28.00-a-person-a-day threshold is still only an aspiration for almost everyone across the region. In East Asia and Pacific, meanwhile, substantial progress was achieved in lifting people above the threshold, particularly in recent decades.

### A few populous economies account for much of the Prosperity Gap in Sub-Saharan Africa

Sub-Saharan Africa consists of 48 economies, but the region is dominated by a handful of populous economies. Three economies alone-Nigeria, Ethiopia, and the Democratic Republic of Congo, in order of population size-account for more than one-third of the region's population and a disproportionate share of the region's Prosperity Gap, 40 percent (refer to figure 2.13).

The three most populous economies in the region share a second characteristic. All three are each currently caught up in a fragile and conflict-affected situation (FCS) (refer to chapter 1, figures 1.11 and 1.12). Although this chapter makes no projections on the expected inequality or Prosperity Gap in coming years, issues impacting global projections of extreme poverty can be expected to influence the Prosperity Gap in FCS economies. The FCS economies in the region that face the weakest welfare growth prospects explain, together, 65 percent of the regional Prosperity Gap. The Democratic Republic of Congo, Ethiopia, and Nigeria represent 40 percent of the regional Prosperity Gap. The Democratic Republic of Congo contributes disproportionately, accounting for 9 percent of the population, but 16 percent of the Prosperity Gap of Sub- Saharan Africa. By contrast, the contribution of Ethiopia and Nigeria to the Prosperity Gap is more nearly in line with their shares in the regional population. Ethiopia accounts for

-----

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

10 percent of the population and 8 percent of the gap, while Nigeria accounts for 18 percent and 16 percent, respectively. Similarly, the contribution of Mozambique to the regional Prosperity Gap is equivalent to almost twice its population share, while the contribution of South Sudan is almost three times its population share. Richer economies, such as Cabo Verde, Mauritius, and the Seychelles, contribute significantly less than their population shares to the regional Prosperity Gap. Côte d'Ivoire and Senegal contribute approximately half their population shares.

## Progress in shared prosperity is slowing

The evidence in this chapter points to genuine but incomplete progress on both dimensions of shared prosperity. The number of high-inequality economies fell from 70 in 2000 to 42 in 2024, and, even in economies that remained above the threshold throughout the period, the mean Gini index improved from about 51 to about 46. The Global Prosperity Gap has narrowed by more than half, from 10.5 in 1990 to 4.5 in 2026. Yet, these headline gains conceal a more sobering fact. One in five people worldwide are still living in high-inequality economies. The share of the world's extreme poor living in high-inequality economies nearly doubled between 2000 and 2024, and nearly one in three are outside China and India. The regional pattern sharpens the concern. The Prosperity Gap in East Asia and Pacific has been narrowed by more than 80 percent between 1990 and 2026, demonstrating that rapid, broad-based progress is possible within a generation. The gap in Sub-Saharan Africa, by contrast, is at 11.1, more than twice the global average today and close to the global average in 1980. The shortfall is concentrated in a few populous economies in a fragile and conflictaffected situation. The MENAAP region has exhibited little improvement since 2015. The task ahead therefore involves not simply to sustain global momentum, but to extend it to the economies in which high inequality is most persistent, poverty is deepest, and the conditions for progress, including peace and stability, are weakest.

## Annex 2A. Country-year data to assess

### high inequality

This annex describes the construction of the country-year dataset used to produce the count of high-inequality economies and the related figures in this chapter. The indicator is defined as the number of economies with a Gini index above 40 based on the most recent household survey available in each economy. The underlying dataset covers 169 economies in which at least one relevant household survey was conducted between 2000 and 2024 and collected from the Poverty and Inequality Platform (PIP). Economies in which no

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 survey has been conducted since 2000 are excluded. National estimates are used throughout, with one exception. Because no nationally representative survey is available on the country, Argentina is the only case in which an urban-only survey is used. Table 2A.1 shows the coverage of the Gini index since 2000. It covers the 169 economies and 1,764 survey-year observations. The coverage differs by welfare concept; however, consumption-based surveys span more economies (100), but represent fewer total observations (584). Income-based surveys cover fewer economies (69), but represent more frequent observations (1,180), reflecting the greater regularity in the surveys administered in richer economies. A country-year panel for 2000-24 is constructed by generating the full set of years for each economy and filling gaps by carrying forward the most recent observed Gini index. For years prior to an economy's first available survey, the first observed value is applied retroactively to earlier years during which no survey exists. A Gini index is thus available on each economy for every year in the panel. This means that the classification associated with an economy remains unchanged until new survey data emerge, at which point the economy may appear to cross the threshold in a single year even if the underlying change occurred gradually over the intervening period. The continuous coverage achieved using this methodology thus has a price. Apparent transitions may merely be a reflection of data updates rather than of economic change. Threshold-based counts should therefore be read as tracking information that becomes available rather than tracking only changes in economic conditions as they occur.

### Annex 2B . Limitations in current measures of inequality

Box 2.1 and annex 2A introduce the key properties of the Gini index. They each note that household survey data exhibit several well-documented measurement limitations that impact cross-country comparisons. This annex elaborates on five of these challenges: the choice of welfare concept, changes in survey design that influence comparability over time, the underrepresentation of top incomes, issues related to bottom-coding, and the use of the household as the unit of measurement. Each of these challenges affects the level of measured inequality and the comparability of estimates across economies and over time. Many of the issues are also relevant in the case of poverty measures.

### Measures by consumption or income affect the level of inequality and cross-country comparability differently

The overview of high-inequality economies in this chapter is based on welfare measures available through nationally representative household surveys in each economy. In some economies, the welfare measure is consumption; in others, it is income. This availability is not random. Wealthier economies and economies in Latin America and the Caribbean typically rely on income-based surveys irrespective of income classification, while lowerincome economies, particularly those in South Asia or Sub-Saharan Africa, predominantly use consumption-based surveys. This matters because the two measures do not yield the same results. Consumption-based Gini values are systematically lower than income-based Gini values because consumption smooths out short-term income fluctuations. Consumption is also more equally distributed than income because of saving behavior and a natural lower bound in consumption measures. Figure 2B.1 illustrates this in economies for which both measures are available in the same survey year. Most observations represented in the figure lie above the 45-degree parity line, confirming that income inequality generally exceeds consumption inequality. The fitted line has a positive slope, showing that more-unequal distributions of consumption also tend to be associated with moreunequal distributions of income. The implication for cross-regional comparisons is noteworthy. Because economies in Latin America and the Caribbean primarily rely on income-based measures, while economies in Sub-Saharan Africa predominantly use consumption-based measures, part of the observed gap in average Gini values between the two regions reflects differences in welfare concepts rather than differences in the underlying distribution. Deudibe et al. (2026) find that converting consumption-based welfare measures into income-equivalent welfare measures would result in the classification of more economies as highly unequal. Most of these additional high-inequality economies would be in Sub-Saharan Africa. Indeed, following such an adjustment, Sub-Saharan Africa would be more unequal on average than Latin America and the Caribbean, the reverse of the outcome suggested by unadjusted survey data (refer to map 2B.1).

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

Consumption and income are not linked by a fixed relationship. Precautionary savings, limited access to credit, and differences in the ability of households to smooth temporary income shocks may all affect how closely current consumption tracks current income. In addition, measured consumption can be sensitive to the method used to capture price changes, particularly if the incidence of inflation varies across goods, locations, or households. Because these factors differ across economies and over time, the gap between consumption- and income-based measures also varies, reinforcing the need for caution in the comparison of inequality based on different welfare concepts across economies.

### Inequality measures may also be affected by changes in survey design

Survey comparability depends on a range of methodological characteristics: the sampling process and sampling frame, the design of a questionnaire, the construction of the welfare aggregate, and the price deflation methods used to convert nominal values into real terms across space and time. If any of these features change between survey rounds, as may occur if statistical systems are updated in an economy, an observed change in the Gini index across two survey periods may reflect a methodological revision rather than a shift in the underlying distribution. The PIP contains metadata on the comparability of estimates on economies over time, thereby allowing users to identify the consecutive survey pairs that are considered methodologically consistent and those that are not. The analysis in this chapter of inequality trends draws on this comparability information where relevant, and spells of consecutive high inequality are interpreted within the context of this limitation (Castañeda Aguilar et al. 2026; World Bank 2026).

### Survey-based measures underestimate inequality at the top of the distribution

Survey-based measures tend to underestimate top incomes (refer, for instance, to Atkinson et al. 2011). High-income households are less likely to participate in surveys, and those that do may underreport income from capital, dividends, and other nonlabor sources that are difficult to capture in household questionnaires. Attempts at correcting for these missing top incomes usually rely on using either (1) administrative tax data or (2) national accounts data. Such corrections consistently yield higher inequality estimates relative to unadjusted survey data (Andreescu et al. 2025). The method used to implement these corrections matters. Applying various correction methodologies using tax data on four Latin American economies shows that different methods can result in different levels, rankings, and changes in inequality across the economies (Lustig and Vigorito 2025).

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 Whether correcting for top incomes would substantially alter the trends reported in this chapter, as opposed to the levels, remains a subject of active empirical debate (Lakner and Milanović 2016; Lustig and Vigorito 2025). There is a risk, however, that the observed decline in measured inequality over time partly reflects a measurement artifact. As economies develop and financial markets deepen, top incomes may become increasingly difficult to capture in household surveys, which could cause measured inequality to narrow even in the absence of true distributional improvement (Korinek et al. 2006).

### Methodological issues at the bottom of the welfare distribution also impact inequality

At the other end of the welfare distribution, methodological issues related to extremely low values also influence inequality measures. The Prosperity Gap, for example, is designed to be particularly sensitive to low values of consumption or income. While consumption-based measures have a natural lower bound at some low positive value, income-based measures can and do include zero and nonnegligible negative values. Organizations differ in how such data points are handled and whether distributions are bottom-coded or truncated (Yonzan et al. 2026). The methodological choice in the way to handle low values, including the threshold established for correcting data, affects the levels of and, possibly, the trends in measures of inequality and shared prosperity. For the data available in the PIP, a bottom-coding threshold of $0.28 (in 2021 PPP terms) is applied (Castañeda Aguilar et al. 2026; Yonzan et al. 2026). This threshold has been chosen because it is not overly intrusive on the surveys, but adequately addresses the impact of small values on distribution-sensitive measures of inequality and shared prosperity. Introducing the threshold has a negligible effect on mean welfare (whether income or consumption) and Gini indexes, although it meaningfully influences the estimated Prosperity Gap in a handful of economies, mostly two decades past.

### Measuring welfare at the household level obscures inequality within households, introducing bias in both directions

Existing measures of poverty and inequality rely on the household as the primary unit of analysis, implicitly assuming that consumption or income is distributed evenly among members of a given household (Haddad and Kanbur 1990). A growing body of evidence suggests this assumption is not valid, particularly in the case of household composition by sex and age (Doss 1996; World Bank 2018). Failing to account for within-household inequality may result in underestimates of true inequality (Jolliffe et al. 2025). At the same time, most measures of inequality do not account for the fact that some goods, such as shelter, can be shared among household members without reducing the availability to other household members (Deaton and Paxson 1998). Treating all consumption as private goods

-----

The Path to Shared Prosperity Is Bum py and U neven

rather than partially public goods would overestimate individual-level inequality. These two sources of bias operate in opposite directions, and their net effect on measured inequality is an open empirical question, the response to which varies across settings. Allowing (some) inequality within the household results in greater equivalence in the importance of withincountry inequality and between-country inequality in global measures. Providing for the sharing of resources within a household (for instance, the fact that housing, electricity services, and so on are enjoyed by all rather than only one household member) reduces inequality because poorer households tend to be larger, and this increases their per capita welfare relatively more.

## Notes

1. The World Bank Poverty and Inequality Platform (PIP) contains statistics derived from household surveys in 172 economies as of September 2026. These economies covered approximately 97.5 percent of the global population in 2024. The analysis in this report uses information for the 169 economies in PIP for which there is a survey at least as recent as 2000. These latter economies represented approximately 97.4 percent of the global population in 2024. Refer to Poverty and Inequality Platform, World Bank, https://pip.worldbank.org/home.
2. These data are presented as a sensitivity check to illustrate the strength of the dependence of the global share on the classification of a few large economies. The classifications of China and India are also based on consumption surveys, which typically yield lower measured inequality than income-based surveys and may capture concentration at the top less fully (refer to box 2.1 and annex 2B).
3. Among economies that have ever been classified as high inequality, the mean longest spell length is essentially the same whether they are regularly surveyed (surveyed nearly every year) or infrequently surveyed (fewer than 10 surveys in 25 years), that is, more than 17.6 years and 17.1 years, respectively. The persistence of high inequality is therefore not an artifact of the frequency of surveys.
4. Adjusting the Gini index of each economy by population yields the same downward trend. Both the simple and the population-adjusted average Gini index fell by about five points in 2000-24. The population-adjusted mean was roughly one to two points higher throughout, indicating that the more populous economies in the group were somewhat more unequal than the smaller ones. However, the two series change in parallel. So, the decline is not an artifact associated with an average driven by small economies.
5. The Prosperity Gap was initially defined using a global prosperity standard of $25.00-a-person-a-day in 2017 PPP terms. When the international poverty lines were updated to reflect 2021 PPP (Foster et al. 2025), the prosperity standard was also updated, to $28.00.
6. If China is excluded from the trends in the Global Prosperity Gap, improvements in the gap are much more modest. In this case, the gap has declined from 8.4 in 1990 to 5.0 in 2026 (refer to figure 2.11).
7. This is the ratio of each economy's share of the Global Prosperity Gap relative to each economy's share of the global population. If all economies had the same Prosperity Gap, this ratio would be 1 for all. In economies in which the Prosperity Gap is greater (smaller) than the global average, this ratio will be above (below) 1. Sorted by their contribution to the Global Prosperity Gap relative to their population size, the 10 economies are South Sudan, Mozambique, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Zambia, the Central African Republic, Malawi, Burundi, the Federal Republic of Somalia, the Republic of Yemen, and the Republic of Congo.

# 3

## The Livable Planet: Tracking Progress

## Summary

- *Rising temperatures, ecosystem loss,food insecurity, and limited access to clean water and* *sanitation all constrain whether development gains can be achieved and sustained. This* *chapter monitors five indicators that, together, track the livable planet pillar of the World Bank* *mission-people at high risk from climate-related hazards, greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions,* *the extent of key ecosystems,food and nutrition insecurity, and access to water, sanitation,* *and hygiene (WASH)-and finds that progress is uneven. Several indicators are moving in the* *wrong direction.*
- *Across all five indicators, the populations most affected are often the same. The poorest people* *and poorest countries have contributed least to global emissions. Yet, they are the most vulnerable* *to climate-related hazards, depend most heavily on degrading ecosystems, experience the highest* *levels of food insecurity, and are least likely to have access to safe water and sanitation.*
- *Global GHG emissions are at record levels, even as economic growth is becoming less tightly* *linked to emissions in many countries.*
- *In the poorest countries, the poverty-reduction benefits of growth far outweigh the costs of* *additional emissions. The scope for reducing emissions is greatest in wealthier countries, where* *emissions are high and the incidence of extreme poverty is low.*
- *Ecosystem loss,food insecurity, and gaps in access to WASH reinforce this pattern,* *compounding vulnerability precisely in places and among populations that possess the least* *capacity for coping. Degrading ecosystems undermine livelihoods and informal safety nets.* *Inadequate water and sanitation erode health and human capital.*
- *These challenges converge most acutely in Sub-Saharan Africa, where climate vulnerability,* *food insecurity, and gaps in basic services are severe.*

A reproducibility package is available for this book in the Reproducible Research Repository at

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

### Introduction to the vision of a livable planet

The World Bank's mission to end extreme poverty and boost shared prosperity on a livable planet recognizes that poverty reduction depends on environmental conditions. Rising temperatures, forest loss, food insecurity, and limited access to clean water and sanitation constrain poverty reduction and compromise the durability of any gains. This chapter monitors five indicators that together track the livable planet pillar of the World Bank vision (refer to figure 3.1). Each captures a different dimension of the challenge: (1) the share of people at high risk from climate-related hazards and (2) the annual emissions of GHGs track the challenges in climate adaptation and mitigation; (3) the extent of key ecosystems monitors the state of nature and biodiversity; and (4) the prevalence of food and nutrition insecurity, together with (5) access to WASH, serves as a proxy for access to life's most basic essential needs.

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

The indicators of the five dimensions of the challenge are interconnected. GHG emissions intensify the hazards to which the poor are exposed. Ecosystem degradation undermines the food systems and water cycles on which the poor depend. Food insecurity and WASH deprivation compound each other through shared pathways of disease, malnutrition, and diminished human capital. A deterioration in any one indicator increases the difficulty of making progress on the others. The same groups are often affected across indicators: the poor and near-poor in low-income and lower-middle-income countries, who have the least capacity to absorb shocks and the fewest alternatives if systems fail. This chapter is primarily a monitoring exercise. It describes what the indicators show, the directions in which they are moving, and the way they are connected to poverty and shared prosperity. It provides a comprehensive treatment of all five indicators. It also incorporates four major data releases that were not available for Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2024 (World Bank 2024b), as follows: the Emissions Database for Global Atmospheric Research 2025 report covering global emissions through 2024 (Crippa et al. 2025); The State of Food Security *and Nutrition in the World 2026 (FAO et al. 2026); the World Health Organization-United* Nations Children's Fund Joint Monitoring Programme 2025 report covering household WASH access in 2000-24 (JMP 2025); and the Global Nature Watch 2024 tree cover loss analysis (WRI 2025). Some indicators vary little from one year to the next. Others do not change because the underlying data have not been updated. If the data have not changed materially since 2024, this is noted explicitly. Readers can thus distinguish genuine updates from findings that are carried forward.

### People at high risk from climaterelated hazards

### Unpacking exposure, vulnerability, and risks

The climate risk indicator tracks individuals who are exposed to at least one of four key climate hazards-floods, droughts, cyclones, and heat waves-and who are identified as highly vulnerable along at least one of these dimensions. Vulnerability is understood in two ways. Physical vulnerability reflects limited mobility and restricted access to basic infrastructure, such as water and electricity. Economic vulnerability is proxied by low income, limited education, and lack of access to financial services or social protection (refer to figure 3.2). The analysis draws on data from 103 economies, covering 86 percent of the global population. Coverage gaps remain, particularly in small island states, which face some of the most severe climate pressures.

### Around 4.5 billion people are living in areas exposed to major hazards

Around 4.5 billion people, approximately 60 percent of the global population, live in places where a damaging climate event is expected at least once during their lives. Heat waves account for the largest share. They affect 42 percent of people worldwide. Droughts follow, at 18 percent, and floods and cyclones affect 10 percent and 8 percent, respectively (refer to figure 3.3). Because climate change is intensifying the frequency and severity of extreme events, these shares are expected to climb. Regional patterns differ sharply. South Asia has the highest proportion of exposed population, at 88 percent, followed by East Asia and Pacific, at 68 percent. Heat wave exposure drives many such results. Four in five South Asians are living under significant heat risk, while the share in East Asia and Pacific is half the population. More than 1 in 10 people are exposed to flooding in East Asia and Pacific; the Middle East, North Africa, Afghanistan, and Pakistan (MENAAP); and South Asia. Drought, meanwhile, is more prevalent across Europe and Central Asia, Latin America and the Caribbean, North America, and Sub-Saharan Africa. Behind these aggregate numbers lie substantive within-region differences. Box 3.1 illustrates the example of Latin America and the Caribbean, where existing data allow the exposure to multiple climate hazards to be analyzed at a granular level within countries and even within selected cities.

Exposure by income group follows an inverted-U curve. Lower-middle-income economies bear the greatest burden, at 73 percent exposure rate, followed by upper-middle-income economies, at 55 percent. Rates are lower in both low-income (41 percent) and high-income economies (32 percent), though the hazards differ. Drought dominates among low-income economies, whereas heat waves are most prevalent in middle-income economies. Nonetheless, climate hazards are not always impartial about who they affect, rich or poor, young or old, women or men (refer to box 3.2).

## BOX 3.1

###### A closer look at Latin America and the Caribbean

More than one-third of the population of Latin America and the Caribbean, around 240 million people, were living in areas exposed to at least one of five major climate hazards in 2020: droughts, heat waves, cyclones, floods, and landslides. Drought is the most widespread of these, affecting 13.4 percent of the Latin American and Caribbean population. The poorest households face disproportionately greater exposure. Nearly 45 percent of poor households are exposed to at least one hazard, compared with 34 percent among nonpoor households. The gap is most pronounced in the case of drought and landslides because poverty tends to be geographically concentrated in areas of fragile, steep, or arid terrain. Subnational analysis reveals distinct hotspots in which poverty and climate exposure converge (refer to map B3.1.1). These hotspots, home to roughly 64 million people, or 10 percent of the regional population (estimated at approximately 673 million people), include the Caribbean coast of the Yucatán, parts of Mexico's southern and Gulf coasts, the Central American Dry Corridor, northeast Brazil, the upper Amazon, and the Chaco region.

###### BOX 3.2

###### The 2026-27 El Niño shows how climate shocks can set back development outcomes

The 2026-27 El Niño event is exceptional, already approaching record levels of intensity in the modern era and expected to strengthen through late 2026. Its impacts are likely to extend well into 2027. The pattern varies sharply by region. Droughts and wildfires are more prevalent in some areas, and heavy rainfall, floods, and landslides in others. Agriculture, water availability, hydropower, fisheries, transport, and energy systems are all being affected. Food and nutrition security is a particular concern. Agricultural disruption can lead to tight food supplies and rising prices, which have serious consequences especially among poor and vulnerable households. It has been estimated that a 5 percent rise in food prices increases the risk of severe child wasting by 14 percent in low- and middle-income economies (Headey and Ruel 2023). Children born under El Niño conditions may also face a heightened risk of impaired growth, with potentially lasting effects. These pressures can fall disproportionately on women and girls because households adjust through

*(continued)*

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

###### BOX 3.2

###### The 2026-27 El Niño shows how climate shocks can set back development outcomes

*(continued)*

reduced food intake, increased unpaid care work, and, in some cases, school withdrawal. Landless households, pastoralists, informal workers, displaced people, and other vulnerable groups have fewer assets to buffer these shocks. Water shortages threaten irrigation and drinking water supplies, while disruptions to hydropower and fisheries may affect energy and livelihoods. Transport disruptions, including through the Panama Canal, may raise the cost of food, fertilizer, and fuel, particularly among small island states and low-income food-importing economies. Changes in disease transmission, wildfire smoke, heat waves, and school closures illustrate how a single climate phenomenon may propagate across multiple development outcomes. These effects can also reinforce gender inequalities because women often play central roles in household food provision, water collection, natural-resource management, and household coping with shocks. The broader lesson is that acute climate shocks are capable of leaving lasting development scars. Although El Niño is transient, its effects can persist through lost assets and lost income, malnutrition, interrupted schooling, ecosystem damage, and reduced fiscal space. In a warmer world, these shocks may compound existing climate stresses and render progress in the longer-term planet outcomes tracked in this chapter more difficult to sustain.

### Around 2.8 billion people are living under highly vulnerable conditions

In 2024, 2.8 billion people, 35 percent of the global population, were considered highly vulnerable to climate in at least one physical or economic dimension (refer to figure 3.2). This headline number captures the scale of the challenge, but it masks an important reality. People become vulnerable along different pathways, and many face a mix of disadvantages across multiple dimensions simultaneously. The single largest driver of vulnerability is lack of social protection, which affects 1.6 billion people. This means that one in five people worldwide have no formal safety net on which to rely if a climate hazard strikes. Financial exclusion is the second most widespread dimension, affecting 980 million people, followed closely by education vulnerability, at 900 million. Income vulnerability-that is, living on less than the extreme poverty threshold (refer to chapter 1)- affects about 10 percent of the world's population. Lack of access to electricity is a burden on

-----

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

570 million people, and 410 million people enjoy only limited access to safe water, as defined by this climate vulnerability indicator. This is distinct from the broader WASH measure, under which 2.1 billion people lack safely managed drinking water. The comparison between the 10 percent figure for income vulnerability and the 35 percent figure for overall vulnerability carries an important implication. The majority of highly vulnerable people are living above the extreme poverty line. Many are in households that have escaped monetary poverty, but have no social protection, no access to financial services, or little education and therefore few means to absorb a climate shock. These gaps are not confined to any income group. They can affect middle-class households as well (refer to chapter 1). The consequences of climate hazards therefore extend beyond the poor and put at risk both the durability of a previous escape from poverty and the economic security characteristic of middle-class status. These dimensions do not operate in isolation. A smallholder farmer in Sub-Saharan Africa, for instance, may simultaneously face income poverty, lack of social protection, limited education, and no access to financial services. This means that a single drought may undermine livelihoods that are not supported by a layer of protection. Such overlapping vulnerabilities, rather than any single factor, determine how heavily a climate shock weighs on households and how long recovery lasts. Understanding the dimensions driving vulnerability and the ways they may overlap is essential to designing interventions that reduce climate risk rather than addressing symptoms in isolation. In this sense, multidimensional poverty is also a source of climate vulnerability. The same deficits that constrain development can also determine whether a climate hazard becomes a major welfare shock.

### About 1.5 billion people are highly vulnerable and highly exposed to climate-related hazards

In 2024, 17.3 percent of people globally were experiencing high risk from climate-related hazards. MENAAP and Sub-Saharan Africa stand out with the highest percentage of people at risk, at 47 percent and 35 percent, respectively (refer to figure 3.4). In both regions, the shares of people who are at risk and the shares of people who are exposed are similar, meaning that vulnerability is nearly universal among people living in hazard-prone areas. In South Asia, the population at risk has declined substantially, to below 20 percent, since 2010, driven by a reduction in vulnerability. The remaining regions all show rates below 15 percent, reflecting, primarily, low vulnerability among people. Most of the people at risk and exposed to hazards in these regions are in the East Asia and Pacific region and in the Latin America and Caribbean region. Low exposure and limited vulnerability are characteristic of Europe and Central Asia and of North America. Since 2010, global progress in the high climate risk indicator has been mixed. While the high-risk share declined from 1 in 3 people in 2010 to 1 in 5 people in 2021, the absolute number of exposed individuals rose by 461 million. This trend highlights the urgent need for policies that not only manage exposure, but address the root causes of vulnerability (Brunckhorst et al. 2026).

The analysis in this section focuses on climate-related hazards as a primary dimension in measuring risk in a livable planet. However, climate hazards are not the only environmental stressors shaping global welfare. Air pollution, water stress, and land degradation impose additional and often overlapping burdens that interact with climate risks to compound the development constraints facing low-income communities and economies. Box 3.3 examines who bears the brunt of these broader environmental burdens, drawing on new subnational evidence from across regions. The findings are consistent with those documented here. Exposure to environmental hazards is systematically greater in poorer countries and, within countries, is concentrated in poorer areas. There is one important nuance. Indoor exposure and outdoor exposure to air pollution are two separate cases. Nonetheless, the climate hazard evidence and the environmental hazard evidence together point to the same underlying conclusion. The communities and economies contributing least to global emissions and environmental degradation are the ones that are absorbing most of the associated costs.

-----

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

## BOX 3.3

###### The burden of environmental hazards is not shared equally

Climate hazards-floods, droughts, heat waves, and cyclones-are not the only environmental risks shaping welfare outcomes in the developing world. Air pollution, water stress, and land degradation impose additional and often overlapping burdens that interact with climate hazards to create a more severe drag on development that falls most heavily on the poorest communities and economies. Environmental challenges disproportionately affect developing economies, thereby undermining progress toward reducing poverty and improving living standards. In low-income economies, an alarming 79 percent of the population is exposed simultaneously to air pollution, water risks, and land degradation, while fewer than 1 percent are free of all three environmental stressors (Damania et al. 2025). In contrast, in high-income economies, 43 percent of the population is not exposed to any stressors, and only 1 percent is troubled by all three. This demonstrates that addressing environmental challenges is not merely desirable. It is smart development. Approximately 75 percent of the global population is exposed to at least one acute environmental or climate hazard (refer to figure B3.3.1). More than half face a climate hazard specifically, while hazardous air pollution and high water stress each burden roughly 37 percent of people globally. These hazards are not evenly distributed. Land degradation affects around 10 percent of the global population overall, but is most acute in Sub-Saharan Africa. Water stress is most widespread in South Asia and in the Middle East and North Africa. In South Asia, nearly everyone faces at least one serious hazard. In many low-income economies and economies caught up in a fragile and conflict-affected situation, these threats overlap and reinforce each other, creating a persistent constraint on welfare and development. More than half the people in low-income economies and 80 percent of people in lower-middle-income economies are exposed to at least land degradation, hazardous air pollution, or high water stress where they live. Among highincome countries, the share is 1 in 10. Pollution tends to be highest in low- and middle-income economies because these economies generally rely more on polluting industries and technologies. About 80 percent of people exposed to unsafe levels of fine particulate matter (PM2.5) outdoors live in these economies (Rentschler and Leonova 2023).a Climate hazards are also disproportionately concentrated in middle-income economies.

The case of air pollution is more nuanced. Poorer economies exhibit higher exposure rates. Within economies, however, the relationship between income and outdoor air pollution can run in the opposite direction. In many settings, outdoor PM2.5 exposure increases with income, particularly in places where transport is a major source of pollution (Behrer and Heft-Neal 2024). In West Africa, dust from the Sahara and from the Sahel region dominates pollution levels, weakening the link between household income and air pollution exposure. But outdoor exposure tells only part of the story. Poorer households are far more likely to cook with solid fuels, driving indoor air pollution well above the levels outdoor monitors capture. These households generally lack access to air purifiers or other protective measures. So, while outdoor pollution may be similar across income groups locally, poorer households face substantially higher total exposure once indoor sources are included, and they possess fewer means to reduce it (Jack and Ryan 2026). Taken together, these findings extend and reinforce the poverty-planet nexus documented throughout this chapter. The burden of environmental degradation in all its forms, not only climate hazards, is systematically skewed toward those with the least capacity to absorb it. a. PM2.5: fine inhalable air pollution particles that measure 2.5 micrometers or less in diameter.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

## Global greenhouse gas emissions

The indicator described in this section tracks total annual GHG emissions from energy, industry, and agriculture. Emissions associated with land use, land use change, and forestry (LULUCF) are shown separately because they are more variable over time and may be either a net source of GHG or a sink absorbing more GHG from the atmosphere than they release. The processes that drive LULUCF emissions are also different relative to those that drive emissions from energy and production. The trends discussed below therefore refer to GHG emissions, excluding LULUCF emissions. Global GHG emissions, excluding LULUCF emissions, reached 52.8 metric gigatons of carbon dioxide equivalent (GtCO₂e) in 2024 (refer to figure 3.5), the highest level on record, but only 0.6 percent above the global GHG in 2023. They are now approximately 48 percent greater than they were in 2000 because they have grown by an average of nearly 1.6 percent annually. With only two exceptions (the 2007-09 global financial crisis and the 2020 COVID-19 shock), emissions have risen continuously since 2000. LULUCF emissions, meanwhile, have remained slightly positive on net over the past several decades, despite substantial year-to-year variation. Limiting warming to 1.5°C is now considered out of reach without a period of overshoot (that is, by reducing emissions each year by more than the annual target). Both the peak level of warming and the duration of the overshoot are important because the amount and length of time that global temperatures are above the safe target limit raises the risks to people, economies, and ecosystems (UNEP 2026).

GHG = greenhouse gas; GtCO2e = metric gigatons of carbon dioxide equivalent; kgCO2e/$ = kilograms of carbon dioxide equivalent per US dollar; LULUCF = land use, land use change, and forestry; MENAAP = Middle East, North Africa, Afghanistan, and Pakistan; tCO2e = metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent.

### Economic growth is increasingly accompanied by lower emissions

For much of the past century, economic growth was closely associated with a rising use of fossil fuels and increasing GHG emissions. This relationship is changing. Decoupling-the weakening of the link between economic growth and GHG emissions-is evident across a growing number of economies. It is not automatic, however. Cleaner technologies, energy transitions, infrastructure, efficiency improvements, and policy choices all shape whether growth translates into higher emissions. The experience across economies therefore shows that there is no single emissions pathway associated with rising incomes. Growth in many major economies has begun to decouple from GHG emissions. Australia, Germany, the United Kingdom, the United States, and several other high-income countries have achieved economic growth, while stabilizing or reducing emissions (refer to figure 3.6). China and India have also experienced relative decoupling. In 2001-22, relative to the growth of the economies, emissions there grew at a substantially slower pace, thereby lowering emissions intensity. Overall, while emissions intensity has fallen across most major emitters, these improvements have not yet been sufficient to produce a sustained decline in global emissions.

Several forces determine the differences in emissions intensity across economies. Population and income growth tend to raise the demand for energy, while the structure of economic activity affects the level of the emissions intensity associated with growth. Efficiency improvements, cleaner power, electrification, and shifts toward less carbon-intensive sectors can offset these pressures. High-income countries have generally gone furthest in doing so, whereas rapidly growing middle-income economies face a larger challenge because energy demand is still rising quickly (Chepeliev et al. 2027). Economic development is nonetheless possible in a context of low emissions no matter the income of an economy. Analysis of 93 economies covered by World Bank Country Climate and Development Reports finds that low-emissions pathways could reduce global GHG emissions by around 70 percent by 2050, while exerting neutral or positive effects on GDP in most

-----

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

economies (World Bank 2025a). Higher-income economies may deliver the largest near-term reductions, but lower-income economies have an opportunity to avoid locking into carbonintensive technologies and infrastructure. The challenge is therefore not whether economic growth and emissions can be decoupled, but whether decoupling can occur sufficiently rapidly and broadly to guide global emissions onto a sustainable downward trend.

### Reductions in extreme poverty and in GHG emissions need not entail a trade-off

Global emissions are concentrated among a relatively small number of large economies, while low-income economies contribute only a small share. In 2024, Brazil, China, the European Union, India, the Russian Federation, and the United States together accounted for 62 percent of global GHG emissions. Sub-Saharan Africa's emissions were the lowest of any region, at approximately 1.6 metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO₂e) per capita in Western and Central Africa and 2.0 tCO₂e per capita in Eastern and Southern Africa. This asymmetry is central to the relationship between emissions and poverty reduction. The economies with the greatest need for more economic growth are generally not the economies driving global emissions. Against this backdrop, the goal of reducing poverty while reducing GHG emissions is not in inherent tension. Estimates indicate that the economic growth needed to eradicate extreme poverty would add less than 5 percent to global emissions relative to the levels of 2019, a marginal increase that reflects the extremely low consumption footprint of the global extreme poor (Wollburg et al. 2023). The estimate is based on historical relationships between economic growth and carbon intensity. Cleaner technologies, renewable energy, electrification, and more efficient buildings and transport offer a new pathway toward future poverty reduction in a context of declining emissions. The balance between the poverty reduction benefits of growth and the future poverty costs of associated emissions differs sharply across countries. Hallegatte et al. (2026) compare the number of people who will escape poverty across economies as a result of a greater economic output of 1 percent in 2026 against the number of people worldwide who will fall into poverty by the end of the century through the added emissions associated with the 1 percent higher growth in 2026. Among the poorest economies, the poverty reduction benefits of economic growth far exceed the future poverty costs associated with additional emissions, often by a factor of 100 or more (refer to figure 3.7). This is because extreme poverty is severe and responsive to economic growth, whereas the emissions of these economies are too small to impose large future poverty costs on the world. In contrast, in wealthy economies in which poverty rates are already relatively low, the direct benefits of poverty reduction that are associated with additional economic growth tend to be smaller or even negative.

## Key ecosystems

The key ecosystems indicator tracks the extent of forests, wetlands, mangroves, coral reefs, savannas, and other biomes that perform critical functions for climate regulation and human welfare. The indicator is currently being updated. The baseline measure for the year 2000 shows that there are approximately 7,860 million hectares of these ecosystems, which cover roughly

-----

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

60 percent of the earth's total land area. The systems are unevenly distributed across the globe. Large shares are concentrated in a relatively small number of countries in regions, such as Latin America, Sub-Saharan Africa, and parts of East Asia and Pacific. Key ecosystems are crucial along multiple pathways. They support livelihoods directly through forests, fisheries, freshwater, soils, and other natural resources; underpin agricultural productivity through pollination, soil fertility, water regulation, and pest control; buffer communities against floods, droughts, and other shocks; regulate disease and ecological stability; and act as globally important carbon sinks. Biodiversity loss can weaken ecosystem stability in ways that are difficult to predict, including through changes in disease dynamics and ecological interactions. Renewable natural capital represents more than 30 percent of total wealth in lowand lower-middle-income countries, while ecosystem-dependent sectors, such as fisheries, support hundreds of millions of livelihoods worldwide. Forests alone absorb approximately 2.4 billion metric tons of carbon dioxide (CO₂) per year, roughly one-third of the CO₂ released annually from the burning of fossil fuels (FAO 2025). Estimates indicate that forests removed about 1.3 GtCO₂e in 2024, but forest fires contributed even more (2.2 GtCO₂e). The LULUCF sector is thus a net GHG source of 0.9 GtCO₂e. This reversal shows how ecosystem degradation can convert a climate asset into a climate liability within a single year (Crippa et al. 2025).

### Primary tropical forest cover suffered a record-breaking setback in 2024

About 9 percent of global primary tropical forest cover was lost in 2001-25 in aggregate, a longrun trend with substantial regional heterogeneity (refer to figure 3.8). But 2024 marked a sharp deterioration. The Global Nature Watch analysis recorded the worst year in tropical primary forest loss since monitoring began (WRI 2025). Tropical regions lost 6.7 million hectares of primary rainforest, nearly twice the 2023 losses and an area approaching the size of Panama. For the first time on record, fires, not agriculture, were the leading cause of tropical primary forest loss, accounting for nearly half of all destruction. In geographical terms, Bolivia's primary forest loss jumped 200 percent, making it the second-largest source of tropical forest loss globally, overtaking the Democratic Republic of Congo for the first time (WRI 2025). In 2025, losses returned closer to the more recent annual average trend, representing a partial, but significant recovery. Permanent agriculture remains the largest driver of primary forest loss over the long run, accounting for 73 percent of loss in 2002-25. The 2024-25 episode therefore illustrates both the vulnerability of the indicator to short-term shocks and the possibility of reversal through deliberate action. The forest trends must be viewed within the broader ecosystem picture. Coral reef degradation is acute in East Asia and Pacific, where bleaching events driven by ocean warming are more frequent and more severe than historical baselines. Wetland loss is a global phenomenon affecting freshwater availability and coastal protection. Tropical deforestation remains concentrated in Latin America and Sub-Saharan Africa, where the overlap between ecosystem loss and poverty is most acute.

### The paths of connection between ecosystem health and poverty reduction

Renewable natural capital-the living components of ecosystems that generate a continuous flow of goods and services as long as they are not depleted more quickly than they regeneraterepresents more than 30 percent of the total wealth in low- and lower-middle-income economies. This means ecosystem degradation is not only an environmental loss, but a direct erosion of the asset base on which development depends. Among the rural poor within these economies, the share in wealth is greater still. Ecosystems are the primary asset among the rural poor, the main source of food, income, and resilience, functioning as an informal safety net for those without access to formal financial systems or social protection. One direct path of connection between ecosystem health and poverty reduction is livelihoods. Deforestation-driven changes in local hydrology reduce agricultural yields even in areas not directly cleared. Damania et al. (2025) estimate that this mechanism generates $379 billion in annual agricultural losses globally. Fishing communities that depend on coastal and reef systems face collapsing stocks as ecosystems degrade. The 200 million jobs globally in the fisheries value chain are disproportionately held by the rural poor in developing countries. In Indonesia, fishery income-mangrove elasticity is estimated at 5.3 percent-9.8 percent per 1 percent of mangrove area lost. Protecting mangroves is valued at $12,364-$22,861 per hectare annually, compared with only $7,610 for the aquaculture that often replaces the mangroves (Yamamoto 2023). Pastoralists in degraded savannas find less forage for their

-----

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

herds, accelerating asset losses. Forest-dependent communities lose nontimber forest products, freshwater regulation, and the natural buffering against floods and droughts that intact ecosystems provide. A second path is the role of ecosystems as a buffer against poverty during climate shocks. Analysis across 23 countries finds that rural households compensate for declining incomes during drought years by increasing the harvesting of wood and other forest products from nearby forests by 30 percent (Noack et al. 2019). In healthy, intact forests, this response can be sustainable, provided that the ecosystem has sufficient time to recover between climate shocks, preserving the buffer for future use. More biodiverse forests can also fully offset the reduction in crop income caused by a severe drought. A recent analysis finds that the most diverse forests can provide food and materials to compensate fully for income lost from a bad drought year. If forests degrade or disappear, the informal insurance they provide disappears with them, rendering households more vulnerable to poverty transitions in bad weather years. The record forest loss documented in 2024 is therefore simultaneously a biodiversity concern, a GHG emissions concern, and evidence of the gradual weakening of the informal safety net on which hundreds of millions of the world's poorest depend. A third path connects ecosystems directly to food security and WASH indicators. Healthy ecosystems underpin agricultural productivity through pollination, soil fertility, water regulation, and pest control. While fertilizers may partially substitute for ecosystem-provided soil services, overuse imposes costs: Excessive fertilizer application causes crop damage and ecosystem degradation that is estimated at up to $3.4 trillion annually worldwide (Damania et al. 2025). This tension between the agricultural intensification needed to produce more food on less land and the ecosystem damage that intensification can cause is central to the relationship between ecosystems and food insecurity indicators. Sectors exhibiting a smaller pollution footprint, such as forestry, renewable energy, and sustainable agriculture, generate more jobs per dollar invested than polluting sectors (Damania et al. 2025). In many developing economies, protecting and restoring key ecosystems can support the job-rich, resilient growth on which durable poverty reduction depends.

## Food and nutrition insecurity

### A mixed picture: Marginal improvement from a troubling peak

Currently, 26.8 percent of the global population is facing moderate to severe food and nutritional insecurity.1 This share is measured as the percentage of people worldwide who are living in a household in which at least one adult is exposed to a low-quality diet or is forced to reduce the quantity of normal consumption during a year because of a lack of resources. The share has remained essentially unchanged. An estimated 28.0 percent of the global population, or 2.3 billion people, experienced moderate or severe food insecurity in 2024, a marginal decline from the 28.4 percent in 2023. This is 335 million more people than in 2019, before

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 the COVID-19 pandemic, and 683 million more than in 2015, when the United Nations Sustainable Development Agenda was adopted. An estimated 7.8 percent of the world's population suffered from hunger in 2025, a modest decline relative to 2024 and 2023, when the shares were 8.1 percent and 8.6 percent, respectively. This is an important improvement, but it is fragile. The most recent estimates are above the prepandemic levels. The high food inflation of recent years has contributed to the slow recovery. According to projections, 510 million to 520 million people could still be chronically undernourished in 2030, of whom nearly 60 percent will be living in Africa. The regional background behind these global aggregates is deeply uneven (refer to figure 3.9). Notable improvements in terms of a decline in food insecurity are evident in South Asia and in Latin America and the Caribbean. Africa and MENAAP have not recovered from the COVID-19 shock. An indicator that is complimentary to the food and nutrition insecurity measure is the prevalence of undernourishment. Here, some achievements have been impressive. In South Asia, undernourishment fell from 19.5 percent to 10.6 percent between 2004-06 and 2023-25. This was a decline of nearly 100 million people. In Latin America and the Caribbean, the
