prevalence of undernourishment dropped from 8.5 percent to 5.1 percent over the same period, a decline of more than 13 million people. Whereas in Sub-Saharan Africa, the prevalence of undernourishment was stubborn, only slightly increasing, from 22.1 percent in 2004-06 to 22.3 percent in 2023-25, the highest rate of all regions. But with high population growth, stagnant prevalence translates into many more malnourished people: from 165.9 million to 277.4 million over the period (refer to chapter 1). Food and nutrition insecurity are revealed in the poor physical growth of children. Stunting, which captures chronic malnutrition among children, imposes lasting costs on human capital through its effects on cognitive development, educational attainment, and physical capacity for work, and it is associated with substantially reduced earnings in adulthood (Galasso and Wagstaff 2019). As of 2024, 150.2 million children under age 5 were stunted. This is a prevalence of 23.2 percent, down from 180.4 million children (26.4 percent) in 2012. Nonetheless, the world is not on track to meet the 2030 target of a 40 percent reduction to 108 million children under age 5 who are stunted. The number of children affected by wasting and underweight (a subset of those stunted) is largely unchanged since 2012, at 6.6 percent and 5.5 percent, respectively. These nutritional outcomes connect directly to the WASH indicator. A child can consume adequate calories yet still be stunted if chronic exposure to fecal contamination triggers repeated diarrheal episodes that impair nutrient absorption.

### The three drivers of food crises: Conflict, climate extremes, and macroeconomic shocks

Conflict is the dominant driver of acute food crises. The prevalence of acute food insecurity has remained persistently high for five consecutive years, fluctuating between 21 percent and 23 percent across the 47 countries and territories experiencing food crises (FAO and WFP 2026). The vast majority (80 percent) of the population living in acute food insecurity reside in economies with protracted crises, a total of 33 countries and territories. This underscores the difficulty of escaping acute food insecurity. Climate extremes, including droughts, floods, and heat waves, are the second major driver, particularly in Sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia, where smallholder agriculture is dependent on rain. The exposure to climate hazards also affects jobs and income generation. A large share of the extreme poor among workers depend on agriculture. Their livelihoods are especially sensitive to climate variability and ecosystem degradation. The third major driver, macroeconomic shocks, including elevated food inflation, has undermined purchasing power and access to healthy diets, especially among low-income populations. FAO et al. (2026) estimate that 2.6 billion people could not afford a healthy diet in 2024, and affordability was narrowing in Africa and many low-income economies even as the global number of those affected falls. By contrast, investments that raise agricultural productivity, improve irrigation and water security, and strengthen market access can simultaneously support food security, resilience, incomes, and employment.

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 The current macroeconomic shock environment has been compounded by the conflict in the Middle East that began in early 2026, creating short-term risks and a projected rise in poverty (refer to chapter 1, box 1.2). The World Food Programme estimates that the conflict could potentially push 45 million additional people into acute hunger by mid-2026 (WFP 2026).

## Water, sanitation, and hygiene

This indicator tracks household access to WASH across three service levels, ranging from basic access to the higher standard of safely managed services. Access to basic drinking water signifies access to water from an improved source within a 30-minute round trip. Basic sanitation indicates that improved facilities are not shared with other households. Basic hygiene refers to the existence of a handwashing facility with soap and water at home. Currently, 91.4 percent of the global population has basic access to drinking water, 82 percent have basic access to sanitation, and 78 percent have basic access to hygiene. The gains in the past two decades have been substantial, reflecting sustained investment and policy effort, but they are a low bar in consideration of the importance of WASH to development (refer to figure 3.10).

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

Access to drinking water can be measured on a ladder of services. At the lowest rung, 9 percent of the global population lacks even basic access. These people cannot reach a structurally protected source, such as a piped supply, borehole, or protected well, within a 30-minute round trip. Among this population, 106 million people drink directly from untreated surface sources, such as rivers, lakes, and ponds. A much larger share-roughly one in four people, or 2.1 billion-lacks safely managed drinking water, a higher standard requiring that water be clean, available on premises, and reliably accessible if needed. Between 2015 and 2024, 961 million people gained access to safely managed drinking water, raising global coverage from 68 percent to 74 percent.2 This progress is far from sufficient. On sanitation, 1.2 billion people gained access to safely managed services over the same period, lifting coverage from 48 percent to 58 percent. Open defecation is practiced by 429 million fewer people and has been eliminated in urban areas. Yet, 3.4 billion people still lack safely managed sanitation, including 354 million who continue the practice. On hygiene, 1.6 billion people gained access to basic services between 2015 and 2024, and global coverage rose from 66 percent to 80 percent. Nonetheless, 1.7 billion people are still without basic hygiene at home, including 611 million with no facility of any kind (JMP 2025). Across the 94 countries with comparable data, water quality, measured by the absence of contamination, is the most common factor limiting access to safely managed drinking water, acting as the primary barrier in slightly more than half of all countries. Accessibility (whether water is available on-premises) and availability (whether the supply is reliable when needed) each constrain progress in roughly one quarter of countries. Progress in water quality has lagged behind the other two components. Infrastructure investments could expand physical access and improve reliability in relatively discrete steps. Ensuring that water is safe to drink requires ongoing pollution management, continuous monitoring, and sustained institutional capacity. This implies that quality improvements are slower, costlier, and more complex relative to the expansion of coverage alone.

### Where the gaps are largest

The inequalities in WASH access are stark and systematic. People are more than twice as likely to lack basic drinking water and sanitation and more than three times as likely to lack basic hygiene in low-income economies than elsewhere. Safely managed drinking water coverage is 38 percentage points lower in fragile settings than in other contexts. Despite overall progress since 2000, access to safely managed drinking water remains deeply unequal between urban and rural populations across all regions. The divide is stark. Among urban residents, 83 percent had access in 2024 compared with only 60 percent among rural populations, leaving roughly one in four rural people worldwide without safe water (refer to figure 3.11). The inequality is most severe in Sub-Saharan Africa, where only 17 percent of rural residents have access, against 53 percent of urban residents, a gap of 36 percentage points and the lowest rural figure globally. Wide gaps also persist in Latin America and the Caribbean and

There, the gap is nearly closed as urban and rural populations both approach universal access. The data make clear that location is one of the most powerful determinants of access to safe drinking water. Rural communities are consistently and substantially left behind.

### The poverty-WASH nexus

WASH deprivation affects efforts in poverty reduction through several simultaneous pathways. Waterborne illness reduces labor productivity and school attendance. Cognitive development in children is impaired during critical periods by exposure to contaminants, such as arsenic and lead. Women's economic participation is constrained by both the time cost of water collection and the menstrual health consequences of inadequate sanitation facilities. Without adequate sanitation and hand hygiene, investments in water supply are systematically undermined. Contamination reenters the system at the household level, perpetuating cycles of disease even if infrastructure has evolved. Sanitation programs, in particular, remain chronically underfunded across many low- and middle-income economies, weakening the returns to broader water investments.

-----

T h e Livab le P lanet: Tracking Prog ress

## The view across all five indicators

Progress across the five indicators has been uneven, and some indicators are moving in the wrong direction. Access to WASH services continues to improve. There were measurable gains in safely managed water and sanitation coverage over 2015-24. However, large gaps persist in the poorest and most fragile economies. Food insecurity has stabilized, but at an elevated level, and the risk of reversal threatens. The prevalence of moderate or severe food insecurity has declined gradually since the pandemic-era peak, except in Africa. Key ecosystems are shrinking. In 2024, tropical primary forest loss reached the worst level on record. Fire is overtaking agriculture as the dominant driver for the first time and temporarily converting the land sector from a carbon sink to a carbon source. The share of people at high risk from climate-related hazards-approximately 1.5 billion-is the indicator most directly responsive to progress in development. Climate hazards are becoming more dangerous because climate change is generating extreme weather events at a pace that is outstripping the rate of decline in vulnerability. GHG emissions are at record levels and rendering global temperature goals more difficult to reach, even as declining emissions in a growing number of economies demonstrate that progress is possible. One of the most important findings that emerges from an examination of the trends in all five indicators is that the populations most affected by each one are often in the same regions. The poorest people and the poorest economies did not cause global emissions to reach record levels, but they are affected the most by the climate hazards that are being intensified by the emissions. Their economic health is dependent on the ecosystems that are disappearing at a record clip. They are the most likely to go hungry if harvests fail or food prices spike. They are the least likely to have safe water and sanitation to protect their children from illness and support the human capital accumulation on which sustained poverty reduction depends. Investments in climate resilience are therefore also investments in poverty reduction.

### Monitoring as a measure of the conditions for poverty reduction

Across all five indicators, data gaps are largest precisely where attention is most needed: in lowincome and fragile and conflict-affected situation economies and in rural areas. Ecosystem monitoring captures area, but not quality. A hectare of degraded secondary forest is not equivalent to a hectare of intact primary forest in terms of carbon storage, biodiversity value, or the ecosystem services communities depend on. Food and nutrition monitoring captures caloric access better than nutritional quality, though expanding indicators on the cost and affordability of healthy diets are beginning to close that gap. WASH monitoring has strengthened substantially. The report of the World Health Organization-United Nations Children's Fund Joint Monitoring Programme for Water Supply and Sanitation (JMP 2025) now covers 160 economies, up from 95 in 2015. However, the safely managed tier remains the most difficult to measure in the lowestincome settings. The climate hazard risk indicator requires spatially resolved hazard data be

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 combined with household microdata on vulnerability. This is a technically demanding exercise that depends on national survey infrastructure precisely where the risk is greatest. Geospatial, satellite, administrative, and other digital data can help fill some of these gaps, including by improving hazard monitoring and early warning where household survey data are limited. These measurement gaps matter in policy. Monitoring the five indicators is not a technical exercise detached from the poverty and prosperity agenda. It is a measure of the conditions under which poverty reduction must occur. A livable planet is not a parallel goal alongside ending poverty. The livable planet must be maintained to achieve and sustain poverty reduction.

## Notes

1. The statistics in this section are drawn from FAO et al. (2026).
2. Safely managed drinking water refers to drinking water from an improved source that is accessible

on the premises, available if needed, and free from fecal and priority chemical contamination. Basic drinking water access refers to drinking water from an improved source, provided that the total time

required to collect the water-including queuing and the round trip-does not exceed 30 minutes.

# 4

## Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires

## More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

## Summary

- *Labor income accounts for the large majority of the household income of the poor. Where* *the share can be measured directly, it represents close to two-thirds of the household income* *among the poorest 40 percent of the welfare distribution. Human capital is the main asset of the* *poor, and, for many, the only asset. The routes into acquiring other assets, physical or human,* *are largely closed to the poor. Poverty reduction therefore runs through the labor market.* *This occurs along two margins: how much the poor work and how much they earn when* *they work.*
- *Rising earnings per worker have greatly contributed to poverty reduction. In 2003-24, growth* *in earnings per worker accounted for almost all welfare growth (that is, growth in consumption* *or income per capita) across every quintile. Increases in employment contributed little, and,* *among the poorest 60 percent, declining employment rates lowered per capita welfare growth.* *Efforts to raise employment remain crucial nonetheless because, for a household in which the* *only asset is labor, the employment of more labor is an opportunity to reduce poverty. More jobs* *are the first path to reducing poverty.*
- *The poor work almost as much as the nonpoor, but poor households generally have more* *dependents relative to nonpoor households. Employment rates among the poor cluster between* *58 percent and 61 percent of working-age adults, against about 67 percent among the nonpoor.* *The gap is small or absent in poorer economies and wide in richer economies. Poor workers* *also work fewer hours than nonpoor workers in most countries among wage workers and the* *self-employed alike, a pattern that points to underemployment rather than to a preference for*

*shorter hours. Each poor worker supports roughly three times as many dependents as a worker at the top of the distribution. So, similar employment rates translate into far lower labor income per household member among the poor.*

- *Barriers to employment among the poor are visible and growing. Gender-specific constraints* *are a key barrier. Employment among women is lowest and the gender employment gap widest* *in the lower middle of the global welfare distribution. Employment among the poor has been* *declining. In 2003-24, it fell in 88 of138 economies and grew less or fell more among the poor* *than among the nonpoor in 99 of137 economies.*
- *Closing employment gaps in poor households would reduce poverty by around a fifth. Raising* *employment in poor households to 75 percent of working-age adults-the rate poor men* *already attain and the level of two decades ago-lowers the poverty rate from 10.8 percent to* *8.6 percent at the $3.00 line and from 51.3 percent to 42.0 percent at the $8.30 line.*
- *Better-paying jobs are the second path to reducing poverty. Poor people earn too little for three* *reasons: low human capital, concentration in activities with the lowest returns, and lower* *returns for the same work. At the $3.00 line, 66 percent of poor workers are in agriculture, and* *79 percent in self-employment. Raising educational attainment among the poor to the nonpoor* *average would cut the extreme poverty rate by 45 percent; moving the poor into wage work, by* *21 percent; and out of agriculture, by 18 percent.*
- *More jobs and better-paying jobs reach different poor people. Higher earnings do most for* *the poorest, most of whom work, but earn little. Higher employment rates do most for the* *near-poor in the lower middle of the distribution, where many adults in poor households-* *particularly women-do not work. Progress on both margins is needed.*
- *Panel evidence on 41 economies shows that the same change in a household's employment* *does not pay off equally everywhere. If the share of a household's working-age members in* *wage employment rises by 10 percentage points, the household's chances of escaping poverty* *improve by more than 2.0 percentage points in richer economies, but by only 0.8 points in the* *poorest economies, where self-employment yields a return close to that of wage work. Self-* *employment also reduces the risk of falling into poverty in rich and poor economies alike.* *So, the work that protects a household is not always the work that raises the household out* *of poverty.*

## Introduction

Human capital is the main asset of the poor, and, among many of the poor, it is the only asset. Households at the bottom of the welfare distribution hold little land, capital, or savings. What they can draw on is their capacity to work and the human capital embodied in work (Bandiera et al. 2022; World Bank 1990). Whether such a household escapes poverty depends on whether its members work and enjoy decent earnings. Creating enough good

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

jobs is a complex challenge in every economy, and making the jobs agenda inclusive of the poor is more difficult still. The poor often live in rural or remote areas, bring less education and fewer skills to the labor market, and work in a narrow range of low-return activities.1 The barriers between the poor and a decent income from work are higher than those the nonpoor face. If the goal is to maximize the contribution to poverty reduction of policies that create jobs and raise what the jobs pay, two questions must be answered. The first is the employment question: whether the poor work and how much they work. The second is the earnings question: how much the poor earn for the work they do. These questions correspond to the two channels through which labor reduces poverty: more jobs and better-paying jobs. The central message of the chapter is that progress against poverty requires both. Raising employment among the poor would substantially reduce poverty, and the chapter quantifies by how much, but a large share of the poor already work, and, for these people, what matters is how little they earn. Each channel leads to a solution of only part of the problem. The chapter addresses these two questions in turn. On employment, it shows that the poor work nearly as much as the nonpoor, that their employment rates have nonetheless been declining over the past two decades, that specific barriers hold the rates down, and that narrowing the resulting gap to a realistic benchmark would remove roughly a fifth of measured poverty at both the $3.00 and the $8.30 poverty lines (in 2021 purchasing power parity terms). Notwithstanding this potential, growth in employment has contributed far less than growth in earnings per worker to the poverty reduction of the past two decades. On earnings, the chapter asks why poor people earn so little when they work. It identifies three reasons: their low human capital; their concentration in activities with the lowest returns, such as agriculture and nonfarm self-employment; and the lower returns to the same work in poorer countries. It estimates by how much poverty would fall if each barrier were relaxed. Even where the poverty rate does not fall, such gains matter. The chapter shows that higher labor income, whether from more employment or higher earnings, considerably reduces the depth of poverty. Two features define the scope of the analysis. First, the evidence is drawn from household surveys that report how much households earn and spend, but present little information on the firms that might employ household members. The chapter therefore examines the labor market from the household perspective: who works, at what, and for what return. The quantities and earnings the surveys record are realized outcomes, the joint result of the labor households supply and the demand for this labor, but they describe neither side of the market individually or thoroughly. The chapter does not model the decisions that generate the supply and demand: how household members are allocated between paid work and other uses of their time or how firms decide to create jobs. The approach represents a choice of focus, not a judgment that either side is less important. Household surveys do capture some production units, but these are the microenterprises that are run by the households directly and that

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 rarely employ anyone beyond household members. They differ fundamentally from formal firms. A full account of how jobs are created would draw on firm-level data the chapter does not analyze.2 The focus on the poor is also a broader undertaking than may appear. Gauged by the higher of the chapter's two main poverty lines, $8.30-a-person-a-day, the poor are about half the world population. The second feature that defines the scope of the analysis is measurement, which is a primary contribution of the chapter. Within the above confines, the chapter quantifies claims about the poor and their work that are commonly made in general terms: how much the poor work, how much less they earn if they work, and how much of the measured poverty is accounted for by each gap in work or in earnings. The measurement relies on household surveys harmonized so that a household's place in the welfare distribution and the labor-market status of its members can be observed together and in a consistent manner to be compared across more than 130 economies and followed over the two decades from 2003 to 2024: the kind of recurrent monitoring of the poor's circumstances that this report series was built to sustain. The chapter probes beyond the familiar observation that the poor are concentrated in agriculture and self-employment in three ways: global simulations of how far poverty would fall if employment or earnings among the poor rose to realistic benchmarks estimated across the developing world rather than in a single country, a within-country comparison of the hours the poor and the nonpoor work, and panel evidence on the labor market transitions that move households into and out of poverty.3 Each of these methods is brought to bear on the question the chapter sets out to answer: how to maximize the impact of the jobs agenda on poverty reduction.

## People earn most of their income from

### work, especially the poor, whose human capital is often their only asset

For most people, labor income is the most important component of income. This is especially true among the poor. Middle-class and richer households draw on a diversified portfolio as they earn rents from land and other physical and financial capital, in addition to income from their own work. The poor have little to fall back on beyond their own labor because labor is nearly the only productive asset the poor own. This observation is not new. As Fields (2011) puts it, the main and often the sole asset of the poor is their labor. Understanding poverty thus requires an understanding of labor markets. The policy corollary is equally well established. World Development Report 1990 identifies the first element of an antipoverty strategy as the effort "to promote the efficient use of the poor's most abundant asset: labor" (World Bank 1990, iii), and the framing has anchored the World Bank's approach to poverty ever since.

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

### Rising earnings per worker have greatly contributed to poverty reduction

Through which margin has labor delivered welfare growth among the poor: more household members in work or higher earnings for each worker? To answer this question, this chapter decomposes the growth in welfare per person over the last two decades into the parts attributable to three components: growth in welfare per worker, growth in the rate of employment among working-age adults, and growth in the share of the population that is of working age. Figure 4.1 reports the result. In every quintile, growth in earnings per worker-the dark blue component-accounts for almost all the growth in welfare per person between 2003 and 2024.4 The contribution of the changing share of the working-age population is small throughout.5 The contribution of employment is small as well, and, among the poor, it is negative. Across the bottom three quintiles, the employment term is subtracted from welfare growth. It turns positive only among the upper two quintiles. Earnings do not merely lead. Among the poorest households, they must more than compensate for an employment margin that is contracting.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

The employment decline that reduces welfare growth is documented country-by-country below. Employment rates among the poor fell in most of the world over this period, and they fell below the rates among the nonpoor.6 That the employment term is negative among the poor is not only an accounting fact. It represents a missed opportunity. Had employment among the poorer 60 percent simply held steady rather than contracting, welfare growth among these households would have been greater, and poverty would have been reduced more than it was. The historical record is therefore not evidence that raising employment is ineffective. The counterfactual constructed below shows the opposite, that is, closing the employment gap would reduce poverty substantially. The record shows instead that this potential has gone unrealized.

### Asset ownership is largely out of reach for poor households; so, efforts to increase income from work are crucial to poverty reduction

In low- and middle-income economies as in high-income economies, income from the poor's own work-wage labor, together with self- and farm employment-accounts for the large majority of the household income of the poor (Gasparini et al. 2011; Leibbrandt et al. 2010; Piketty 2014). The evidence is derived from two distinct literatures. The first measures income shares directly in settings where labor and capital income are separately recorded: harmonized household surveys in Latin America (Gasparini et al. 2011), comparable series in South Africa (Leibbrandt et al. 2010), and tax-based decompositions in rich economies, where the bottom half of the distribution earns income almost entirely from labor (Piketty 2014). In these data, capital income is a measured category and is close to zero at the bottom of the income distribution. Across 18 Latin American countries, it averages under 3 percent of total income, and the richest quintile accounts for roughly four-fifths of what little is recorded (Gasparini et al. 2011). In South Africa, it contributes about 1 percent of income in the poorest decile (Leibbrandt et al. 2010). The harmonized surveys assembled in the World Bank's Global Monitoring Database (GMD) permit the same direct decomposition.7 In Latin America and the Caribbean, the only region in which the GMD currently supports the labor-capital split, labor income accounts for close to two-thirds of total income among the poorest 40 percent of the distribution. This figure is a lower bound on the labor share because pensions (and possibly remittances) are classified as nonlabor income in the underlying data. Pensions are more naturally understood as deferred labor income, reflecting past contributions from work. Treating them as such would raise the measured share. The second literature decomposes rural incomes into farm and nonfarm components (Banerjee and Duflo 2007; Davis et al. 2010, 2017; Haggblade et al. 2010). Here, capital income does not appear as a separate category. Across 41 surveys in Sub-Saharan Africa, onfarm activity alone supplies about half the income of the poorest quintile, and nonfarm wage employment only

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

8 percent (Davis et al. 2017). In rural India, the lowest quintile draws more than a quarter of its income from agricultural wage labor, the lowest return form of work, a share that falls to 2 percent at the top (Haggblade et al. 2010). The sectoral focus of this literature is relevant because most of the poor are working in agriculture, but sector of work is not the same as source of income. Farm income is mixed income. It is the joint return to household labor, land, tools, livestock, and seeds. Household surveys in agrarian settings cannot separate the labor return from the capital return because wages for family farm labor and the rental rate for family-owned land or equipment are difficult to measure or estimate. This literature therefore does not yield a single clean quantitative labor income share for the poor. But, among poor households, the capital component embedded in farm income is small because their holdings of land, tools, and savings are minimal (Banerjee and Duflo 2007)-not literally zero, but minor. In sum, even though the decomposition cannot be made precisely, farm income among the poor is mostly a return to labor. Poor households do not have diversified income sources because the routes into asset ownership are largely closed to them and because human capital is difficult to build. The first obstacle is the cost of acquiring productive assets in the presence of imperfect credit markets. Investing in physical or human capital typically requires an outlay that must be financed up front. Where credit markets are imperfect, borrowing to finance this investment requires collateral the poor do not have. Households with wealth that falls below the threshold at which investment becomes feasible therefore cannot invest and remain confined to supplying labor. The resulting distribution of who owns capital and who supplies labor reproduces itself across generations (Carter and Barrett 2006; Galor and Zeira 1993). Empirical evidence bears this out. Wealth is far more concentrated than income. Land, in particular, is distributed more unequally than income in nearly every country in which both can be measured. So, the assets that generate nonlabor income are precisely those the poor are least well positioned to accumulate. The limited credit that reaches poor households is thus often rationed at high cost (World Bank 2005).8 The second obstacle concerns the poor's single asset. Human capital is difficult for poor households to accumulate, and the disadvantage is transmitted from parents to children. Intergenerational mobility in education-the degree to which children's schooling can exceed the schooling of their parents-is markedly lower in developing economies than in high-income economies, and, within the developing world, the prospects of upward movement from the bottom of the distribution have narrowed among more recent cohorts (Narayan et al. 2018). The mechanisms operate early and cumulatively. Steep wealth gradients in schooling persist across the developing world (Filmer and Pritchett 1999). More than half the children in low- and middle-income economies cannot read and understand a simple text by age 10 (World Bank 2018). Childhood stunting-concentrated among the poor-permanently impairs cognitive development and adult earnings (Victora et al. 2008), and gaps in skills open up before school begins and are compounded with age (Heckman 2006). The result is a self-reinforcing cycle:

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 Poverty erodes the human capital that is the poor's principal asset, which depresses what their labor can earn, which then perpetuates the poverty that began the sequence. Because the routes to other assets are closed and human capital is slow to build, the welfare of poor households shifts with their labor income alone. Efforts to increase employment are crucial for exactly this reason. For a household in which the only asset is labor, employing more labor is a direct route out of poverty.

### Employment rates are slightly lower among the poor than among the nonpoor, and poor households show higher dependency ratios

This subsection takes up the first of the chapter's two major questions, how much the poor work. The answer has two parts: whether working-age adults are working at all and, among those working, how many hours they work. The poor fall slightly short of the nonpoor on both counts. Across the global welfare distribution, employment rates among the poor are only modestly below the rates among the nonpoor, and poor workers work somewhat fewer hours. The rest of the chapter turns to the second major question: the type of work the poor do and the earnings from this work. Among the poor, the share of working-age adults in employment clusters between roughly 58 percent and 61 percent regardless of how poor they are. The share reaches about 67 percent only among the nonpoor-a gap of some 6 percentage points (refer to figure 4.2, panel a). The pattern holds across the entire distribution. Figure 4.2, panel b, traces employment rates by global welfare percentile in 2024. The rates rise from the bottom of the distribution to the top, but the increase is small compared with the enormous differences in welfare between the poorest and the richest percentiles.9 What matters for household welfare is not only the share of adults who work, but how many people each worker must support. On this measure, the poor and the nonpoor diverge sharply. Figure 4.2, panel b, plots the total dependency ratio: children ages under 15 and adults ages more than 65 per 100 working-age adults across the global welfare distribution. The ratio falls steeply as welfare rises, from about 110 at the poorest percentiles to about 38 at the top. A working-age adult near the bottom supports roughly three times as many dependents as one near the top. This dependency gradient reflects the demography of poverty (refer to chapter 1). Poor families have more children both because higher-fertility households are more likely to be poor and because low-income countries are at an earlier stage of the demographic transition, where the share of children is structurally high. Similar rates of employment among adults therefore translate into different labor income per household member. So, the 6-point participation gap understates the disadvantage the poor face in converting work into welfare for the entire household.

-----

The near-parity of the employment rate between the poor and the nonpoor is a global average. Grouping countries by income level yields a simple typology of the employment gap. At the $3.00 line, the poor work about as much as the nonpoor in low-income economies (67.7 percent against 68.1 percent) and slightly more in lower-middle-income economies (59.2 percent against 57.9 percent). The gap widens with country income. Employment among the extreme poor falls to 36.5 percent in upper-middle-income economies and to 26.5 percent in high-income economies, against roughly 66 percent and 70 percent among the nonpoor (refer to figure 4.3). The counterpart is inactivity rather than unemployment. The share of people out of the labor force among the poor rises from about 30 percent in low-income economies to 65 percent in high-income economies. A plausible hypothesis is that, where social protection is absent, the poor must work to survive, whereas safety nets and higher reservation wages in richer economies make nonparticipation feasible. Two cautions apply. First, the extreme poor in high-income economies are a small and selected group. So, their measured employment rate is based on few observations. Second, the relationship is descriptive, not causal. With these caveats, the pattern is clear. The employment gap between the poor and the nonpoor is small or absent in poorer countries and wide in richer countries.

## Barriers to employment among the poor

### are visible and have been growing over the last two decades

While joblessness is not an obstacle specific to the poor, this section shows that the picture is more complex. The poor face distinct or exacerbated barriers to finding jobs, and their employment rate has expanded less or declined more relative to the rate among the nonpoor. Indeed, increasing the rates at which the poor are working would reduce poverty considerably.

### Gender-specific constraints are a key barrier to more employment among the poor

The labor market participation patterns described above differ by gender (refer to figure 4.4). Male employment rates are high and relatively flat across most of the global welfare distribution, rising only in the top percentiles. Female employment rates across countries trace a U-shape. Relatively high at the lower income levels, the rates fall to their lowest levels across the lower middle of the distribution and rise again across roughly the upper half. The gender gap that separates the two profiles is widest in the lower middle of the distribution-around the 20th to 40th percentiles-and narrows at both ends, closing partially among the poorest and converging toward parity among the richest.

*Sources: Calculations based on Bussolo, Lakner, et al. 2026; Global Monitoring Database (version September 2026),* internal database, World Bank. *Note: The figure plots the share of men and women in work within each percentile of the global welfare* distribution in 2024. Working-age population is defined as ages 15-64. Vertical dashed lines denote the $3.00- and $8.30-a-person-a-day poverty lines (in 2021 purchasing power parity terms).

This shape is well established in the literature on female labor force participation and economic development (Boserup 1970; Durand 1975; Goldin 1994). The theory is that, at low incomes, female participation is relatively high because the need to secure subsistence outweighs the low returns to women's work and because of the dominance of subsistence agriculture, which has low barriers to entry. The theory posits that, as incomes rise, the income effect dominates and participation falls because women's wages at this higher level are too low to pull women into the labor force. At higher incomes, the substitution effect dominates as rising wages and education draw women back into employment. However, the relationship is correlational rather than causal. Many countries, particularly in the Middle East and South Asia, sit well below the curve and do not demonstrate a U-pattern in the country development path over time (Gaddis and Klasen 2014). Moreover, trends in female labor force participation rates across regions are inconsistent with the U-shape suggested by the cross-sectional pattern (Klasen 2019). The gender employment gap reflects constraints that fall specifically on women and interact with the conditions of poverty. Available and affordable childcare, safe and affordable transport, and prevailing social norms about women's roles all shape whether women in poorer households can take up any employment and, if they do, the type of employment

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 they can pursue. These constraints bind women differently along the distribution relative to men (Bussolo et al. 2024; Field and Vyborny 2022; Jayachandran 2021). They are visible in a snapshot of who is working today. The next subsection turns to the record over time, in which the barriers to employment show up as a two decades-long decline in the share of the poor who work.

### Employment rates among the poor have been declining

Over the last two decades, employment rates among the poor have fallen in most of the world, and they have fallen more quickly-or risen more slowly-among the poor than among the nonpoor in the same countries. Figure 4.5 sets out the evidence country-by-country. Panel a plots the employment rate of the $8.30-a-person-a-day poor in each economy in 2024 against the corresponding level in 2003. The points below the 45-degree line are countries in which employment among the poor declined. The majority of the points lie below the line, indicating the employment among the $8.30-a-person-a-day poor fell between 2003 and 2024 in 88 of 138 economies. Panel b compares the annualized change in employment among the poor with the change among the nonpoor in the same economy. The majority lies below the 45-degree line in 99 of 137 economies, indicating that employment rates expanded less or contracted more among the poor than among the nonpoor. The decline does not reflect whether young people stay longer in school. Splitting the working-age population into a 15-25 group and a 25-64 group shows that the two-decade fall in employment is larger among the younger group, consistent with rising school enrollment-a welcome development. But a decline of about the same magnitude is also present among the 25-64 group. So, longer schooling does not account for the decline (refer to annex 4F). Several mechanisms may explain this decline, none of which is explicitly tested here. One explanation is compositional. More and better work will reduce poverty, and, so, as poverty falls, as observed in this time period, there may be a compositional effect driving the decline in employment rates among the poor. Thus, in later years, one could expect that households that are in poverty are more entrenched in terms of the lack of access to work. The pattern of employment across the distribution in 2024 does not offer support to a compositional effect driven by two decades of poverty reduction. Employment is slightly higher among the extreme poor, at 60.8 percent, than among households between the extreme poverty line and $4.20-a-person-a-day (58.3 percent) (refer to figure 4.2, panel a). The uneven pace of structural transformation is a second mechanism. As agriculture's share in employment falls, the nonagricultural sector often creates too few jobs to absorb the workers leaving agriculture. Across emerging and developing economies, more rapid

Upper-middle income High income productivity growth has, on average, accompanied a falling rather than a rising employmentto-population ratio. The decline is steepest early in development, when agriculture's employment share is still high (Ohnsorge et al. 2026; Rodrik 2016). Because the poor are concentrated in agriculture, they are the first to be affected if the frequency with which labor leaves agriculture outpaces the ability of other sectors to absorb the labor. A third, more tentative channel is technological. Skill-biased technical change and automation may lower the relative demand for less well educated workers, though, in developing countries, the evidence bears more on the widening skill premium than on the levels of employment.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 Fourth, the gender-specific constraints taken up in the previous subsection bind most tightly among the poor. The same demand-side shortfall underlies the parallel decline in women's participation because capital-intensive services create few jobs for women leaving a contracting agricultural sector (Deshpande and Singh 2024). These mechanisms are not mutually exclusive. The country-by-country changes illustrated in figure 4.5, panel b, also indicate why the potential of the employment margin has been difficult to realize. The changes cluster near zero among the poor and the nonpoor alike. So, in most economies, employment rates shifted within a narrow band over two decades.

### Closing employment gaps among poor households would reduce poverty by around a fifth

The subsections above establish that employment among the poor has been falling and that specific, identifiable barriers-the gender gap prominent among them-hold it down. This raises a quantitative question: How much would poverty decline if these barriers were relaxed? This question is answered by simulating the poverty effects of a rise in employment among poor households to a realistic benchmark. The benchmark is an employment rate of 75 percent among working-age adults in poor households. This is achieved by assigning currently nonworking adults to work-each at the household's own observed welfare per worker-until the household reaches the rate. This is purely an accounting exercise. It does not consider general equilibrium effects. So, wages do not change, and no existing worker is displaced. A 75 percent target is not arbitrary. Three independent considerations determine this target. It is approximately the level that would restore the employment the poor have lost over the last two decades. It is also the rate at which men in poor households already work. So, reaching it amounts to closing the within-household gender gap. It is likewise the employment rate prevailing among the top quintile of the global distribution. Restoration, gender equalization, and convergence to richer households thus point to the same benchmark, and the increment it implies-about 15 percentage points above the current rate of roughly 60 percent-is consistent with levels that have actually occurred.10 Closing the gap to this benchmark significantly reduces poverty at both international poverty lines (refer to figure 4.6). At the $3.00 line, the poverty rate falls from 10.8 percent to 8.6 percent, a reduction of about a fifth. At the $8.30 line, it falls from 51.3 percent to 42.0 percent, also about a fifth. The poverty gap and the percent poverty gap fall by similar or somewhat larger proportions at each line. The percent poverty gap is the poverty rate, multiplied by the average percent increase in income

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs a poor person requires to reach the poverty line (refer to annex 4B). These are large effects for a single margin. They confirm that the employment deficit among the poor is consequential. Shifting participation to a level the poor themselves attain among men and attained more broadly two decades ago would lift a substantial share of households out of poverty.

Observed Counterfactual *Sources: Calculations based on Bussolo, Lakner, et al. 2026; Global Monitoring Database (version September 2026),* internal database, World Bank. *Note: In panels a and b, the counterfactual assigns nonworking adults (ages 20-64) in poor households to work,* each at the household's observed welfare per worker, up to a household employment rate of 75 percent (in a genderblind fashion). The observed bars show the baseline, and the counterfactual bars show the simulated outcome in the poverty rate, poverty gap, and percent poverty gap. Poverty lines are $3.00- and $8.30-a-person-a-day (in 2021 purchasing power parity terms). The exercise holds wages, sector composition, and the number of existing workers fixed. Panels c to f apply the same benchmark to women and to men separately. Nonworking women (men) are assigned to work, each at the household's own observed welfare per worker, until women's (men's) employment in the household reaches 75 percent.

The aggregate reduction is shared across countries, though unevenly. Applied country-bycountry, the counterfactual lowers the poverty rate everywhere, but the size of the reduction varies systematically with how poor the country is to begin with (refer to annex 4C). The largest poverty reductions-on the order of 20 to 25 percentage points-occur in countries at moderate observed poverty levels. The reductions are smaller in countries in which poverty is already low because few households are poor to begin with, so few can be lifted across the line. They are smaller again in countries in which poverty is deep because most poor households sit far below the line, and welfare per worker is often too low for an added worker to carry a household across the line. This pattern is specific to the poverty rate, which registers only households that cross the poverty line. The poverty gap and the percent poverty gap, which account for welfare gains among poor households whether or not the households cross the line, fall roughly in proportion to the observed levels of the gaps across the full range of countries, including the poorest.

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

One feature of the result warrants comment. The simulation reduces poverty more at the $8.30 line than at the $3.00 line. The reason depends on the sorts of households each version reaches. The $8.30 simulation additionally mobilizes workers in households between the two lines, where a larger share of working-age adults is currently not working. Much of this capacity is represented by women. As the above subsection on gender-specific barriers shows, women's employment is lowest across the lower middle of the distribution. So, it is there that raising household employment to the benchmark draws in the largest number of new workers. Two variants of the simulation apply the benchmark to women and to men separately and show how much of the result resides in women's employment. One assigns nonworking women to work until 75 percent of working-age women in the household are employed; the other does the same for men, each on the terms of the household-level exercise (refer to figure 4.6, panels c-f). Raising women's employment to the benchmark lowers the $3.00 poverty rate from 10.8 percent to 8.5 percent and the $8.30 poverty rate from 51.3 percent to 40.8 percent; raising men's employment lowers the corresponding rates from 10.8 percent to 10.4 percent and from 51.3 percent to 49.8 percent. The poverty gap measures follow the same pattern. At the $3.00 line, the percent poverty gap falls from 9.7 percent to 7.8 percent under the women's variant and to 9.2 percent under the men's. The men's variant is small because most men in poor households already work at or above the benchmark. For the same reason, the women's variant adds more workers than the householdlevel exercise. In the latter, men's employment above the benchmark counts toward the household rate. So, fewer women need to be drawn in to reach the benchmark, whereas the women's variant raises women's employment to the benchmark regardless of how many men work.11 The two variants are therefore separate exercises rather than a split of the household-level result. Their effects do not sum to the household-level result, and the women's variant lowers poverty slightly more relative to the household-level exercise. The poverty reduction available through more work among the poor is thus, in practice, poverty reduction through more work among women. The exercise is a deliberately optimistic benchmark reached by assumption rather than by an analysis of the likely impacts of the policies that would tend to attain the benchmark. Even on these favorable terms, increases in employment alone reduce poverty by only a fifth. This effect is modest because the simulations assume that the additional workers earn the household's own observed welfare per worker.12 If these earnings are low, the impacts on household welfare are limited.

## Better-paying jobs represent the second

### path to poverty reduction

The employment counterfactual in the above section does more than measure what raising participation could achieve. By moving the quantity of work to a realistic upper bound, it isolates what employment cannot do. Even if nonworking adults are put to work, poverty falls by about a fifth, but it is not eradicated. The remaining poverty cannot be attributed to the poor

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 who are not working because, in the counterfactual, employment rates are at 75 percent. This turns the statement that the poor earn too little from a near-tautology to a finding. That the poor have low income is true by definition. That their poverty survives once their labor matches the quantity of labor richer households supply is not. The poverty that remains is the poverty more work does not remove. The earnings shortfall that remains is large. Dividing the percent poverty gap by the poverty rate recovers the average percent increase in income the poor require to climb to the poverty line. After the employment counterfactual, the percent poverty gap at the $3.00 line is 7.8 percent, and the poverty rate is 8.6 percent, implying that the average poor person would need to raise their earnings by about 91 percent to cross the line. At the $8.30 line, the corresponding rise is 133 percent.13 Even if75 percent of its working-age adults in every poor household is employed, escaping poverty would require the earnings of the poor to double at the lower line and more than double at the higher line. Among the poorest, almost none of the poverty that remains represents a problem of participation. As the above subsection on employment rates shows, the extremely poor already work at rates close to the rates among the nonpoor. So what holds them below the poverty line is the price of their labor (their earnings) rather than the quantity of their labor. Earnings can rise by two means: through a higher return to the work the poor already do or through a move into more productive activities. The rest of this section examines the barriers that stand in the way of such a rise. One qualification of the quantity-versus-price framing concerns the intensity of work, that is, the hours the poor supply, which the participation rate does not capture. Box 4.1 discusses this qualification in detail.

## BOX 4.1

###### Hours and underemployment: The intensive margin

The employment rate captures whether a person works, not how many hours the person works in a given period, that is, it captures the extensive margin, not the intensive margin. The empirical literature has approached the intensive margin in two ways. The first compares average hours across countries at different income levels. The cross-country evidence is contested. Bick et al. (2018), using a harmonized sample of about 50 economies, find that hours worked per adult fall in richer economies. Gethin and Saez (2026), drawing on a far larger set of economies representing most of the world's population, find, instead, that the cross-country relationship is close to flat. Their result is mildly bell-shaped. National income explains little of the variation in hours. The matter is unsettled. The second approach, more relevant here, compares hours within economies across the earnings distribution: whether poorer workers work more or fewer hours than richer workers in the same economy. Direct evidence is thin. Bick et al. (2018)

*(continued)*

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

###### BOX 4.1

**Hours and underemployment: The intensive margin (continued)**

appear to be the main source, but it points one way. They estimate an elasticity of hours worked with respect to the hourly wage of about -0.09. As the hourly wage falls, hours rise, a gradient strongest in the poorer economies and fading, sometimes reversing, in the richer economies. The intuition is that, where pay per hour is low, workers must work longer to reach a subsistence income. The Global Monitoring Database supports a direct comparison of hours by poverty status, country-by-country. Figure B4.1.1 plots mean weekly hours of poor against nonpoor workers at the $3.00 line, for wage workers (panel a) and for the self-employed (panel b). A point above the 45-degree line marks an economy in which the nonpoor work longer hours relative to the poor. In most countries, the poor work slightly fewer hours than the nonpoor in both panels. So, the deficit is not a by-product of the poor's concentration in selfemployment, and it is widest in the low-income economies (the points of which lie furthest from the line). The hours of the self-employed are more dispersed

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

###### BOX 4.1

**Hours and underemployment: The intensive margin (continued)**

than the wage hours-low in the poorest economies and high in some richer ones-because self-employment hours are also the most difficult to measure; part of the spread is noise. The patterns are similar at the $8.30 line. The gradient runs against the finding of Bick et al. (2018). The negative elasticity of hours to the hourly wage implies that the poorest, paid least per hour, work the longest hours, most of all in the poorest countries. The comparison by poverty status, in the present sample of economies, shows the reverse and, most clearly, where this elasticity should be strongest. Two considerations bear on the divergence. Poverty status is a household measure, shaped by the number of dependents and earners as much as by any one worker's wage. The comparison here, meanwhile, splits workers at a single poverty line rather than along the wage distribution across which Bick et al. (2018) estimate. So, a gradient in hours by poverty status need not follow the gradient by wage. The composition of the poor's work does not account for the divergence because the same shortfall appears among wage workers alone (refer to figure B4.1.1, panel a), the population on which the Bick et al. (2018) elasticity is estimated; but it does bear on how the shortfall should be read. The poor are concentrated in selfemployment and agriculture, where the hours available are limited by demand and by season and where hours per worker run about 10 percent below the hours in nonfarm activities (Gollin et al. 2014). Workers pressed to earn a subsistence income therefore cannot always find the additional hours. Fewer hours under this constraint point to underemployment more than to a preference for shorter hours. Time-related underemployment is the standard term for this phenomenon. The International Labour Organization (ILO 2013) defines it as the situation of people in work who want to work and who are available to work more hours than their employment provides. A worker content with fewer hours is not underemployed. The measurement is difficult and partly conceptual. How many hours a person usually works depends on the reference period and on the type of employment, including wage versus self-employment. The literature often treats self-employment as disguised unemployment. According to this view, low-productivity informal work is taken up because regular full-time wage employment is unavailable (Breza and Kaur 2025; Donovan et al. 2023). So, a low intensive margin reflects a structural inability to obtain regular, full-time work more than it reflects a preference for a specific hours count. This discussion also highlights that the distinction between employment as a quantity of work and earnings as the price of this work is clean in principle,

*(continued)*

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

###### BOX 4.1

**Hours and underemployment: The intensive margin (continued)**

but not in the data. The per worker earnings measure used throughout the chapter bundles the price of an hour with the number of hours worked. So, part of the poor's lower earnings according to this measure is the smaller number of hours the poor work, not merely the lower price they receive for the labor and the hours. The intensive margin therefore belongs to the employment side of the argument, too. Beyond increasing employment rates, raising the hours of those who already work, but who obtain fewer hours of work than the number they are willing and ready to supply, would also increase labor income and thus reduce poverty. The line between more jobs and better jobs is less clean under this perspective than first appears.

### Barriers that shape earnings among the poor: Low human capital, concentration in low returns activities, and low returns to work

Across much of the developing world, most work is self-employment, and wage jobs are scarce (Breza and Kaur 2025; World Bank 2012). The scarcity is part of the reason the work of the poor pays so little: it isolates them in lower-return self-employment. The literature on structural transformation describes how higher-return work is created as economies develop. Two elements of that account bear most directly on the poor. The first is productivity in agriculture, where most of the poor work. Higher yields raise the incomes of farming households directly, which makes agricultural productivity an important source of poverty reduction in its own right. Rising farm productivity also generates the surplus that allows households to diversify into work outside farming and sustains the growth of the rest of the economy. The second is the expansion of firms outside agriculture, which determines whether those who take up work outside farming are absorbed into higher-paying work or into the informal activities described above in the discussion of the employment decline (Cusolito and Maloney 2018). Cross-country evidence also relates the composition of growth to the pace of poverty reduction: a given rate of growth reduces poverty more quickly if it is accompanied by a movement of workers and earnings toward nonagriculture than if its sectoral composition is neutral (Bourguignon 2003; Bussolo, Brunckhorst, et al. 2026; Loayza and Raddatz 2010; Ravallion 2022). Finance constrains both processes through the growth of the firms that might hire the poor and through the working capital of the farms and enterprises most of the poor already run (de Mel et al. 2008; Didier and Cusolito 2024). The full transformation unfolds over decades rather than years. Neither process is observed in household surveys. The same is true of the market structures and demand-side frictions that shape the processes. This subsection considers instead what

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 the surveys do record by taking up two of the three barriers to higher earnings that the surveys reveal: low human capital and concentration in the activities with the lowest returns. The third barrier, the lower returns to the same work in poorer countries, is examined in the subsection below on labor market transitions in panel data. The first barrier reaches back to the self-reinforcing relationship described above: Poverty depresses the human capital the poor are able to apply to their work. This low human capital constrains what the work can earn, and the low earnings leave little of what is needed to build the human capital of the next generation.

#### Low human capital and concentration in low-return activities

Figure 4.7 quantifies the first two of the barriers, that is, the human capital the poor bring to the labor market and the concentration of the poor in low-return work. In each case, the poor are concentrated in the positions in which earnings are lowest. The gaps are present at both lines, but sharpest at the $3.00 line. At the $3.00 line, 61 percent of the poor have only primary education, versus 25 percent among the nonpoor; 79 percent are in self-employment rather than wage work, versus 35 percent; and 66 percent work in agriculture, versus 20 percent. At the $8.30 line, the corresponding gaps are smaller-43 percent versus 15 percent on primary education; 59 percent versus 24 percent on self-employment; and 43 percent versus 9 percent on agriculture-because the $8.30-a-person-a-day poor are a less deprived population, though the gaps remain substantial on every count.

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

Two points about this concentration emerge upon observation of the country-by-country results in annex 4D relative to the global aggregate shown in figure 4.7. First, the concentration is a within-country fact, not a by-product of the concentration of the poor in poor countries. In nearly every economy examined, poor workers, relative to the nonpoor, are less well educated, more often self-employed, and more often in agriculture. Second, the poor are concentrated in low-earning positions. Thus, welfare per worker rises with schooling, is higher in wage work than in self-employment, and is higher outside agriculture than in agriculture in virtually every economy, although the largest proportional gaps occur in the poorest economies. Yet, agriculture is not a single occupation. Farming households differ along two dimensions that the single category conceals: the share of their income that derives from the farm and the share of what they grow that they sell. Among small-scale farming households in Ethiopia, Nigeria, Tanzania, and Uganda that cultivate fewer than five hectares, about 58 percent draw a substantial share of their income from farming and sell less than half of what they produce. Another 14 percent farm principally for the market, and 25 percent earn most of their income outside the farm. The first group is the largest in each of the four countries and larger still at the bottom of the distribution: about 67 percent of households in the poorest quintile fall within it (Ouedraogo et al. 2026). The poor are concentrated not simply in agriculture, but in the part of agriculture least well connected to markets. The disadvantage is sharper among women, and it strikes twice. The subsection on gender-specific barriers shows that poor women work far less often than poor men. Among those who do work, the employment is more highly concentrated in the lowest return positions, more frequently in selfemployment, which includes operating a small business or working in a household enterprise, and in agriculture and, less often, in wage work (Lo Bue et al. 2022) (refer to annex 4D). A poor woman is thus less likely to work and, if she does work, is more likely to be in a job that pays the least. Low educational attainment, self-employment, and work in agriculture often overlap. The same lack of schooling that directly lowers earnings also sorts workers into self-employment and into agriculture. So, the poor tend to be disadvantaged on all three counts at once. This is the reason the concentration documented here is the starting point of the earnings argument, not the conclusion. The concentration establishes where the poor work, not the cost of each position. The self-employed earn less than wage workers (as proxied by welfare per worker), but they also differ in age, location, and other characteristics. The raw earnings gap between the two thus mixes the returns to and the worker composition of the type of work. Isolating the returns-comparing otherwise similar workers on either side of each divide-is the task of the next subsection.

#### Earnings in higher-return work and the resulting decline in poverty

The concentration documented above raises a quantitative question: How much more would the poor earn in higher-return work? Three counterfactual scenarios answer the question, one for each dimension along which the poor are concentrated. The first raises the schooling of the poor to the nonpoor average. The second moves the poor from self-employment into wage

work.14 The third moves them from agriculture into other sectors. Each is a switch, that is,

a counterfactual reassignment that predicts what a worker would earn on the destination terms,

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 while leaving the worker's characteristics unchanged. The methodology for constructing these counterfactuals is set out in annex 4D. This exercise does not assign a poor worker the average pay of wage workers, workers outside agriculture, or workers with more schooling. Each switch removes one of the barriers that separate poor workers from the nonpoor-less schooling, exclusion from wage work, and confinement to agriculture-and asks what this particular worker, with their own age, location, and other recorded characteristics, would earn once the barrier is lifted. The distinction matters because the two groups-the self-employed and wage workers-differ in composition. Relative to wage workers, the self-employed include many more young and less well educated workers. The raw difference between average earnings thus mixes the returns to the type of work with the characteristics of the people doing the work. Measured this way, raising the education of poor workers to the nonpoor average increases the predicted earnings of the poor workers by about 40 percent; the move from self-employment to wage work, by about 16 percent; and the move from agriculture to nonagriculture, by about 36 percent. These comparisons hold constant only what the surveys record. Workers also differ in ways surveys do not capture, such as in health, the quality of the schooling they have received, and the contacts that open doors to better-paying work. Where these unrecorded differences help explain who reaches such work in the first place, the estimated gains will overstate what removing the observed barriers alone would deliver. Applied across the population, each switch assigns every poor worker in the relevant group the earnings predicted according to their own profile, and poverty is recomputed (refer to figure 4.8). The exercise is partial equilibrium. The destination wages are held fixed, and no incumbent is displaced. The reassigned workers thus fill additional jobs rather than compete for existing jobs. The simulation has two versions. In the first, only poor people below the $3.00 line are treated, and the change in the poverty rate is evaluated at the same line. The second reflects the same operation for the $8.30 line. If poor workers below the $3.00 line are the treated group, raising education reduces the $3.00 poverty rate the most, by 45 percent (from 10.7 percent to 5.9 percent), and the poverty gap and percent poverty gap by 54 percent and 60 percent, respectively. The move from self-employment to wage work reduces the poverty rate by 21 percent, and the move out of agriculture, by 18 percent, with comparable reductions in the two gap measures. Education does more for two reasons. It reaches a broader group (that is, all poor workers below the nonpoor education level, not only those in one sending sector), and the premium is the largest of the three. Extending the treated group to poor workers below the $8.30 line leaves the ordering unchanged, but shrinks the poverty rate reductions (refer to figure 4.9). Education lowers the $8.30 poverty rate by 20 percent (from 52.9 percent to 42.2 percent); the move to wage work, by 10 percent; and the move out of agriculture, by 7 percent. The share of poor workers below the nonpoor education level in self-employment or in agriculture thins as the line rises, leaving fewer available for each switch. The gap measures still fall substantially in all three cases. The per worker gain does not weaken at the higher line, even if the poverty rate, which counts only line crossings, moves less.

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 That education delivers the largest reduction does not make education the most readily attainable, and the three switches differ in the horizon over which they could be realized. Raising the education of the poor is slow, and the returns arrive only as cohorts pass through school into work.15 Expanding wage or nonagricultural employment can, in principle, occur more quickly through growth in labor demand, though which sectors might absorb the poor and on what scale is far from straightforward. Moreover, the frictions that keep wage work scarce still apply (Breza and Kaur 2025; Donovan et al. 2023). The route with the shortest horizon, which is therefore particularly important in the short run, is one the switches do not cover: raising the returns to the work the poor already do, especially the yields in smallholder farming, which employs two-thirds of the extreme poor who work. This route does not require that cohorts pass through school or that wage employment expand, and the growth of agricultural productivity has historically reduced poverty among the poorest by more than growth elsewhere (Christiaensen et al. 2011). As farm households spend their higher incomes, demand also rises for the goods and services of local nonfarm enterprises, creating work outside farming as well (Haggblade et al. 2010). Raising productivity within agriculture and moving into higher-return work outside agriculture are thus not competing routes. Gains from the first may arise sooner, while gains from the second depend on how quickly firms outside agriculture expand. The counterfactuals reveal how much poverty each switch would remove, not how quickly or readily the outcome could be brought about.

#### The incidence of the gains across the distribution

So far, the simulations have been summarized according to their effect on poverty overall. The summary leaves open where in the distribution the gains fall, that is, whether they reach the poorest households or the households closer to the poverty line. The growth incidence curve answers this question. For each point along the welfare distribution, from the poorest percentile to the richest, the curve shows by how much a simulation raises welfare in percentage terms. Comparing the various curves reveals that the earnings simulations and the employment simulation concentrate the gains among different parts of the poor population. The three earnings simulations raise welfare the most among the poorest and by a wide margin. At the first percentiles, the gain exceeds 50 percent, declining steadily as welfare rises (refer to figure 4.10). Each simulation assigns a poor worker the earnings their characteristics would command with more education, in wage work, or outside agriculture. Because similar characteristics receive similar predictions, the gain is roughly the same in absolute terms among poor workers throughout the distribution. The same absolute gain is worth proportionally more, however, to a worker who started with less. Consider two poor workers whose simulated earnings each rise by $1.00 a day. For the one who was earning $1.00, the earnings double; for the one who was earning $2.00, the earnings rise by half. The curve therefore peaks at the poorest percentiles and declines as welfare rises.

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs
