The employment simulation operates differently. It raises the quantity of work a household supplies rather than the price the household's labor commands. The gain thus depends on how many workers the household adds relative to how many it already has. A household with three members in work that puts a fourth to work raises its welfare by a third. Among the households the simulation reaches, increases of about this size are the rule, that is, close to 30 percent to 40 percent, whatever the household's level of welfare. This is unlike the earnings simulations, in which the proportional gain falls as welfare rises. The curve is shaped instead by how many households the simulation reaches at each welfare level because only those households that already have at least one worker and employ fewer than 75 percent of their working-age adults are affected, while the other households gain nothing. The share is lowest among the poorest households, among which more households either have no worker at all or already employ most of their working-age adults. The share rises to a plateau through the lower middle of the distribution and falls to zero at the poverty line, because only poor households are treated. The curve accordingly forms a hump: low at the bottom, highest across the lower middle, and tapering above the line.

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 The two sets of simulations reach different poor people. This is the central message of the incidence analysis. Which margin does more-putting more household members to work or raising what workers earn-depends on where the poverty line is drawn. At the $3.00 line, the earnings curves lie above the employment curve across almost the whole range of the poor, and the employment hump is narrow and low. Among the extreme poor, the binding constraint is the price of labor rather than the quantity of labor at every point below the line. At the $8.30 line, the employment hump is broad, peaking around the 40th percentile and rising above the earnings curves through much of the lower middle. The households it reaches are disproportionately those with working-age women out of work because employment among women is lowest across the lower middle (refer to the subsection on gender-specific barriers to employment and box 4.2).

## BOX 4.2

**Poor women: Fewer are in work, and those who work are paid the least**

The chapter's employment result is largely about women. This box brings the findings on women together and adds one piece the main text does not report: how the working poor are distributed across activities by gender. The disadvantage among women are compounded: Fewer poor women than poor men work, and those who do work are concentrated in activities that pay least. The first main disadvantage is described in the text above. Women's employment is lowest across the lower middle of the global distribution (refer to figure 4.4). The 75 percent benchmark of the employment simulation is the rate men already attain, so reaching it means employing more women. The two variants of the simulation show that raising women's employment to the benchmark reduces poverty by about a fifth, while raising men's barely affects poverty (refer to figure 4.6, panels c-f). The gains reach households in the lower middle, which are disproportionately those with working-age women out of work (refer to figure 4.10). The second main disadvantage is documented in annex 4D. Among the working poor, women are more concentrated than men in self-employment and in agriculture, the two positions with the lowest returns. Near the $3.00 line, employed poor women are 15 to 20 percentage points less likely than employed poor men to hold a wage job, and about 10 percentage points more likely to work in agriculture (refer to annex 4D, figures 4D.4 and 4D.5). The earnings simulations do not separate this disadvantage out. Each predicts a single gain for the poor as a group, averaging over women and men. The two disadvantages are compounded and become amplified. Following the current pattern of women's work, if more women work, this means more workers are involved in activities that pay least. So, the poverty reduction the employment route can deliver depends on where the additional women can work, not only on how many women take up work. The constraints on women's employment-childcare, transport, negative social norms, and the shortage of demand for women's labor outside agriculture-therefore govern both the quantity of women's work and the returns to the work.

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

The implication is not that one route is superior, whether the quantity of work or the returns, but that the two reach largely different groups. Raising earnings delivers the largest gains to the poorest households. Raising employment delivers the largest gains to the near-poor higher up, where more households have working-age adults, many of them women, who are not in work. This is what the incidence analysis adds to the chapter's argument: More work and better-paying work are two pathways that serve different parts of the poor population, not alternatives between which to choose.16

#### Lower returns in poorer economies: Panel data evidence on labor market transitions

The simulations described so far ask what poverty would look like if poor workers had access to work with higher returns, holding everything else fixed. Panel data, which follow the same individuals over time, allow the hypothetical shifts to be set against real shifts: the labor market transitions individuals have actually made and the poverty changes they have actually experienced. Evidence from nationally representative panels in 41 economies across East Asia, Europe, Latin America, South Asia, and Sub-Saharan Africa, with windows of two to seven years, links shifts in the employment composition of households to the movements of households into and out of poverty (Bussolo, Torre, et al. 2026).17 Because poverty is a household outcome and the relevant margin in poorer settings is often the reallocation of working-age members across activities rather than any single worker's job change, the analysis tracks the shares of a household's working-age members in wage employment, nonagricultural self-employment, agriculture, and nonemployment. The evidence validates and qualifies the results described above: (1) What a given transition delivers depends heavily on the country setting, and the ordering the simulations assume-wage work above self-employment and nonagriculture above agriculture, the ranking known in the literature as a job ladder-holds in some settings but not others.18 (2) The transitions poor households actually undergo differ by setting as well, and much of the movement in poorer economies is sideways rather than upward. (3) Employment performs a function the simulations do not model, namely, not only escape from poverty, but protection against falling into poverty. The first finding is that the returns to a shift into wage employment are far larger in richer economies than in poorer economies. If the share of household members in wage employment increases by 10 percentage points, the probability of escaping poverty rises by about 2.4 percentage points in upper-middle-income economies and 2.2 percentage points in highincome economies, but by only 0.8 percentage points in low- and lower-middle-income economies (refer to figure 4.11). Nonwage work exhibits (almost) the opposite gradient. In low- and lower-middle-income economies, a comparable shift into nonwage employment raises the probability of escaping poverty by 0.5 percentage points, not far below the return to wage employment in the same economies. In upper-middle-income economies, meanwhile, the effect is statistically indistinguishable from zero, and, in high-income economies, it is negative. The route out of poverty thus differs by setting. Where wage jobs pay well, they do the lifting, and, where they do not, the advantage of wage work over other forms of work is small. This pattern is consistent with the productivity differences between rich and poor economies that persist even among formal firms in the same sector (Hsieh and Klenow 2009; Restuccia 2019).

-----

Where wages track productivity, the same transition yields a larger poverty-exit return in a richer economy. This compounds a disadvantage on the same margin because a rising share of wage employment is a marker of structural transformation (Breza and Kaur 2025): poorer economies have both less wage work and lower returns to wage work. Disaggregating nonwage work into its components-employer status, agricultural selfemployment, and nonagricultural self-employment-demonstrates that the ranking of activities by contribution to poverty exit differs by income group. In upper-middle-income economies, employer status contributes most to poverty exit, followed by wage employment, and both dominate self-employment. But, even there, the lower rungs invert: Agricultural self-employment contributes more to exit than nonagricultural self-employment, the contribution of which is statistically indistinguishable from zero. In the high-income European economies, only wage employment matters; no form of self-employment raises the probability of exit. In both low- and lower-middle-income economies, the ordering is reversed outright: Nonagricultural self-employment contributes more to poverty exit than wage employment, in part because much of what is recorded as wage employment in these settings is informal and low-productivity (Fields et al. 2023). The ranking of activities that the simulations assume is therefore a feature of more highly developed labor markets, not a universal one.19

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

The second finding concerns the transitions poor households actually achieve. Poor households edge toward employment everywhere, but how much and from where differ by income level. Among households that start poor, the increase in the share of working-age members in wage employment rises with country income: about 3.4 percentage points in low- and lowermiddle-income economies, 5.4 in upper-middle-income economies, and 7.7 in high-income economies (refer to figure 4.12). The source of the movement differs even more than the size of the movement. In upper-middle- and high-income economies, essentially all the movement is drawn from nonemployment. The not-working share falls by 5.7 and 8.1 percentage points, respectively, while the remaining employment statuses largely offset one another. In low- and lower-middle-income economies, by contrast, poor households reallocate across activities as much as into work: Agriculture sheds 2.2 percentage points and nonemployment an equal 2.2, while wage employment and nonagricultural self-employment absorb the movement.20 Part of the observed churn is thus sideways, that is, across agriculture, nonagricultural self-employment, and spells out of work, rather than up a single ladder, consistent with the cycling between marginal employment and nonemployment documented by Donovan et al. (2023) and the fragmented activity portfolios of poor households described by Banerjee and Duflo (2007).21 This is the dynamic counterpart of a point the simulations make statically: A transition counts as an improvement only if it raises the returns to work, and, in poorer settings, a meaningful part of the observed movement does not do this.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 A final finding is that the transitions that lift households out of poverty are not the same as those that keep households from falling into poverty. Nonagricultural self-employment is the clearest case. It protects against entry into poverty among every country income group, including the high-income economies, where it does nothing to move the poor out of poverty, and its point estimates generally exceed those of wage employment. Employment serves two distinct functions, that is, a route out of poverty and a guard against falling into poverty. The activities that serve one need not serve the other. Built on cross-sectional data, the simulations model only the first function. The panel evidence shows that the second matters as well and that it runs through different activities. Taken together, these findings sharpen the chapter's argument. What employment can do about poverty depends not only on access to work, but on what work pays. The transitions that are realized agree with the simulations that returns are central. But what a given transition delivers depends on the setting. The same move into wage employment pays far more in a richer economy than in a poorer one. The activities that lift households out of poverty differ across countries. What self-employment accomplishes in poorer economies is what wage work accomplishes in richer economies. Employment protects households from falling into poverty and lifts them out of poverty. The activities that guard against poverty are not always the ones that do the lifting. There is, in short, no single composition of employment that serves the poor everywhere. What works depends on the stage of development and on whether the aim is escape or protection.

## Final remarks: Poverty reduction requires

### both more jobs and better-paying jobs

This chapter opens with an observation: Human capital is the main asset of the poor, and, among many of the poor, it is the only asset. Whether a poor household can escape poverty depends on two questions about the labor of the household: how fully the household members work and how much they earn from work. The evidence assembled here responds that progress requires movement in both dimensions. Raising employment among the poor to a realistic benchmark would remove roughly a fifth of measured poverty. Yet, a large share of the poor already work, and, for these poor, the binding constraint is the low returns to their labor rather than the quantity of the labor. More jobs and more better-paying jobs are not competing strategies. The contrast between them is sharper in the framing than in the evidence. Two considerations soften the contrast. First, among the poor, a better job is, in large part, a new job, that is, wage employment in place of self-employment, work outside agriculture in place of work within agriculture, and occupations that become available with more schooling (Bandiera et al. 2022). Moving a worker into work with higher returns raises the returns to the worker's labor and adds a more productive job at the same time: a single reallocation that is at once more and better, not two strategies to weigh against each other. The trade-off that remains

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

lies within this better jobs route: whether to raise the returns to the work the poor already do or to move the poor into work that returns more. Rather than more against better, this is where the policy choice is actually drawn. Second, even if work does not generate higher returns, obtaining work is an improvement. In a household with an adult out of work, more work raises welfare by an amount corresponding to whatever the poor earn from their work. For these poor, more is already better. Considered from these two angles, the agenda is less a choice between the amount of the poor's work and the productivity of the poor than a choice about the effort needed to raise both by drawing in adults who are not working and by lifting the returns on the work others already do. The evidence on hours blurs the division between quantity and earnings from a third angle. Earnings per worker-the measure on which the poor's disadvantage is assessed throughout the chapter-are the product of the pay an hour commands and the number of hours worked. The poor work somewhat fewer hours than the nonpoor (refer to box 4.1). So, part of what registers as lower earnings is in fact a smaller quantity of work. The quantity margin is therefore wider than participation alone. It includes the underemployed worker who would supply more hours, not only the adult drawn into work. Raising the hours would thus reduce poverty through the same channel as raising employment. The evidence presented in this chapter is derived from household surveys that record who works and at what, but not the firms that might hire the workers. The chapter gives, by design, an account from the household perspective. Several of its central findings can be understood only based on evidence on how jobs are created. Wage work is scarce, and a large share of the poor's self-employment reflects the absence of wage work. Absorbing the poor into sectors generating higher returns would require that these sectors expand severalfold. The same move into wage work pays far more in a richer economy because the returns reflects the productivity of the firms and the economy a worker joins, not the worker alone. Whether the poor can reach better-paying work depends on whether such work is created. What determines its creation-the allocation of capital and finance, the strength of competition, and the adoption of new technology-lies outside the evidence presented here, though the literature on each is substantial (Didier and Cusolito 2024; Hsieh and Klenow 2009; World Bank 2026). Artificial intelligence (AI) is the newest entry on that list, and its incidence is the least settled. Exposure to generative AI is far lower in low- and middleincome economies than in high-income economies-about 4.5 percent of jobs against 14.2 percent, because employment in poorer economies is concentrated in agriculture, manual work, and enterprises of fewer than 10 people (World Bank 2026). Less exposure to displacement is not the same as standing to gain, however. The structure that limits displacement also limits access to new work and to productivity gains. The mechanism that would matter the most for the poor is not the creation of high-skill jobs, but the use of these tools to raise returns on the activities that the poor already undertake. Whether poor workers would capture such gains is unresolved. World Development Report 2026 (World Bank 2026)

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 offers cross-country rather then within-country distributional evidence. On who gains inside a country, it describes mechanisms and case evidence, including that the firms best placed to act on AI advice are those already performing well. The sign of the specific impact of AI on the employment and earnings of poor households is therefore still an open question. The data assembled in this chapter that link labor market outcomes to poverty status across the world's population is the kind of evidence that could be used to answer the question once measures of AI exposure and adoption can be brought in. The time horizon is long on every margin. Education among the poor yields returns only as cohorts pass through school into work. The disadvantages associated with lack of education are transmitted across generations. Upward mobility from the bottom is still difficult among recent cohorts. The expansion of employment opportunities, more rapid in principle, has, in practice, progressed little. The employment rates among the poor have barely changed over the past two decades. This and other evidence implies that an agenda for poverty reduction and for development in such a context must be both ambitious and targeted at the long term. Progress will be counted in cohorts and generations rather than months or years.

## Annex 4A. Data and methodology

### Data sources and coverage

This annex describes the data presented in the chapter. The main data combine harmonized household survey microdata from the Global Monitoring Database with official poverty and welfare estimates from the World Bank's Poverty and Inequality Platform (PIP).22 The GMD provides the underlying microdata used for poverty monitoring across low-, middle-, and selected high-income economies and contains harmonized information on welfare, demographic characteristics, labor market outcomes, education, and household composition. To improve the coverage of the global population, the analysis supplements GMD microdata with harmonized tabulations from the Luxembourg Income Study (LIS) database for nine high-income economies where comparable microdata are not available within the GMD

framework.23 Together, the GMD and LIS sources provide near-global coverage of 96 percent

of the world population and 96 percent of low- and lower-middle-income economy populations, enabling the construction of globally comparable profiles across the welfare distribution. The analysis covers 150 economies across three reference years-2003, 2013, and 2024-that capture the trends in global poverty and living standards over the last two decades (refer to table 4A.1). Because nationally representative household surveys are not available annually in all countries, surveys collected in nearby years are aligned to a common reporting year before being pooled for analysis.

-----

### Expansion of harmonized labor market information

A major contribution of the current profiling database is the substantial expansion of harmonized labor market information within the GMD. Historically, the GMD was developed primarily to support global poverty monitoring. The harmonization effort thus focused on welfare aggregates and core demographic characteristics. Although labor information was collected in many household surveys, harmonization was conducted selectively and with varying degrees of completeness, particularly among surveys from the early 2000s. Over the past several years, regional and global GMD teams undertook a comprehensive effort to harmonize labor market variables across a larger set of household surveys. This involved reviewing original questionnaires, survey documentation, and national coding structures; harmonizing employment concepts; constructing internationally comparable classifications; and validating outputs against survey reports and official labor statistics. Particular attention was assigned to three labor dimensions: labor force status, employment status, and sector of work. Harmonizing sector-of-work information required the extensive mapping of country-specific industry classifications to ISIC Revision 4, often involving reviews of detailed survey documentation and national classification systems to ensure comparability across countries, survey vintages, and regions.24 The effort substantially expanded the availability of labor market information. The coverage of harmonized labor indicators rose from approximately 70 percent to more than 90 percent of the population represented in the profiling database, greatly improving representativeness and enabling consistent analysis across countries and over time. Despite these improvements, the harmonization of labor indicators remains more challenging than the harmonization of demographic variables because of differences in survey design, labor definitions, reference periods, and the implementation of international statistical standards.

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

### Demographic alignment and population reweighting

The objective of the profiling exercise is to describe the characteristics of the global population in a common reporting year. Because household surveys are conducted in different years and population structures evolve over time, survey weights are first recalibrated to represent the demographic composition of the reporting year. For each survey, original household weights are adjusted through iterative proportional fitting. The calibration aligns weighted population totals to externally benchmarked demographic distributions corresponding to the reporting year and performed separately for each country and reference year. The calibration targets are gender (male and female) and five age-groups (0-14, 15-24, 25-44, 45-64, and 65+). Population benchmarks are taken from official demographic projections for the reporting year.25 Let wi denote the original survey weight. Calibrated weights, w\*, are obtained by minimizing the distance between original and adjusted weights, subject to the demographic calibration constraints. The resulting weights reproduce population totals in the reporting year, while preserving the joint distribution of characteristics observed in the survey. Unless otherwise noted, all estimates throughout the chapter use these calibrated weights.

### Welfare alignment with ficial PIP estimates

Following demographic alignment, welfare distributions are adjusted to reproduce the official PIP estimates for the reporting year. Let y s denote welfare observed in survey year s. Welfare values are adjusted using the growth factors embedded in the official PIP lineup methodology to allow the calibrated-weight welfare distribution to reproduce official PIP mean welfare estimates and official PIP poverty estimates for the reporting year t. The adjustment preserves household welfare rankings within each survey, while updating welfare levels to be consistent with official poverty monitoring statistics. The procedure is illustrated by a concrete case. A survey collected in 2020 and aligned to 2024 first receives demographic adjustments to reflect the 2024 population structure. Welfare values are then adjusted to reproduce official 2024 PIP mean welfare and poverty estimates.

### Construction of the global welfare distribution

Beyond poverty-based classifications, the chapter analyzes characteristics across the global welfare distribution. To facilitate comparisons over time, global percentile groups are defined using a harmonized set of welfare thresholds constructed separately for each reference year from the World Bank's global welfare distribution database, which covers 218 economies and contains 1,000 welfare bins per country.26 Welfare values are based on the PIP lineup methodology.

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

The resulting thresholds define the welfare cutoffs corresponding to each percentile of the global population in the reference year. Individuals in the profiling database are assigned to percentile groups according to the position of their welfare level relative to these global thresholds. This approach differs from the construction of percentiles within countries. Thus, all individuals are evaluated against the same global welfare distribution, enabling direct comparisons across countries, regions, and income groups. An important implication is that percentile thresholds are derived from the full global distribution of 218 economies, whereas the profiling database covers a somewhat smaller subset on which harmonized demographic and socioeconomic variables are available. Consequently, percentile groups within the profiling sample do not represent exactly 1 percent of the world population.

### Sample definitions and variable-specific coverage

The analysis uses different reference populations depending on the characteristic under examination. Demographic indicators, including poverty status, age structure, gender composition, household composition, and geographic location, are calculated using the full population represented in the profiling database. Educational attainment is restricted to individuals ages 15 or older. Labor market indicators are calculated for the working-age population ages 15-64, a restriction that improves comparability across countries with different schooling patterns, retirement systems, and labor market institutions. For labor force participation and employment indicators, the reference population consists of all individuals ages 15-64 on whom labor market information is available. For sector of employment, occupation, employment type, and industry analyses, the reference population is further restricted to employed individuals ages 15-64. Individuals who are unemployed, outside the labor force, or have missing employment status are excluded. An additional challenge is that household surveys differ in the reference period used to collect labor information. While many surveys follow the International Labour Organization standard of measuring employment status based on work performed during the seven days preceding the interview, others use the previous month, the previous 12 months, or measures of usual economic activity (refer to table 4A.2). Differences in recall periods can affect measured levels of labor force participation, employment, unemployment, and sectoral composition. Shorter recall periods capture current labor market conditions more accurately, but may be more sensitive to temporary fluctuations. Longer recall periods may better reflect usual employment arrangements, but may not capture current conditions as precisely. To maximize comparability, harmonized labor indicators are constructed using standardized definitions wherever possible. The chapter therefore focuses on broad labor market categorieslabor force participation, employment status, employment type, and sector of work-that are generally more comparable across surveys relative to detailed measures, such as hours worked, earnings, or job tenure (refer to table 4A.3).

### Missing data and coverage assessment

Core demographic variables-welfare, age, gender, and household composition-are available for nearly all observations and countries and achieve close to full population coverage (refer to box 4A.1). Coverage is lower on labor market variables, particularly labor force status, employment status, and sector of work, where information is missing because of nonresponse or because the variable was not collected in the underlying survey instrument. For each profiling variable, the chapter documents the number of economies represented, the share of the global population covered, the share of the global poor covered, and the share of missing observations. Unless otherwise noted, estimates are calculated using all available observations for the relevant characteristic. Sample coverage thus varies across figures and tables. This approach maximizes global representation, while avoiding the distortions that would arise from restricting all analyses to the smaller subset of surveys with complete information on every variable.

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

## BOX 4A.1

###### Defining jobs, employment, and employment status

Labor market outcomes are central to understanding how individuals and households generate income and escape poverty. The chapter uses jobs as the primary lens through which economic opportunities are examined. Labor indicators reported in the chapter are derived from harmonized household survey data contained in the Global Monitoring Database. Wherever possible, data harmonization follows internationally recognized standards established by the International Labour Organization, while preserving comparability across a large and diverse set of survey instruments.

**Labor force status. Labor force status classifies people into three categories:**

(a) employed individuals who worked for at least one hour during a specific reference period (usually the previous seven days), including individuals temporarily absent from a job, regardless of whether work was formal or informal, paid or entailed work in a household enterprise, such as farming or a small business where there is no explicit paycheck attached to the work; (b) unemployed individuals who were not employed, but who were actively seeking employment, including those waiting to start a new job; and (c) individuals not in the labor force who were neither employed nor actively seeking employment.

**Employment status. Employment status characterizes the relationship between**

workers and the economic unit in which they work. In the chapter, the focus is on two categories: wage workers, that is, people who are employees (regardless of formal status or contract status), and the self-employed, which consists of workers who are working either in their own business or in a business that belongs to another household member (a household enterprise) and that is (typically) not associated with a regular paycheck.

**Sector of work. The sector of work classifies employed individuals according**

to the principal economic activity of their main job during the reference period. National industry classifications are mapped to the International Standard Industrial Classification of All Economic Activities (ISIC Revision 4) wherever possible.a

**Working hours. Hours are the number of hours worked in the main job during**

the reference period, typically the previous seven days. Questions on the hours worked vary considerably across surveys, including differences in recall periods, the treatment of secondary jobs, the treatment of temporary absences, and the reporting of seasonal work. Cross-country comparisons of working hours should arguably be interpreted with greater caution than employment status or labor force participation indicators.

*(continued)*

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

###### BOX 4A.1

###### Defining jobs, employment, and employment status (continued)

**Measurement considerations. Labor indicators are inherently more difficult to**

compare internationally than demographic or educational variables because survey instruments differ in reference periods, questionnaire design, treatment of seasonal work, and implementation of international standards. A particularly important issue is the transition from the standards adopted by the 13th International Conference of Labour Statisticians (ILO 1983) to those adopted by the 19th International Conference of Labour Statisticians (ILO 2013). Under the earlier framework, subsistence production (where output was mostly consumed by the household and not sold) was classified as employment because it fell within the System of National Accounts production boundary. Under the revised framework, employment is more narrowly defined as work performed for pay or profit, while own-use production is not classified as employment. As countries gradually adopt the revised standards, the change in employment rates between surveys may reflect a definitional change if data are not used with caution. Wherever possible, labor indicators are measured in the chapter based on the earlier employment definition. Thus, for example, people and households involved in subsistence farming are generally considered employed. a. Refer to ISIC (International Standard Industrial Classification of All Economic Activities) (dashboard), Statistics Division, Department of Economic and Social Affairs, United Nations, https://unstats.un.org/unsd/classifications [/Econ/ISIC.cshtml.](https://unstats.un.org/unsd/classifications/Econ/ISIC.cshtml)

### Annex 4B . The percent poverty gap

Three poverty measures recur in the chapter: the poverty rate, the poverty gap, and the percent poverty gap. The first two are standard. The third is less familiar and is defined here. Let yi denote the welfare of person i, and let z be the poverty line. The poverty rate H is the share of the population with welfare below z. It records whether a person is poor, but reveals nothing about how far below the line the individual falls. The poverty gap (PG) weights each poor person by the shortfall from the line as a fraction of the line, as follows:

*PG = H* 1 *z - yi*

*Nz z* (4B.1)

*i Nz where Nz denotes the set (and number) of poor people, those with yi ≤ z. The PG measures the average depth of poverty in the units of the line.*

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

The percent poverty gap (PPG) normalizes each poor person's shortfall by their own welfare, as follows:

*PPG = H* 1 *z API*

-1 100 = H (4B.2)

x

*Nz i Nz yi*

The term (z/yi - 1) × 100 is the percent increase in person i's own welfare required to reach the line. The PPG thus has a simple interpretation. It is the poverty rate, multiplied by the average percent increase (API) needed to bring each poor person to the poverty line. This decomposition into H × API is used directly in the counterfactual exercises of the chapter, where a simulated income distribution y• yields PPG(y•) = H(y•) × API(y•), separating the change in poverty into the part operating through the poverty rate and the part operating through the average shortfall among those who remain poor. The own-welfare normalization makes the PPG sensitive to the depth of poverty in a way the PG is not sensitive. Because z/yi rises without bound as yi approaches zero, a person deep below the line contributes disproportionately to the average. Someone at 10 percent of the line requires a 900 percent increase to reach it, while someone at 90 percent of the line requires only an 11 percent increase. The PPG therefore rises steeply if the welfare of the poorest is extremely low and falls sharply if their welfare improves even by amounts too small to move the poverty rate. The PPG is the poverty measure that corresponds to the Prosperity Gap of Kraay et al. (2023), and it is closely related to the Watts index, which sums log shortfalls ln z - ln yi rather than proportional ones. It is not, however, the squared poverty gap, or FGT2, of Foster et al. (1984). FGT2 achieves distribution sensitivity by squaring the line-normalized shortfall, [(z - yi)/z]2, which remains bounded for each individual. The PPG normalizes by own welfare, z/yi - 1, which is unbounded as yi → 0. Both are distribution sensitive among the poor, but through different weighting, and they are not interchangeable. Where the chapter reports proportional reductions in the PPG, the relevant object is the change in this own-welfare-normalized measure, not in FGT2.

### Annex 4C . The employment counterfactual, country-by-country

The chapter reports the employment counterfactual as a global aggregate. Applied separately to each economy, the aggregate reduces the poverty rate in every economy, but the magnitude of the reduction varies with the economy's observed poverty level, and the pattern differs across poverty measures. This annex documents and explains the variation. Figure 4C.1 plots, for each economy, the counterfactual poverty rate against the observed poverty rate at each poverty line. Points lie below the 45-degree line wherever the

Points below the 45-degree line denote a reduction in poverty. Poverty lines are* $3.00- and $8.30-a-person-a-day (in 2021 purchasing power parity terms).

counterfactual reduces poverty, which is everywhere. The vertical distance from the line-the size of the reduction-is largest at observed moderate poverty, reaching 20 to 25 percentage points (roughly 30 percent to 55 percent observed poverty at the $3.00 line, and 40 percent to 70 percent at the $8.30 line), and shrinks toward both extremes. Where observed poverty is low, few households remain to be lifted out of poverty. Where it is deep, points are close to the 45-degree line, and most households are so far below the line that the welfare added by an additional worker earning the household's own low welfare per worker does not carry the household across the line. The convexity-large reductions at moderate poverty, small ones at the extremes-is specific to the poverty rate because the poverty rate registers only households that cross the line. The poverty gap and the percent poverty gap behave differently. Figures 4C.2 and 4C.3 show that both fall roughly in proportion to their observed levels across the full range of economies, with no flattening in the case of deep poverty. The reason is that these measures account for any welfare gain to a poor household whether or not the gain lifts the household across the line. In a deep-poverty economy where the counterfactual leaves most households below the line, the poverty rate barely moves. However, the gap and percent poverty gap still narrow because the added earnings reduce the distance to the line among the households that remain poor.

The source of the convexity is visible in the shape of the global welfare distribution (refer to figure 4C.4). Most of the global population is living at welfare levels between $3.00 and roughly $20.00 a day, and the density peaks slightly below the $8.30 line; about a 10th of the population lies below $3.00. Where a poverty line cuts this distribution governs how many households may move across it under a given proportional welfare gain. The $3.00 line sits in a thin part of the distribution, with little mass on either side. A welfare boost therefore lifts relatively few households across the line. The $8.30 line sits close to the peak, where the density slightly below the line is high. The same proportional boost thus moves many households across the line. The country-level convexity in the poverty rate is the cross-country counterpart of this mechanism. This means the poverty rate's response to a welfare gain depends on the size of the population mass that sits in the immediate vicinity of the line.

###### Annex 4D Barriers to earnings: Country-

### .

### level evidence and methodology

The chapter establishes, for the world as a whole, that poor workers have less education and are more often in self-employment and in agriculture relative to the nonpoor. This annex

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

shows that each pattern holds within economies in nearly every economy observed and reports the unconditional earnings differences associated with the self-employment and agricultural divides. These unconditional differences are descriptive, that is, they compare average welfare per worker across groups without holding worker characteristics fixed. They thus combine the returns to each type of work with differences in the education, age, and location of the workers in this work. The conditional estimates that isolate the returns are reported in the chapter.

### Education

Below the $8.30 line, the majority of workers have completed only primary education or less, and, in virtually every economy, the rate of secondary completion is lower among the poor than among the nonpoor. The widest gaps are in low- and lower-middle-income economies (refer to figure 4D.1, panels a and b). The welfare per worker with secondary educational attainment is above the welfare per worker with lower educational attainment in virtually every economy (refer to figure 4D.1, panel c). The human capital gap extends beyond schooling. Childhood stunting, which directly impairs cognitive development, is consistently more prevalent among the poor. There is a twofold to sevenfold difference between the poorest and richest quintiles in the country evidence. Broader summary measures that combine education with health show the same gradient across countries.27

### Self-employment

Self-employment accounts for the large majority of employment below the $8.30 line. Wage employment rises steeply with income, reaching roughly 80 percent of employment at the top of the distribution (refer to figure 4D.2, panel a). In nearly every economy, the poor are more highly concentrated than the nonpoor in self-employment (refer to figure 4D.2, panel b). The welfare per self-employed worker is below the welfare per wage worker in virtually every economy. The largest proportional gaps are in low- and lower-middle-income economies (refer to figure 4D.2, panel c). The proportional reading requires care. The welfare comparison is plotted on logarithmic axes. Vertical distance from the 45-degree line is thus the ratio of the two welfare levels, not the dollar difference, and a smaller proportional gap at higher welfare levels still corresponds to a large absolute difference. The gradient is consistent with a substantial body of evidence that self-employment in low-income settings is often a residual activity, undertaken for want of access to wage work rather than by choice. Margolis (2014) estimates that roughly two-thirds of self-employment in the developing world reflects the absence of a better alternative, and Breza et al. (2021) provide causal evidence that a substantial share of agricultural self-employment reflects labor market rationing (refer also to Fields 2019). On this reading, the barriers to wage employment behind the pattern-thin local labor markets, few firms, transport and information constraints-are themselves barriers to higher earnings among the poor.

Agriculture's share of employment declines steeply with income. Below the $8.30 line, it accounts for roughly 40 percent to 60 percent of employment, and, above the median, it is negligible (refer to figure 4D.3, panel a). Welfare per agricultural worker is systematically below welfare per nonagricultural worker, and the largest proportional gaps are in the poorest economies (refer to figure 4D.3, panel c). The agricultural welfare gap is proportionally wider than the self-employment gap (the cluster of dots representing the countries sits further below the 45-degree line), which anticipates the larger conditional premium to leaving agriculture. The same logarithmic-scale qualification applies: Low- and lower-middle-income economies sit farthest from the line. In the former, agricultural welfare is the smallest fraction relative to nonagricultural welfare. Meanwhile, richer economies sit closer to the line, yet at far higher welfare levels. So, even the smaller proportional gap in the richer economies represents a large absolute difference.

### Gender

The concentration documented above is more pronounced among women than among men and in a way that compounds a disadvantage established in the chapter. As the text shows, poor women work for pay far less often relative to poor men. Among women who do work, the composition of the employment is also worse. Poor women are more highly concentrated than poor men in lower-return types of work and sectors. The disadvantage is therefore twofold: a lower probability of working at all and, conditional on working, a higher probability of working in the activities that pay the least. On the type of employment, poor women are less likely than poor men to hold wage jobs and more likely to be in self-employment, including nonremunerated labor contributions to family microenterprises (refer to figure 4D.4). The gap in wage employment is on the order of

It narrows as welfare rises and reverses among the richest workers, where women slightly overtake men in wage employment, a crossing that reflects the entry of educated women into professional and public sector work at the top of the distribution. On sector, poor women are more highly concentrated than poor men in agriculture by roughly 10 percentage points at the bottom of the distribution, though the two converge toward the middle (refer to figure 4D.5, panel a). The mirror image holds outside agriculture, but with a further distinction the figure does not fully resolve. Within nonagriculture, men are concentrated in industry across the distribution, while women move into services and overtake men toward the top there. Figure 4D.5, panel b, merges industry and services into a single nonagricultural category. So, the within-nonagriculture pattern is noted, but not displayed. The policy-relevant range is the lower half of the distribution, where the gender difference in the composition of work is largest and where, combined with the lower participation described in the text, poor women are farthest from the higher-return work available to the nonpoor.

### Estimating the conditional premiums and the participation penalty

The concentration patterns documented so far are descriptive. They establish where the poor work and that the activities in which the poor are concentrated are associated with lower average earnings. They do not, on their own, establish how much of this earnings difference

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs represents returns to the activity rather than a reflection of the workers who are in the activity. The unconditional welfare gaps reported above combine the two. Isolating the returns requires a counterfactual that holds worker characteristics fixed, while varying the activity, and a measure of how much less likely the poor are to be found in the higher-return activity to begin with. This subsection sets out the two objects used in the main text and in the earnings counterfactuals: a conditional premium, that is, the predicted earnings gain from moving a worker into a higher-return activity, and a conditional penalty in participation, that is, the gap between poor and nonpoor workers with the same observable characteristics in the probability of being in the activity. Figure 4D.6 describes how these two objects are constructed. For the two counterfactual reassignments of a poor worker from one activity to another (which, for brevity, is called a switch), holding their characteristics fixed and applying the destination activity's earnings schedule, the premium is a price comparison. Two Mincer earnings equations are estimated, one on each side of the divide (for example, one on agricultural earners, and one on nonagricultural earners), each regressing log earnings on education, age, gender, and location and estimated on all observed earners, poor and nonpoor together.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 The characteristics of a given poor worker are then run through both equations, and the predicted gain ∆ˆ is the ratio of predicted destination earnings to predicted origin earnings,

*i*

minus one. The worker's own characteristics are held fixed. The change is in the earnings schedule applied. The premium thus answers the question: What would this worker earn if the destination activity's earnings structure applied to the worker's unchanged profile? The set of ∆ˆ

*i*

is averaged across poor workers to give a country-level premium. The education switch is constructed differently because education is a characteristic of the worker rather than a property of the activity. Here, a single Mincer equation is estimated, and the counterfactual changes the worker's education endowment, raising it to the nonpoor average or to secondary completion, while holding the worker's other characteristics at the poor average and applying the same earnings schedule to both the actual and the counterfactual endowment. The premium is the resulting predicted percentage gain. In contrast to the sectoral and employment-type switches, which swap the earnings schedule and hold characteristics fixed, the education switch swaps a characteristic and holds the schedule fixed. It therefore uses one equation rather than two. The preferred specification estimates this single equation with education-level dummies and an education-by-gender interaction. The returns to schooling are therefore allowed to differ by gender rather than constrained to a common coefficient. The penalty is estimated separately from a linear probability model of participation in the higher-return activity, for instance, the probability of working in nonagriculture regressed on a poverty indicator and the same observable characteristics, across all workers.

The coefficient on the poverty indicator, *γˆ* 1, is the conditional gap in participation between otherwise similar poor and nonpoor workers. It is a descriptive object: a residual gap that persists after controlling for education, age, gender, and location and that is consistent with a barrier to entry, but does not establish one. The chapter's language reflects this, describing the poor as concentrated in lower-return activities rather than as barred from higher-return activities. The premium and the penalty are derived from different models and are not mechanically linked. An economy may exhibit a large penalty and a small premium, or the reverse. Figure 4D.7 plots the two against each other for the agricultural case. In almost every economy, the poor are underrepresented in nonagriculture, and the predicted gain from switching is large and positive. Thus, both objects are sizable. The country income groups are interspersed rather than clustered, indicating that this is a feature of poor workers rather than of poor economies. The two are negatively related across economies, a pattern consistent with a common underlying friction, but partly attributable to shared selection. Where the poor are more highly concentrated in agriculture, poor agricultural workers tend to have weaker observable characteristics, which lowers their predicted agricultural earnings and raises the predicted gain. So, the exercise relies on the levels, not on the relationship.

Three features of the construction signal that the estimated premiums should be read as upper bounds on the true returns to switching. First, the earnings variable is welfare per workerhousehold consumption, divided by the number of workers, allocated to the household's majority sector-rather than an individual wage. This bundles labor and nonlabor income. The majority-rule allocation creates a wedge in mixed-sector households that is signed against the smaller sector, typically agriculture, biasing its measured earnings downward and the predicted gain from leaving agriculture upward. Dropping economies where agriculture employs fewer than 10 percent of workers where the wedge is largest leaves the qualitative pattern unchanged. Second, the estimates document association rather than causation. The conditional penalty thus captures the sorting of the poor across activities, not the mechanism. Third, the destination equation is estimated on workers observed in the destination activity, who may be positively selected on characteristics not observed in the data. If so, predicted destination earnings overstate what a switching worker would actually earn, again biasing the premium upward. All three considerations push in the same direction. So, the reported magnitudes bound the true returns from above rather than below, and the qualitative finding-that sizable conditional premiums survive the controls-is the robust one.

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026

### Annex 4E . Growth incidence curves: Construction and interpretation

This annex sets out how the growth incidence curves are constructed and what accounts for their differing shapes across the four counterfactuals.

### The common framework

The growth incidence curve, introduced by Ravallion and Chen (2003), plots the proportional growth in welfare at each percentile of the distribution. Let Q0(p) be the baseline quantile function. Sort all individuals by baseline per capita welfare and read off the welfare level at percentile p. Let Q1(p) be the counterfactual quantile function, constructed the same way after the shock and the reranking. The curve is as follows:

*GIC(p)= Q1(p)* -1 (4E.1)

*Q0(p)*

The curve is anonymous. Each quantile function records the welfare level at a percentile of its own distribution. So, GIC(p) compares the welfare of whoever occupies percentile p after the shock to the welfare of whoever occupied it before, without following any individual household across the two. The two occupants of percentile p are, in general, different households. Each counterfactual changes the welfare of the treated households according to a rule and leaves the rest unchanged. The treated households are poor at the $3.00 or the $8.30 line, and, for some counterfactuals, additionally in a given sector or type of employment. The rule either multiplies their welfare by a factor (in the employment counterfactual), or assigns each poor worker a predicted earnings level (in the three earnings counterfactuals). The population is then reranked, and the curve plotted. The reranking is what makes the curve positive well above the poverty line (beyond percentile 10 at $3.00, and percentile 45 at $8.30), even though every counterfactual acts only on poor households. Treated households earn more. The counterfactual distribution therefore shifts right, and the welfare threshold at each percentile rises. A treated household moves up in rank, while an untreated household, its own welfare unchanged, is overtaken and falls in rank. A treated household near the line can pass many untreated households and land far above the line. The gains shown above the line are thus not gains among the nonpoor. At a percentile above the line, Q1(p) is the welfare of a formerly poor household that climbed into that rank, thereby exceeding Q0(p), the welfare of the household that used to sit there.

### An approximate decomposition

It is useful to think of the curve as a product of two factors. Consider the household occupying percentile p after the shock. If it is treated, its welfare is its baseline welfare, times

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

the multiplier; if untreated, its welfare is unchanged. Approximating the untreated occupant as coming from a baseline rank close to p so that the associated welfare is close to Q0(p) and the contribution to the ratio is close to one, and averaging over which type occupies the percentile, one obtains the following approximation:

*GIC(p) ≈ (multiplier at p - 1) × (share of households at p that were treated)* (4E.2) The approximation is not exact. An untreated household occupying percentile p after the shock came from a higher baseline percentile p + k, having fallen in rank. So, its welfare is *Q0(p + k), and its contribution to the ratio is Q0(p + k)/Q0(p) > 1, not one. The displacement k* is small only if the baseline distribution is locally flat or the shock is modest. Where Q0 is steep, equation 4E.2 understates the contribution of untreated occupants and overstates the dilution. The anonymous curve is fundamentally a comparison of two sorted distributions rather than a household-level object, and equation 4E.2 reimports a fixed-identity reading. It is a qualitative guide, not an identity. What the approximation gets right is the qualitative shape, which two ingredients suffice to determine: (1) the multiplier among treated households as a function of the welfare level, and (2) the treated share, that is, the share of households at a given welfare level that were shocked. The curve rises with each ingredient. Holding the treated share fixed, a larger multiplier means the treated households that end up at percentile p exceed the baseline welfare at p by more; so, Q1(p)/Q0(p) is larger. Holding the multiplier fixed, a larger treated share means more of percentile p is occupied by these multiplied-up households rather than by untreated households that merely fell in rank; so, again, the ratio is larger. The two factors move in opposite directions across the distribution, and their interaction produces the observed shapes.

### The employment counterfactual

*The multiplier. The shock raises each poor household below the target employment rate of* 75 percent up to that rate, multiplying the household's per capita welfare by the workercount ratio 0.75 nmax/ncurr, where ncurr is the household's current number of workers, and *nmax is the number that would work if every working-age adult worked. Poor households* already at or above 75 percent are left unchanged. Because per capita welfare is multiplied by this ratio, the household's proportional gain is the multiplier, minus one, and the baseline welfare level cancels. The level of poverty of the household does not affect the percentage gain; only the worker ratio has an effect. Setting aside a spike in the bottom one or two percentiles (an average dominated by a few large households with many idle adults and an extreme worker ratio, best treated as a small-cell outlier), the multiplier rises from about 1.29 in the bottom percentiles to a plateau near 1.38 (refer to figure 4E.1, panel a). The variation is small: the multiplier is close to constant, around a 30 to 40 percent increase, across the range of the poor.

-----

Panel a: the* multiplier, the ratio of counterfactual to current workers (0.75 nmax/n curr) for treated households, averaged over poor households; the vertical axis is truncated, so the true variation is small, roughly 1.29 to 1.39. The poverty lines are $3.00- and $8.30-a-person-a-day (in 2021 purchasing power parity terms).

*The treated share. A poor household is treated if it has at least one current worker and its* employment rate is below 75 percent.28 The treated share at a given welfare level is the share of households residing there that meet these conditions. It is not constant across the poor range (refer to figure 4E.1, panel b). It is lowest at the bottom-about 32 percent at the first percentile-and rises to a plateau near 50 percent to 55 percent through the lower middle. Two groups account for the low share at the bottom: households with no current worker and households with employment rates already at or above 75 percent. Even among the poorest, a nontrivial number already employ at least three-quarters of their working-age adults and are therefore not treated. Above the poverty line, the treated share falls to zero because households there are outside the counterfactual. The share drops near percentile 10 in the $3.00 variant and near percentile 45 in the $8.30 variant. *The resulting hump. With the multiplier close to constant, the shape of the curve* follows the treated share, and the product of the two produces a hump. At the bottom, the curve is positive, but moderate because the treated share is at its lowest, that is, a substantial share of the poorest households are ineligible, having no worker or already employing three-quarters or more of their working-age adults. Through the lower

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs

middle, the curve is highest where the treated share is on a plateau and the multiplier is at full value. At and above the line, the curve falls as the treated share drops to zero and the percentiles are increasingly occupied by untreated households with unchanged welfare. The proportional gain shown at each percentile is smaller than a fully treated percentile would show. In equation 4E.2, where the treated share is near one-half, the curve reaches only about half of the multiplier, minus one. The untreated households at that welfare level dilute the average.

### The two employment variants

The mechanism is the same at both lines. The variants differ only in the range of the households treated. Below the $3.00 line, the two treated-share profiles coincide. So, the variants apply the identical shock there, and they differ only where the treated share drops to zero. For the $3.00 variant, it drops slightly after percentile 10. The hump is therefore narrow. The treated share rises out of the bottom, reaches a plateau briefly, and is cut off at the line. For the $8.30 variant, the plateau extends to about percentile 45 before the share drops. So, the hump is broad, sustained across the lower half, and peaks around percentile 40. The difference between the two curves is thus the width of the treated-share plateau, which is set by where each poverty line cuts the distribution, not a difference in the size of the per household shock.

### The earnings switch: The largest gains are among the poorest

The three earnings counterfactuals do not scale a household's existing earnings by a factor. They assign each poor worker a predicted earnings level. For the sector and employmenttype switches, the worker's own characteristics are valued under the destination earnings equation. The worker is therefore repriced as if working in the higher-return segment. For the education switch, the characteristics are valued under a single equation whereby education is raised to the nonpoor average. In each case, the worker is moved into a common pricing of characteristics. Because the pricing is common, the absolute earnings gain is broadly similar across poor households, but not identical, because characteristics differ and are valued accordingly, though they are of comparable magnitude. The poorest households start from the lowest baseline earnings. The same absolute gain is thus largest in proportional terms at the bottom. Because the curve plots proportional gains, the earnings curves are highest at the first percentiles-exceeding 50 percent-and decline as the baseline rises. This is in contrast with the employment shock. Where per capita welfare is multiplied by a worker ratio, the proportional gain is the ratio, minus one, regardless of the household's level of welfare. The shape follows the treated share rather than peaking at the bottom. Among the three switches, education is the strongest, for the reasons established in the main text. It treats the broadest population, which thins out less steeply across the distribution relative to the case of agriculture or self-employment, and it carries the largest conditional premium of the three.

## Annex 4F. The employment decline among

### the poor and the role of more time in school

School enrollment among young people has risen over the past two decades, a welcome development that mechanically lowers measured employment among 15- to 24-year-olds. If the decline in employment among the poor documented in the main text were driven by young people staying in school longer, it would carry a different interpretation: not a loss of work, but an investment in human capital. This annex tests that possibility by repeating the two main exercises on a working-age population restricted to ages 25-64, an age range in which enrollment plays almost no role. The conclusion is unchanged in both cases. The first exercise is the decomposition of welfare growth (refer to figure 4F.1). Under the narrower age definition, growth in welfare per worker remains the dominant source of welfare growth across the distribution, contributing 3.53 percentage points annually in the bottom

Employment grows less or falls more among the poor than among the nonpoor in 90 of 136 economies, against 99 of 137 on the wider definition. Figure 4F.2 also reports the averages by country income group. Across low-, lower-middle-, upper-middle-, and high-income economies alike, every income-group average lies below the 45-degree line, and employment rates generally declined among poor adults even where they rose among the nonpoor. The same holds under both age definitions. Longer schooling among young people therefore does not account for the fall in employment among the poor. The decline is present and of similar magnitude among adults old enough for enrollment to be irrelevant.
