## Notes

1. Refer to chapter 1 for more details on poverty profiles and the trends in poverty.
2. How jobs are created and by which firms is the subject of the forthcoming World Development Report on jobs. The constraints on this firm side of the market are documented elsewhere: the productivity differences that separate firms and economies (Hall and Jones 1999; Hsieh and Klenow 2009; Restuccia 2019); the financing constraints that limit the growth of firms and of household enterprises (de Mel et al. 2008; Didier and Cusolito 2024); the scope for services-led development (Nayyar et al. 2021); and technology, automation, and artificial intelligence as emerging determinants of labor demand (World Bank 2026).
3. The global simulations are descriptive exercises. Each asks how much measured poverty would fall if one feature of the poor's work were different, holding the rest of the observed distribution as it is. None evaluates a policy or the responses a policy might generate. The purpose is to show where the largest gains reside, a question separate from the question about how policies might realize the gains. There are four simulations: an employment simulation that raises the share of working adults in poor households to a benchmark of 75 percent, and three earnings simulations, one for each dimension along which the poor are concentrated. One variant applies the employment simulation to women and to men separately. All hold wages fixed and displace no existing worker. So, each represents an upper bound, and each changes one margin only. The employment simulations hold welfare per worker fixed. The earnings simulations hold the quantity of work fixed.
4. Welfare here is household consumption or household income, according to the core welfare measure of the underlying survey. The decomposition divides welfare in two ways: welfare per person, by all household members, and welfare per worker, by the members in work. Welfare per worker is used throughout as a proxy for earnings per worker because labor is the dominant source of income among these households. The harmonized welfare aggregate is the variable available on a consistent basis across the full set of surveys, whereas individual earnings are missing from many of the surveys and not fully harmonized in the rest of the surveys. So, a measure built on individual earnings would cover far fewer economies.
5. This conclusion accords with the decomposition literature on poverty reduction, which reaches the conclusion through a different method. Analyzing changes in poverty across a range of developing countries, Inchauste et al. (2014) find that growth in labor income (L x w) is the dominant proximate contributor and, within labor income, that earnings (w) rather than employment (L) drive this result: "the increase in workers' earnings was relatively more important in reducing poverty than the increase in the number of workers or in the number of jobs. … It was workers' [earnings] growth that made the difference rather than higher employment rates" (Inchauste et al. 2014, 54-56). Their analysis cannot disentangle whether earnings rose through improved job quality, higher productivity, or longer hours, a question the present chapter takes up directly in the sections that follow.
6. Refer to figure 4.1. This figure carries an implication for inequality. Because the bottom four quintiles all grew more quickly than the top quintile, inequality within this sample fell over the period, driven especially by the gains in the middle of the distribution. The pattern echoes the Lakner-Milanović elephant curve (Lakner and Milanović 2016). The sample comprises low- and middle-income economies and excludes high-income economies. Including the latter would add the affluent trunk of the global distribution. So, the result here describes falling inequality within the developing world rather than across the world as a whole. Refer to chapter 2 for more discussion on global inequality patterns.
7. The GMD is the principal data source for nearly all the quantitative analysis in this chapter. It is a collection of nationally representative household surveys harmonized to common definitions of welfare, demographics, and labor market indicators.
8. Whether credit constraints and the lumpiness of investment generate self-perpetuating poverty traps rather than slow convergence remains an open empirical question. The canonical model is that

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs of Banerjee and Newman (1993), in which borrowing constraints and a single lumpy investment produce multiple equilibriums and an S-shaped relation between current and future assets. Balboni et al. (2022) document such an S-shape among ultrapoor women in rural Bangladesh, with an asset threshold below which households decumulate. The broader evidence is mixed. Kraay and McKenzie (2014) find few clean instances of asset-threshold traps, and Karlan et al. (2026) report that the Bangladesh result is sensitive to the treatment of zero-asset observations and may reflect locationspecific growth rather than wealth thresholds. The distinction matters less for the argument here than the observation common to these studies, namely, that the constraints binding the poorest often include low human capital and limited mobility, alongside the shortage of physical capital.

9. The gap is not a matter of open unemployment. Measured unemployment is low-on the order of 3 percent to 4 percent-and similar across poverty groups. It is consolidated with nonemployment in the figures because, in low- and middle-income settings, the binding margin is labor force participation rather than the rate at which active job-seekers fail to find work. The poor who are not working are, for the most part, not unemployed in the formal sense, but are outside the labor force, a distinction that matters because the barriers to participation differ from the frictions that generate unemployment.
10. Working status is missing in some household members in the underlying surveys. Households with missing status are left untreated. So, the counterfactual reaches a smaller share of households than full data coverage would allow, and the reported poverty reductions are underestimates. The same applies to the earnings counterfactuals described in this chapter. (Refer to annex 4A for details on GMD coverage.)
11. Because the women's variant sets a target for women's employment alone, in households where men already work above the benchmark, overall household employment can end above 75 percent once women reach the benchmark. The women's variant thus does not hold to the household-level target, and figure 4.6, panels c-f, cannot be read as components of panels a and b.
12. The assumption is favorable, most visibly in the women's variant. A new woman worker is credited with the earnings of her household's existing earners, mostly men, whereas the activities that working poor women actually hold pay less.
13. The poverty gap gives the same picture in terms of the line rather than in terms of own income. After the employment counterfactual, the poverty gap, divided by the poverty rate, is about 35 percent at the $3.00 line and 40 percent at the $8.30 line. Thus, the average poor person remains at this percentage distance below the line.
14. Whether informal self-employment is chosen or imposed is debated. One argument views it as an evasion of regulation. The other as an effect of the exclusion from better work. The heterogeneity of informal activity reconciles the two arguments because different types of such activity are associated with different drivers. At the lower end, where the poor are, the balance tilts toward exclusion given that most small informal units fall outside regulation because they are small (not to escape the regulation) and because a large share of the poor's self-employment reflects the absence of wage work rather than a preference for such work (Breza et al. 2021; Bussolo and Sharma 2022; Margolis 2014). This absence is not a matter of preference, but of demand-side rationing whereby wage jobs are too few to go around, and self-employment is the residual left to the poor (Breza and Kaur 2025).
15. The returns to education also depend on the demand for skilled workers. If the supply of graduates expands, but demand does not increase, the education premium can be reduced. In Latin America and the Caribbean, a large increase in graduates has been accompanied by declining returns to education, suggesting that there are limits to supply-side-only educational improvements. The evidence is not causal, but it qualifies the assumption, maintained in the counterfactual, that destination returns are unchanged.
16. All four curves remain positive well above the poverty line (beyond the 10th percentile at $3.00 and the 45th at $8.30), even though each counterfactual acts only on poor households. This reflects reranking rather than any gain to the nonpoor. A poor household with rising welfare overtakes households formerly above the line. So, at a percentile above the line, the curve compares whoever

-----

POVERTY, PROSPERITY, AND PLANET REPORT 2026 occupies that rank after the shock with whoever occupied the position prior to the shock. The gains shown above the line accrue to formerly poor households that climbed into these ranks. The formal construction of the curves and the decomposition of the employment hump are set out in annex 4E.

17. The sample covers 41 economies: 10 low- or lower-middle-income economies, 8 upper-middleincome economies, and 23 high-income economies, using the World Bank fiscal year 2022/23 income classification. It represents approximately 4.3 billion people (52.9 percent of the world population and 55.3 percent of world employment). (Note that there is no coverage for the MENAAP region, and the 23 high-income economies are in Europe.) Poverty status is assessed against the poverty line corresponding to each economy's income classification, that is, $3.00-, $4.20-, and $8.30-a-persona-day (in 2021 purchasing power parity terms) in low-, lower-middle, and upper-middle-income economies, respectively, and $29.40 for the high-income group. So, escaping poverty means crossing the standard applicable to the economy's own income level.
18. The job ladder ranks types of work by average earnings and treats movement between the types as climbing or descending. A transition is an improvement only if it moves a worker to a higher-paying rung. The term originates in the labor economics literature on career wage growth through job change (Topel and Ward 1992). The application used here follows Fields et al. (2023), who rank work status by mean real earnings and study transitions up and down the ladder using panel data. The panel evidence demonstrates that this ordering is not universal.
19. This is not an artifact of restricting attention to poor households. In full-sample regressions of the change in log per capita income on the same transitions, nonagricultural self-employment again matches or exceeds wage employment in the low- and lower-middle-income groups. In the richer groups, the outcomes diverge: self-employment raises incomes across the distribution, but not the probability that a poor household escapes poverty.
20. A parallel panel analysis of small-scale farming households in Ethiopia, Nigeria, Tanzania, and Uganda, using the livelihood types described above, reaches a consistent conclusion: The households that leave poverty are those with incomes that shift away from farming toward activities outside farming, rather than those that enlarge or commercialize their farms. Refer to Ouedraogo et al. (2026) for the estimates and the underlying typology.
21. Compared with the 49 country sample in Donovan et al. (2023), the panels here are longer (two to seven years versus two to three quarters), nationally representative, including rural areas, and extend to lower-income levels. Donovan et al. (2023) confirm in supplementary analyses that their results hold for rural areas and for at least one country below $3,400 GDP per capita (Rwanda), but their core sample remains predominantly urban and middle income.
22. Refer to Global Monitoring Database, internal database, World Bank; Poverty and Inequality Platform, World Bank, https://pip.worldbank.org/home.
23. LIS economies are Australia; Canada; Germany; Israel; Japan; the Republic of Korea; Taiwan, China; the United Kingdom; and the United States. Refer to 2025 data of LIS Database (Luxembourg Income Study Database), LIS Cross-National Data Center in Luxembourg, http://www.lisdatacenter.org/our [-data/lis-database/.](http://www.lisdatacenter.org/our-data/lis-database/)
24. Refer to ISIC (International Standard Industrial Classification of All Economic Activities) (dashboard), Statistics Division, Department of Economic and Social Affairs, United Nations, [https://unstats.un.org/unsd/classifications/Econ/ISIC.cshtml.](https://unstats.un.org/unsd/classifications/Econ/ISIC.cshtml)
25. Population figures are based on population projections of World Population Prospects (dashboard), Population Division, Department of Economic and Social Affairs, United Nations, https://population [.un.org/wpp/.](https://population.un.org/wpp/)
26. Refer to 1000 Binned Global Distribution (dataset), World Bank, https://datacatalog.worldbank.org [/search/dataset/0064304/1000-binned-global-distribution.](https://datacatalog.worldbank.org/search/dataset/0064304/1000-binned-global-distribution)
27. The World Bank's Human Capital Index Plus (HCI+), launched in 2026, combines education, health, and accumulated work experience on a 0-325 scale, by which each additional point corresponds to roughly 1 percent higher expected future earnings. Low-income countries score about 116 against about 254 among high-income economies. The chapter cites HCI+ for the cross-country education

-----

Tackling Poverty and Vulnerability Requires More Jobs and Better-Paying Jobs and health gradient. Its employment component is built partly on the assumption that unemployment and underemployment are concentrated among the poor, a premise the chapter treats with caution (refer to box 4.1). In low-income settings, the poor can rarely afford not to work, and the evidence that their hours are lower is weak. Refer to HCI+ (Human Capital Index Plus) (dashboard), Human Capital Project, World Bank, https://humancapital.worldbank.org/hciplus/methodology/.

28. Households with no current worker are excluded because the multiplier 0.75 nmax/ncurr is undefined if *ncurr = 0.*

-----

*Poverty, Prosperity, and Planet Report 2026: Jobs at the Center of Poverty Eradication* assesses global progress toward ending extreme poverty and boosting shared prosperity on a livable planet. After three decades of historic gains, extreme poverty reduction has stalled. In 2026, an estimated 811 million people, 1 in 10 worldwide, are living on less than $3.00 a day, while nearly half of humanity is living on less than $8.30 a day. Poverty is increasingly concentrated in Sub-Saharan Africa and fragile and conflict-affected situations, where rapid population growth, insufficient economic growth, and exposure to various shocks make progress extremely difficult. Through the deeply probing lens of poverty, prosperity, and planetary sustainability, the report finds that the number of economies with high inequality is falling. Yet, many people, including a growing share of the extreme poor, are still living in highly unequal economies. And progress in closing the Global Prosperity Gap-an indicator that combines the tracking of both progress in average income and improvements in equity-has also slowed. Climate hazards, ecosystem degradation, food insecurity, and inadequate water and sanitation are threatening the gains in development, especially among those people and households with the fewest resources to withstand and recover from shocks. Ending extreme poverty would add relatively little to the cost or impacts of global emissions, demonstrating that poverty reduction and climate protection are not competing goals. Placing jobs at the center, the report offers new evidence on how employment and earnings shape household welfare. Using survey data linking poverty with labor market outcomes, it offers a global profile of poor and nonpoor workers and examines pathways out of poverty through job opportunities. Poor people work nearly as much as the nonpoor but earn less, work fewer hours, and support more dependents. And one in three poor people have no employment. Both more jobs and better-paying jobs will reduce poverty, but the effects of each reach different groups. Increasing earnings matters most for the poorest, while increasing rates of employment, particularly among women, benefits the near-poor the most. Poverty reduction requires that access to income-generating opportunities expands and that the returns to the work poor people already perform increase. This report-by tracking where poverty is concentrated, the progress on inequality, and the status of measures of a livable planet, combined with a unique profile of the work of the poor-seeks to inspire a data-driven agenda for reinvigorating global progress in these many dimensions.
